I wasn’ t sure if we had enough books in our library , so I\nwrote another one!', 'INTRODUCTION\nThis book is about a software\ndevelopment technique called object-\noriented programming (OOP) and how\nit can be used with Python. Before\nOOP, programmers used an approach\nknown as procedural programming, also called\nstructured programming, which involves building a\nset of functions (procedures) and passing data around\nthrough calls to those functions. The OOP paradigm\ngives programmers an efficient way to combine code\nand data into cohesive units that are often highly\nreusable.\nIn preparation for writing this book, I extensively researched existing\nliterature and videos, looking specifically at the approaches taken to explain\nthis important and wide-ranging topic. I found that instructors and writers\ntypically start by defining certain key terms: class , instance variable ,\nmethod , encapsulation , inheritance , polymorphism , and so on.\nWhile these are all important concepts, and I’ll cover all of them in depth\nin this book, I’ll begin in a dif ferent way: by considering the question,\n“What problem are we solving?” That is, if OOP is the solution, then what\nis the problem? T o answer this question, I’ll start by presenting a few\nexamples of programs built using procedural programming and identifying\ncomplications inherent in this style. Then I’ll show you how an object-', 'oriented approach can make the construction of such programs much easier\nand the programs themselves more maintainable.', 'Who Is This Book For?\nThis book is intended for people who already have some familiarity with\nPython and with using basic functions from the Python Standard Library . I\nwill assume that you understand the fundamental syntax of the language\nand can write small- to medium-sized programs using variables, assignment\nstatements, if/elif /else  statements, while  and for loops, functions and\nfunction calls, lists, dictionaries, and so on. If you aren’ t comfortable with\nall of these concepts, then I suggest that you get a copy of my earlier book,\nLearn to Pr ogram with Python 3  (Apress), and read that first.\nThis is an intermediate-level text, so there are a number of more\nadvanced topics that I will not address. For example, to keep the book\npractical, I will not often go into detail on the internal implementation of\nPython. For simplicity and clarity , and to keep the focus on mastering OOP\ntechniques, the examples are written using a limited subset of the language.\nThere are more advanced and concise ways to code in Python that are\nbeyond the scope of this book.\nI will cover the underlying details of OOP in a mostly language-\nindependent way , but will point out areas where there are dif ferences\nbetween Python and other OOP languages. Having learned the basics of\nOOP-style coding through this book, if you wish, you should be able to\neasily apply these techniques to other OOP languages.\nPython Version(s) and Installation\nAll the example code in this book was written and tested using Python\nversions 3.6 through 3.9. All the examples should work fine with version\n3.6 or newer .\nPython is available for free at https://www .python.or g. If you don’ t have\nPython installed, or you want to upgrade to the latest version, go to that site,\nfind the Downloads tab, and click the Download  button. This will\ndownload an installable file onto your computer . Ther efore, all variable and\nfunction names in this book ar e written using camel case.', 'How Will I Explain OOP?\nThe examples in the first few chapters use text-based Python; these sample\nprograms get input from the user and output information to the user purely\nin the form of text. I’ll introduce OOP by showing you how to develop text-\nbased simulations of physical objects in code. W e’ll start by creating\nrepresentations of light switches, dimmer switches, and TV remote controls\nas objects. I’ll then show you how we can use OOP to simulate bank\naccounts and a bank that controls many accounts.\nOnce we’ve covered the basics of OOP , I’ll introduce the pygame\nmodule, which allows programmers to write games and applications that\nuse a graphical user interface (GUI) . With GUI-based programs, the user\nintuitively interacts with buttons, checkboxes, text input and output fields,\nand other user -friendly widgets.\nI chose to use pygame with Python because this combination allows me\nto demonstrate OOP concepts in a highly visual way using elements on the\nscreen. Pygame is extremely portable and runs on nearly every platform and\noperating system. All the sample programs that use the pygame package\nhave been tested with the recently released pygame version 2.0.\nI’ve created a package called pygwidgets  that works with pygame and\nimplements a number of basic widgets, all of which are built using an OOP\napproach. I’ll introduce this package later in the book, providing sample\ncode you can run and experiment with. This approach will allow you to see\nreal, practical examples of key object-oriented concepts, while\nincorporating these techniques to produce fun, playable games. I’ll also\nintroduce my pyghelpers  package, which provides code to help write more\ncomplicated games and applications.\nAll the example code shown in the book is available as a single download\nfrom the No Starch website:  https://www .nostar ch.com/object-oriented-\npython/ .\nThe code is also available on a chapter -by-chapter basis from my GitHub\nrepository: https://github.com/IrvKalb/Object-Oriented-Python-Code/ .\nWhat’s in the Book', 'This book is divided into four parts.  Part I  introduces object-oriented\nprogramming:\nChapter 1  provides a review of coding using procedural programming. I’ll\nshow you how to implement a text-based card game and simulate a bank\nperforming operations on one or more accounts. Along the way , I discuss\ncommon problems with the procedural approach.\nChapter 2  introduces classes and objects and shows how you can represent\nreal-world objects like light switches or a TV remote in Python using\nclasses. Y ou’ll see how an object-oriented approach solves the problems\nhighlighted in the first chapter .\nChapter 3  presents two mental models that you can use to think about\nwhat’ s going on behind the scenes when you create objects in Python. W e’ll\nuse Python T utor to step through code and see how objects are created.\nChapter 4  demonstrates a standard way to handle multiple objects of the\nsame type by introducing the concept of an object manager object. W e’ll\nexpand the bank account simulation using classes, and I’ll show you how to\nhandle errors using exceptions.\nPart II  focuses on building GUIs with pygame:\nChapter 5  introduces the pygame package and the event-driven model of\nprogramming. W e’ll build a few simple programs to get you started with\nplacing graphics in a window and handling keyboard and mouse input, then\ndevelop a more complicated ball-bouncing program.\nChapter 6  goes into much more detail on using OOP with pygame\nprograms. W e’ll build a Shapes program to demonstrate this concept.\nChapter 10  covers inheritance, which allows you to create a set of\nsubclasses that all use common code built into a base class, rather than\nhaving to reinvent the wheel with similar classes. W e’ll look at a few real-\nworld examples where inheritance comes in handy , such as implementing\nan input field that only accepts numbers, then rewrite our Shapes example\nprogram to use this feature.\nChapter 1 1 wraps up this part of the book by discussing some additional\nimportant OOP topics, mostly related to memory management. W e’ll develop dif ferent timer classes that allow a\nprogram to keep running while concurrently checking for a given time\nlimit.\nChapter 14  explains animation classes you can use to show sequences of\nimages. It concludes with a short wrap-up for the book.\nDevelopment Environments\nIn this book, you’ll need to use the command line only minimally for\ninstalling software. All installation instructions will be clearly written out,\nso you won’ t need to learn any additional command line syntax.\nRather than using the command line for development, I believe strongly\nin using an interactive development environment (IDE). An IDE handles\nmany of the details of the underlying operating system for you, and it\nallows you to write, edit, and run your code using a single program. IDEs\nare typically cross-platform, allowing programmers to easily move from a\nMac to a W indows computer or vice versa.\nThe short example programs in the book can be run in the IDLE\ndevelopment environment that is installed when you install Python. IDLE is\nvery simple to use and works well for programs that can be written in a\nsingle file. When we get into more complicated programs that use multiple\nPython files, I encourage you to use a more sophisticated environment\ninstead; I use the JetBrains PyCharm development environment, which\nhandles multiple-file projects more easily . The Community Edition is\navailable for free from https://www .jetbrains.com/ , and I highly recommend\nit. PyCharm also has a fully integrated debugger that can be extremely\nuseful when writing lar ger programs. For more information on how to use\nthe debugger , please see my Y ouTube video “Debugging Python 3 with\nPyCharm” at https://www .youtube.com/watch?v=cxAOSQQwDJ4&t=43s/ .\nWidgets and Example Games\nThe book introduces and makes available two Python packages:\npygwidgets  and pyghelpers . Using these packages, you should be able to\nbuild full GUI programs—but more importantly , you should gain an', 'understanding of how each of the widgets is coded as a class and used as an\nobject.\nIncorporating various widgets, the example games in the book start out\nrelatively simple and get progressively more complicated. Chapter 16  walks\nyou through the development and implementation of a full-featured video\ngame, complete with a high-scores table that is saved to a file.\nBy the end of this book, you should be able to code your own games—\ncard games, or video games in the style of Pong, Hangman, Breakout,\nSpace Invaders, and so on. Object-oriented programming gives you the\nability to write programs that can easily display and control multiple items\nof the same type, which is often required when building user interfaces and\nis frequently necessary in game play .\nObject-oriented programming is a general style that can be used in all\naspects of programming, well beyond the game examples I use to\ndemonstrate OOP techniques here. I hope you find this approach to learning\nOOP enjoyable.\nLet’s get started!', 'PART I\nINTRODUCING OBJECT-ORIENTED\nPROGRAMMING\nThis part of the book introduces you to object-\noriented programming. We’ll discuss problems\ninherent in procedural code, then see how object-\noriented programming addresses those concerns.\nThinking in objects (with state and behavior) will\ngive you a new perspective about how to write code.\nChapter 1  provides a review of procedural Python. I start by presenting a\ntext-based card game named Higher or Lower , then work through a few\nprogressively more complex implementations of a bank account in Python\nto help you better understand common problems with coding in a\nprocedural style.\nChapter 2  shows how we might represent real-world objects in Python\nusing classes. W e’ll write a program to simulate a light switch, modify it to\ninclude dimming capabilities, then move on to a more complicated TV\nremote simulation.\nChapter 3  gives you two dif ferent ways to think about what is going on\nbehind the scenes when you create objects in Python.\nChapter 4  then demonstrates a standard way to handle multiple objects of\nthe same type (for example, consider a simple game like checkers where\nyou have to keep track of many similar game pieces). W e’ll expand the\nbank account programs from Chapter 1 , and explore how to handle errors.', '1\nPROCEDURAL PYTHON EXAMPLES\nIntroductory courses and books\ntypically teach software development\nusing the procedural programming\nstyle, which involves splitting a\nprogram into a number of functions\n(also known as procedures or subroutines). You pass\ndata into functions, each of which performs one or\nmore computations and, typically, passes back\nresults. This book is about a different paradigm of\nprogramming known as object-oriented\nprogramming (OOP) that allows programmers to\nthink differently about how to build software. Object-\noriented programming gives programmers a way to\ncombine code and data together into cohesive units,\nthereby avoiding some complications inherent in\nprocedural programming.\nIn this chapter , I’ll review a number of concepts in basic Python by\nbuilding two small programs that incorporate various Python constructs.\nThe first will be a small card game called Higher or Lower; the second will', "be a simulation of a bank, performing operations on one, two, and multiple\naccounts. Both will be built using procedural programming—that is, using\nthe standard techniques of data and functions. Later , I’ll rewrite these\nprograms using OOP techniques. The purpose of this chapter is to\ndemonstrate some key problems inherent in procedural programming. W ith\nthat understanding, the chapters that follow will explain how OOP solves\nthose problems.\nHigher or Lower Card Game\nMy first example is a simple card game called Higher or Lower . In this\ngame, eight cards are randomly chosen from a deck. The first card is shown\nface up. The game asks the player to predict whether the next card in the\nselection will have a higher or lower value than the currently showing card.\nFor example, say the card that’ s shown is a 3. The player chooses “higher ,”\nand the next card is shown. If that card has a higher value, the player is\ncorrect. In this example, if the player had chosen “lower ,” they would have\nbeen incorrect.\nIf the player guesses correctly , they get 20 points. If they choose\nincorrectly , they lose 15 points. If the next card to be turned over has the\nsame value as the previous card, the player is incorrect.\nRepresenting the Data\nThe program needs to represent a deck of 52 cards, which I’ll build as a list.\nEach of the 52 elements in the list will be a dictionary (a set of key/value\npairs). T o represent any card, each dictionary will contain three key/value\npairs: 'rank' , 'suit' , and 'value' . The rank is the name of the card (Ace,\n2, 3, … 10, Jack, Queen, King), but the value is an integer used for\ncomparing cards (1, 2, 3, … 10, 1 1, 12, 13). For example, the Jack of Clubs\nwould be represented as the following dictionary:\n{'rank': 'Jack', 'suit': 'Clubs', 'value': 11}\nBefore the player plays a round, the list representing the deck is created\nand shuf fled to randomize the order of the cards. I have no graphical", "representation of the cards, so each time the user chooses “higher” or\n“lower ,” the program gets a card dictionary from the deck and prints the\nrank and the suit for the user . The program then compares the value of the\nnew card to that of the previous card and gives feedback based on the\ncorrectness of the user ’s answer .\nImplementation\nListing 1-1  shows the code of the Higher or Lower game.\nNOTE\nAs a r eminder , the code associated with all the major listings in this\nbook is available for download at https://www .nostar ch.com/object-\noriented-python/  and https://github.com/IrvKalb/Object-Oriented-\nPython-Code/ . You can either download and run the code or type it\nin yourself.\nFile: HigherOrLowerProcedural.py\n# HigherOrLower  \n \nimport random  \n \n# Card constants  \nSUIT_TUPLE = ('Spades', 'Hearts', 'Clubs', 'Diamonds')  \nRANK_TUPLE = ('Ace', '2', '3', '4', '5', '6', '7', '8', '9',  \n'10', 'Jack', 'Queen', 'King')  \n \nNCARDS = 8  \n \n# Pass in a deck and this function returns a random card from  \nthe deck  \ndef getCard(deckListIn):  \n    thisCard = deckListIn.pop() # pop one off the top of the  \ndeck and return  \n    return thisCard  \n \n# Pass in a deck and this function returns a shuffled copy of  \nthe deck     \ndef shuffle(deckListIn):  ", "    deckListOut = deckListIn.copy()  # make a copy of the  \nstarting  deck \n    random.shuffle(deckListOut)  \n    return deckListOut  \n \n#  Main code  \nprint('Welcome to Higher or Lower.')  \nprint('You have to choose whether the next card to be shown  \nwill be higher or lower than the current card.')  \nprint('Getting it right adds 20 points; get it wrong and you  \nlose 15 points.')  \nprint('You have 50 points to start.')  \nprint() \n \nstartingDeckList = []  \n1 for suit in SUIT_TUPLE:  \n    for thisValue, rank in enumerate(RANK_TUPLE):  \n        cardDict = {'rank':rank, 'suit':suit,  \n'value':thisValue + 1}  \n        startingDeckList.append(cardDict)  \n         \nscore = 50  \n \nwhile True:  # play multiple games  \n    print()  \n    gameDeckList = shuffle(startingDeckList)  \n  2 currentCardDict = getCard(gameDeckList)  \n    currentCardRank = currentCardDict['rank']  \n    currentCardValue = currentCardDict['value']  \n    currentCardSuit = currentCardDict['suit']     \n    print('Starting card is:', currentCardRank + ' of ' +  \ncurrentCardSuit)  \n    print()  \n \n  3 for cardNumber in range(0, NCARDS):  # play one game of  \nthis many  cards \n        answer = input('Will the next card be higher or lower  \nthan the ' +  \n                       currentCardRank + ' of ' +  \n                       currentCardSuit + '?  (enter h or l):  \n') \n        answer = answer.casefold()  # force lowercase  \n      4 nextCardDict = getCard(gameDeckList)  \n        nextCardRank = nextCardDict['rank']  \n        nextCardSuit = nextCardDict['suit']  \n        nextCardValue = nextCardDict['value']  ", '        print(\'Next card is:\', nextCardRank + \' of \' +  \nnextCardSuit)  \n \n      5 if answer == \'h\':  \n            if nextCardValue > currentCardValue:  \n                print(\'You got it right, it was higher\')  \n                score = score + 20  \n            else:  \n                print(\'Sorry, it was not higher\')  \n                score = score - 15           \n \n        elif answer == \'l\':  \n            if nextCardValue < currentCardValue:  \n                score = score + 20  \n                print(\'You got it right, it was lower\')  \n \n            else:  \n                score = score - 15  \n                print(\'Sorry, it was not lower\')  \n \n        print(\'Your score is:\', score)  \n        print()  \n        currentCardRank = nextCardRank  \n        currentCardValue = nextCardValue  # don\'t need  \ncurrent suit  \n \n  6 goAgain = input(\'To play again, press ENTER, or "q" to  \nquit: \')  \n    if goAgain == \'q\':  \n        break  \n \nprint(\'OK bye\')\nListing 1-1 : A Higher or Lower game using pr ocedural Python\nThe program starts by creating a deck as a list 1. Each card is a dictionary\nmade up of a rank, a suit, and a value. For each round of the game, I\nretrieve the first card from the deck and save the components in variables 2.\nFor the next seven cards, the user is asked to predict whether the next card\nwill be higher or lower than the most recently showing card 3. The next\ncard is retrieved from the deck, and its components are saved in a second\nset of variables 4. The game compares the user ’s answer to the card drawn\nand gives the user feedback and points based on the outcome 5. When the', "user has made predictions for all seven cards in the selection, we ask if they\nwant to play again 6.\nThis program demonstrates many elements of programming in general\nand Python in particular: variables, assignment statements, functions and\nfunction calls, if/else  statements, print statements, while  loops, lists,\nstrings, and dictionaries. This book will assume you're already familiar with\neverything shown in this example. If there is anything in this program that\nis unfamiliar or not clear to you, it would probably be worth your time to\nreview the appropriate material before moving on.\nReusable Code\nSince this is a playing card–based game, the code obviously creates and\nmanipulates a simulated deck of cards. If we wanted to write another card-\nbased game, it would be great to be able to reuse the code for the deck and\ncards.\nIn a procedural program, it can often be dif ficult to identify all the pieces\nof code associated with one portion of the program, such as the deck and\ncards in this example. In Listing 1-1 , the code for the deck consists of two\ntuple constants, two functions, some main code to build a global list that\nrepresents the starting deck of 52 cards, and another global list that\nrepresents the deck that is used while the game is being played. Further ,\nnotice that even in a small program like this, the data and the code that\nmanipulates the data might not be closely grouped together .\nTherefore, reusing the deck and card code in another program is not that\neasy or straightforward. In Chapter 12 , we will revisit this program and\nshow how an OOP solution makes reusing code like this much easier .\nBank Account Simulations\nIn this second example of procedural coding, I’ll present a number of\nvariations of a program that simulates running a bank. In each new version\nof the program, I’ll add more functionality . Note that these programs are not\nproduction-ready; invalid user entries or misuse will lead to errors. The", 'intent is to have you focus on how the code interacts with the data\nassociated with one or more bank accounts.\nTo start, consider what operations a client would want to do with a bank\naccount and what data would be needed to represent an account.\nAnalysis of Required Operations and Data\nA list of operations a person would want to do with a bank account would\ninclude:\nCreate (an account)\nDeposit\nWithdraw\nCheck balance\nNext, here is a minimal list of the data we would need to represent a bank\naccount:\nCustomer name\nPassword\nBalance\nNotice that all the operations are action words (verbs) and all the data\nitems are things (nouns). A real bank account would certainly be capable of\nmany more operations and would contain additional pieces of data (such as\nthe account holder ’s address, phone number , and Social Security number),\nbut to keep the discussion clear , I’ll start with just these four actions and\nthree pieces of data. Further , to keep things simple and focused, I’ll make\nall amounts an integer number of dollars. I should also point out that in a\nreal bank application, passwords would not be kept in cleartext\n(unencrypted) as it is in these examples.\nImplementation 1—Single Account W ithout Functions\nIn the starting version in Listing 1-2 , there is only a single account.\nFile: Bank1_OneAccount.py', "# Non-OOP\n# Bank Version 1\n# Single account  \n \n1 accountName = 'Joe'  \naccountBalance = 100  \naccountPassword = 'soup'  \n \nwhile True:  \n  2 print()  \n    print('Press b to get the balance')  \n    print('Press d to make a deposit')  \n    print('Press w to make a withdrawal')  \n    print('Press s to show the account')  \n    print('Press q to quit')  \n    print()  \n \n    action = input('What do you want to do? ')  \n    action = action.lower()  # force lowercase  \n    action = action[0]  # just use first letter  \n    print()  \n     \n    if action == 'b':  \n        print('Get Balance:')  \n        userPassword = input('Please enter the password: ')  \n        if userPassword != accountPassword:  \n            print('Incorrect password')  \n        else:  \n            print('Your balance is:', accountBalance)  \n \n    elif action == 'd':  \n        print('Deposit:')  \n        userDepositAmount = input('Please enter amount to  \ndeposit: ')  \n        userDepositAmount = int(userDepositAmount)  \n        userPassword = input('Please enter the password: ')  \n \n        if userDepositAmount < 0:  \n            print('You cannot deposit a negative amount!')  \n \n        elif userPassword != accountPassword:  \n            print('Incorrect password')  \n \n        else:  # OK \n            accountBalance = accountBalance +  \nuserDepositAmount  ", "            print('Your new balance is:', accountBalance)  \n         \n    elif action == 's':  # show \n        print('Show:')  \n        print('       Name', accountName)  \n        print('       Balance:', accountBalance)  \n        print('       Password:', accountPassword)  \n        print()  \n \n    elif action == 'q':  \n        break  \n \n    elif action == 'w':  \n        print('Withdraw:')  \n \n        userWithdrawAmount = input('Please enter the amount  \nto withdraw: ')  \n        userWithdrawAmount = int(userWithdrawAmount)  \n        userPassword = input('Please enter the password: ')  \n \n        if userWithdrawAmount < 0:  \n            print('You cannot withdraw a negative amount')  \n \n        elif userPassword != accountPassword:  \n            print('Incorrect password for this account')  \n \n        elif userWithdrawAmount > accountBalance:  \n            print('You cannot withdraw more than you have in  \nyour account')  \n \n        else:  #OK \n            accountBalance = accountBalance -  \nuserWithdrawAmount  \n            print('Your new balance is:', accountBalance)  \n \nprint('Done')\nListing 1-2 : Bank simulation for a single account\nThe program starts of f by initializing three variables to represent the data\nof one account 1. Then it displays a menu that allows a choice of operations\n2. The main code of the program acts directly on the global account\nvariables.", "In this example, all the actions are at the main level; there are no\nfunctions in the code. The program works fine, but it may seem a little long.\nA typical approach to make longer programs clearer is to move related code\ninto functions and make calls to those functions. W e’ll explore that in the\nnext implementation of the banking program.\nImplementation 2—Single Account with Functions\nIn the version of the program in Listing 1-3 , the code is broken up into\nseparate functions, one for each action. Again, this simulation is for a single\naccount.\nFile: Bank2_OneAccountW ithFunctions.py\n# Non-OOP\n# Bank 2\n# Single account  \n \naccountName = ''  \naccountBalance = 0  \naccountPassword = ''  \n \n1 def newAccount(name, balance, password):  \n    global accountName, accountBalance, accountPassword  \n    accountName = name  \n    accountBalance = balance  \n    accountPassword = password  \n \ndef show():  \n    global accountName, accountBalance, accountPassword  \n    print('       Name', accountName)  \n    print('       Balance:', accountBalance)  \n    print('       Password:', accountPassword)  \n    print()  \n \n2 def getBalance(password):  \n    global accountName, accountBalance, accountPassword  \n    if password != accountPassword:  \n        print('Incorrect password')  \n        return None  \n    return accountBalance  \n    \n3 def deposit(amountToDeposit, password):  ", '    global accountName, accountBalance, accountPassword  \n    if amountToDeposit < 0:  \n        print(\'You cannot deposit a negative amount!\')  \n        return None  \n         \n    if password != accountPassword:  \n        print(\'Incorrect password\')  \n        return None  \n     \n    accountBalance = accountBalance + amountToDeposit  \n    return accountBalance  \n     \n4 def withdraw(amountToWithdraw, password):  \n  5 global accountName, accountBalance, accountPassword  \n    if amountToWithdraw < 0:  \n        print(\'You cannot withdraw a negative amount\')  \n        return None  \n \n    if password != accountPassword:  \n        print(\'Incorrect password for this account\')  \n        return None  \n     \n    if amountToWithdraw > accountBalance:  \n        print(\'You cannot withdraw more than you have in your  \naccount\')  \n        return None  \n \n  6 accountBalance = accountBalance - amountToWithdraw  \n    return accountBalance  \n \nnewAccount("Joe", 100, \'soup\')  # create an account  \n \nwhile True:  \n    print()  \n    print(\'Press b to get the balance\')  \n    print(\'Press d to make a deposit\')  \n    print(\'Press w to make a withdrawal\')  \n    print(\'Press s to show the account\')  \n    print(\'Press q to quit\')  \n    print()  \n \n    action = input(\'What do you want to do? \')  \n    action = action.lower()  # force lowercase  \n    action = action[0]  # just use first letter  \n    print()  \n     \n    if action == \'b\':  ', "        print('Get Balance:')  \n        userPassword = input('Please enter the password: ')  \n        theBalance = getBalance(userPassword)  \n        if theBalance is not None:  \n            print('Your balance is:', theBalance)  \n \n  7 elif action == 'd':  \n        print('Deposit:')  \n        userDepositAmount = input('Please enter amount to  \ndeposit: ')  \n        userDepositAmount = int(userDepositAmount)  \n        userPassword = input('Please enter the password: ')  \n \n      8 newBalance = deposit(userDepositAmount, userPassword)  \n        if newBalance is not None:  \n            print('Your new balance is:', newBalance)    \n \n--- snip calls to appropriate functions ---  \n \nprint('Done')\nListing 1-3 : Bank simulation for one account with functions\nIn this version, I’ve built a function for each of the operations that we\nidentified for a bank account (create 1, check balance 2, deposit 3, and\nwithdraw 4) and rearranged the code so that the main code contains calls to\nthe dif ferent functions.\nAs a result, the main program is much more readable. For example, if the\nuser types d to indicate that they want to make a deposit 7, the code now\ncalls a function named deposit()  3, passing in the amount to be deposited\nand the account password the user entered.\nHowever , if you look at the definition of any of these functions—for\nexample, the withdraw()  function—you’ll see that the code uses global\nstatements 5 to access (get or set) the variables that represent the account.\nIn Python, a global  statement is only required if you want to change the\nvalue of a global variable in a function. However , I am using them here to\nmake it clear that these functions are referring to global variables, even if\nthey are just getting a value.", "As a general programming tenet, functions should never  modify global\nvariables. A function should only use data that is passed into it, make\ncalculations based on that data, and potentially return a result or results. The\nwithdraw()  function in this program does work, but it violates this rule by\nmodifying the value of the global variable accountBalance  6 (in addition to\naccessing the value of the global variable accountPassword ).\nImplementation 3—T wo Accounts\nThe version of the bank simulation program in Listing 1-4  uses the same\napproach as Listing 1-3  but adds the ability to have two accounts.\nFile: Bank3_T woAccounts.py\n# Non-OOP\n# Bank 3\n# Two accounts  \n \naccount0Name = ''  \naccount0Balance = 0  \naccount0Password = ''  \naccount1Name = ''  \naccount1Balance = 0 Name', account1Name)  \n        print('       Balance:', account1Balance)  \n        print('       Password:', account1Password)  \n        print()  \n \ndef getBalance(accountNumber, password):  \n  3 global account0Name, account0Balance, account0Password  \n    global account1Name, account1Balance, account1Password  \n \n    if accountNumber == 0:  \n        if password != account0Password:  \n W e\nneed a dif ferent way to handle an arbitrary number of accounts.", "Implementation 4—Multiple Accounts Using Lists\nTo more easily accommodate multiple accounts, in Listing 1-5  I’ll represent\nthe data using lists. I’ll use three parallel lists in this version of the program:\naccountNamesList , accountPasswordsList , and accountBalancesList .\nFile: Bank4_N_Accounts.py\n# Non-OOP Bank\n# Version 4\n# Any number of accounts - with lists  \n \n1 accountNamesList = []  \naccountBalancesList = []  \naccountPasswordsList = []  \n \ndef newAccount(name, balance, password):  \n    global accountNamesList, accountBalancesList,  \naccountPasswordsList  \n  2 accountNamesList.append(name)  \n    accountBalancesList.append(balance)  \n    accountPasswordsList.append(password)  \n    \ndef show(accountNumber):  \n    global accountNamesList, accountBalancesList,  \naccountPasswordsList  \n    print('Account', accountNumber)  \n    print('       Name', accountNamesList[accountNumber])  \n    print('       Balance:',  \naccountBalancesList[accountNumber])  \n    print('       Password:',  \naccountPasswordsList[accountNumber])  \n    print()  \n \ndef getBalance(accountNumber, password):  \n    global accountNamesList, accountBalancesList,  \naccountPasswordsList  \n    if password != accountPasswordsList[accountNumber]:  \n        print('Incorrect password')  \n        return None  \n    return accountBalancesList[accountNumber]  \n \n--- snipped additional functions ---  \n \n# Create two sample accounts", '3 print("Joe\'s account is account number:",  \nlen(accountNamesList))  \nnewAccount("Joe", 100, \'soup\')  \n \n4 print("Mary\'s account is account number:",  \nlen(accountNamesList))  \nnewAccount("Mary", 12345, \'nuts\')  \n \nwhile True:  \n    print()  \n    print(\'Press b to get the balance\')  \n    print(\'Press d to make a deposit\')  \n    print(\'Press n to create a new account\')  \n    print(\'Press w to make a withdrawal\')  \n    print(\'Press s to show all accounts\')  \n    print(\'Press q to quit\')  \n    print()  \n \n    action = input(\'What do you want to do? To\ncreate a new account, I append the appropriate value to each of the three\nlists 2.', 'Since I am now dealing with multiple accounts, I use the basic concept of\na bank account number . Every time a user creates an account, the code uses\nthe len()  function on one of the lists and returns that number as the user ’s\naccount number 3, 4. When I create an account for the first user , the length\nof the accountNamesList  is zero. Therefore, the first account created will\nbe given account number 0, the second account is given account number 1,\nand so on. Then, like at a real bank, to do any operation after creating an\naccount (like deposit or withdraw funds), the user must supply their account\nnumber 5.\nHowever , this code is still working with global data; now there are three\nglobal lists of data.\nImagine viewing this data as a spreadsheet. It might look like Table 1-1 .\nTable 1-1 : A Table of Our Data\nAccount number Name Password Balance\n0 Joe soup 100\n1 Mary nuts 3550\n2 Bill frisbee 1000\n3 Sue xxyyzz 750\n4 Henry PW 10000\nThe data is maintained as three global Python lists, where each list\nrepresents a column in this table. For example, as you can see from the\nhighlighted column, all the passwords are grouped together as one list. The\nusers’ names are grouped in another list, and the balances are grouped in a\nthird list. W ith this approach, to get information about one account, you\nneed to access these lists with a common index value.\nWhile this works, it seems extremely awkward. The data is not grouped\nin a logical way . For example, it doesn’ t seem right to keep all users’\npasswords together . Further , every time you add a new attribute to an\naccount, like an address or phone number , you need to create and access\nanother global list.\nInstead, what you really want is a grouping that represents a row in the\nsame spreadsheet, as in Table 1-2 .', "Table 1-2 : A Table of Our Data\nAccount number Name Password Balance\n0 Joe soup 100\n1 Mary nuts 3550\n2 Bill frisbee 1000\n3 Sue xxyyzz 750\n4 Henry PW 10000\nWith this approach, each row represents the data associated with a single\nbank account. While this is the same data, this grouping is a much more\nnatural way of representing an account.\nImplementation 5—List of Account Dictionaries\nTo implement this last approach, I’ll use a slightly more complicated data\nstructure. But each of the functions in the program must still have\naccess to the global list of accounts. As we’ll see in the next section,\ngranting functions access to all account data raises potential security risks.\nIdeally , each function should only be able to af fect the data of a single\naccount.\nCommon Problems with Procedural\nImplementation\nThe examples shown in this chapter share a common problem: all the data\nthe functions operate on is stored in one or more global variables. For the\nfollowing reasons, using lots of global data with procedural programming is\nbad coding practice:\n. Any function that uses and/or changes global data cannot easily be reused\nin a dif ferent program. A function that accesses global data is operating on\ndata that lives at a dif ferent (higher) level than the code of the function\nitself. That function will need a global  statement to access this data. Y ou\ncan’t just take a function that relies on global data and reuse it in another\nprogram; it can only be reused in a program with similar global data.\n. Many procedural programs tend to have lar ge collections of global\nvariables. By definition, a global variable can be used or changed by any\npiece of code anywhere in the program. Assignments to global variables are\noften widely scattered throughout procedural programs, both in the main', "code and inside functions. Because variable values can change anywhere, it\ncan be extremely dif ficult to debug and maintain programs written this way .\n. Functions written to use global data often have access to too much data.\nWhen a function uses a global list, dictionary , or any other global data\nstructure, it has access to all the data in that data structure. However ,\ntypically the function should operate on only one piece (or just a small\namount) of that data. Having the ability to read and modify any data in a\nlarge data structure can lead to errors, such as accidentally using or\noverwriting data that the function was not intended to touch.\nObject-Oriented Solution—First Look at a\nClass\nListing 1-7  is an object-oriented approach that combines all the code and\nassociated data of a single account. There are many new concepts here, and\nI will get into all the details starting in the next chapter . While I am not\nexpecting you to fully understand this example yet, notice that there is a\ncombination of code and data in a single script (called a class ). Here is your\nfirst look at object-oriented code.\nFile: Account.py\n# Account class  \n \nclass Account():  \n    def __init__(self, name, balance, password):  \n        self.name = name  \n        self.balance = int(balance)  \n        self.password = password  \n    \n    def deposit(self, amountToDeposit, password):  \n        if password != self.password:  \n            print('Sorry, incorrect password')  \n            return None  \n \n        if amountToDeposit < 0:  \n            print('You cannot deposit a negative amount')  \n            return None  \n ", "        self.balance = self.balance + amountToDeposit  \n        return self.balance  \n         \n    def withdraw(self, amountToWithdraw, password):  \n        if password != self.password:  \n            print('Incorrect password for this account')  \n            return None  \n \n        if amountToWithdraw < 0:  \n            print('You cannot withdraw a negative amount')  \n            return None  \n \n        if amountToWithdraw > self.balance:  \n            print('You cannot withdraw more than you have in  \nyour account')  \n            return None  \n \n        self.balance = self.balance - amountToWithdraw  \n        return self.balance  \n \n    def getBalance(self, password):  \n        if password != self.password:  \n            print('Sorry, incorrect password')  \n            return None  \n        return self.balance  \n \n    # Added for debugging  \n    def show(self):  \n        print('       Name:', self.name)  \n        print('       Balance:', self.balance)  \n        print('       Password:', self.password)  \n        print()\nListing 1-7 : First example of a class in Python\nFor now , take a look at the functions and see how they’re similar to our\nearlier procedural programming examples. Listing 2-2  shows the general form of a class in Python.\nclass <ClassName> (): \n \n    def __init__(self, <optional param1> , ..., <optional  \nparamN>): \n        #  any initialization code here  \n \n    # Any number of functions that access the data\n    # Each has the form:  \n \n    def <functionName1> (self, <optional param1> , ..., \n<optional paramN> ): \n        #  body of function  \n \n    #  ... more functions  \n \n    def <functionNameN> (self, <optional param1> , ..., \n<optional paramN> ): \n        #  body of function\nListing 2-2 : The typical form of a class in Python\nYou begin a class definition with a class  statement specifying the name\nyou want to give the class. The convention for class names is to use camel\ncase, with the first letter uppercase (for example, LightSwitch ). Following\nthe name you can optionally add a set of parentheses, but the statement\nmust end with a colon to indicate that you’re about to begin the body of the\nclass. (I’ll explain what can go inside the parentheses in Chapter 10 , when\nwe discuss inheritance.)\nWithin the body of the class, you can define any number of functions. All\nthe functions are considered part of the class, and the code that defines them\nmust be indented. Each function represents some behavior that an object\ncreated from the class can perform. OOP functions are given a special name: method .', 'method\nA function defined inside a class. A method always has at\nleast one parameter , which is usually named self.\nThe first method in every class should have the special name __init__ .\nWhenever you create an object from a class, this method will run\nautomatically . Therefore, this method is the logical place to put any\ninitialization code that you want to run whenever you instantiate an object\nfrom a class. The name __init__  is reserved by Python for this very task,\nand it must be written exactly this way , with two underscores before and\nafter the word init (which must be lowercase). In reality , the __init__()\nmethod is not strictly required. However , it’s generally considered good\npractice to include it and use it for initialization.\nNOTE\nWhen you instantiate an object fr om a class, Python takes car e of\nconstructing the object (allocating memory) for you. The special\n__init__()  method is called the “initializer” method, wher e you\ngive variables initial values. (Most other OOP languages r equir e a\nmethod named new() , which is often r eferr ed to as a constructor .)\nScope and Instance V ariables\nIn procedural programming, there are two principal levels of scope:\nvariables created in the main code have global  scope and are available\nanywhere in a program, while variables created inside a function have local\nscope and only live as long as the function runs. When the function exits,\nall local variables (variables with local scope) literally go away .\nObject-oriented programming and classes introduce a third level of\nscope, typically called object scope , though sometimes referred to as class\nscope  or more rarely as instance scope . They all mean the same thing: the\nscope consists of all the code inside the class definition.', 'Methods can have both local variables and instance variables . In a\nmethod, any variable whose name does not start with self.  is a local\nvariable and will go away when that method exits, meaning other methods\nwithin the class can no longer use that variable. Instance variables  have\nobject scope, which means they are available to all methods defined in a\nclass. Instance variables and object scope are the keys to understanding how\nobjects remember data.\ninstance variable\nIn a method, any variable whose name begins, by convention,\nwith the prefix self.  (for example, self.x ). Instance variables\nhave object scope.\nJust like local and global variables, instance variables are created when\nthey are first given a value and do not need any special declaration. The\n__init__()  method is the logical place to initialize instance variables. Here\nwe have an example of a class where the __init__()  method initializes an\ninstance variable self.count  (read as “self dot count”) to zero  and another\nmethod, increment() , that simply adds 1 to self.count :\nclass MyClass():  \n    def __init__(self):  \n        self.count = 0  #  create self.count and set it to 0  \n    def increment(self):  \n        self.count = self.count + 1  # increment the variable\nWhen you instantiate an object from the MyClass  class, the __init__()\nmethod runs and sets the value of the instance variable self.count  to zero.\nIf you then call the increment()  method, the value of self.count  goes\nfrom zero to one. If you call increment()  again, the value goes from one to\ntwo, and on and on.\nEach object created from a class gets its own set of instance variables,\nindependent of any other objects instantiated from that class. In the case of\nthe LightSwitch  class, there is only one instance variable,\nself.switchIsOn , so every LightSwitch  object will have its own\nself.switchIsOn . Therefore, you can have multiple LightSwitch  objects,', 'each with its own independent value of True  or False  for its\nself.switchIsOn  variable.\nDifferences Between Functions and Methods\nTo recap, there are three key dif ferences between a function and a method:\n. All methods of a class must be indented under the class  statement.\n. All methods have a special first parameter that (by convention) is named\nself .\n. Methods in a class can use instance variables, written in the form  self.\n<variableName> .\nNow that you know what methods are, I’ll show you how to create an\nobject from a class and how to use the dif ferent methods that are available\nin a class.\nCreating an Object from a Class\nAs I said earlier , a class simply defines what an object will look like. T o use\na class, you have to tell Python to make an object from the class. That variable then refers to the resulting object.', 'THE INSTANTIATION PROCESS\nFigure 2-3  shows the steps involved in instantiating a LightSwitch  object from the\nLightSwitch  class, going from the assignment statement into Python, then to the code\nof the class, then back out through Python again, and finally back to the assignment\nstatement.\nFigure 2-3 : The process of instantiating an object\nThe process consists of five steps:\nOur code asks Python to create an object from the LightSwitch  class.\nPython allocates space in memory for a LightSwitch  object, then calls the __init__ ()\nmethod of the LightSwitch  class, passing in the newly created object.\nThe __init__ () method of the LightSwitch  class runs. Y ou’ll get a better sense of this\nin Chapter 4 , where I’ll discuss how to create and maintain a lar ge number\nof instances made from a class.\nPython Data T ypes Are Implemented as Classes\nIt might not surprise you that all built-in data types in Python are\nimplemented as classes. Here is a simple example:\n>>> myString = 'abcde'  \n>>> print(type(myString))  \n<class 'str'>\nWe assign a string value to a variable. When we call the type()  function\nand print the results, we see that we have an instance of the str string class.\nThe str class gives us a number of methods we can call with strings,\nincluding myString.upper() , myString.lower() , myString.strip() , and\nso on.\nLists work in a similar way:\n>>> myList = [10, 20, 30, 40]  \n>>> print(type(myList))  \n<class 'list'>\nAll lists are instances of the list  class, which has many methods\nincluding myList.append() , myList.count() , myList.index() , and so on.\nWhen you write a class, you are defining a new data type. Y our code\nprovides the details by defining what data it maintains and what operations\nit can perform. After creating an instance of your class and assigning it to a\nvariable, you can use the type()  built-in function to determine the class\nused to create it, just like with a built-in data type. Here we instantiate a\nLightSwitch  object and print out its data type:\n>>> oLightSwitch = LightSwitch()  \n>>> print(type(oLightSwitch))  \n<class 'LightSwitch'>", 'Just like with Python’ s built-in data types, we can then use the variable\noLightSwitch  to call the methods available in the oLightSwitch  class.\nDefinition of an Object\nTo summarize this section, I’ll give my formal definition of an object .\nobject\nData, plus code that acts on that data, over time.\nA class defines what an object will look like when you instantiate one.\nAn object is a set of instance variables and the code of the methods in the\nclass from which the object was instantiated. Any number of objects can be\ninstantiated from a class, and each has its own set of instance variables.\nWhen you call a method of an object, the method runs and uses the set of\ninstance variables in that object.\nBuilding a Slightly More Complicated Class\nLet’s build on the concepts introduced so far and work through a second,\nslightly more complicated example in which we’ll make a dimmer switch\nclass. A dimmer switch has an on/of f switch, but it also has a multiposition\nslider that af fects the brightness of the light.\nThe slider can move through a range of brightness values. T o make\nthings straightforward, our dimmer digital slider has 1 1 positions, from 0\n(completely of f) through 10 (completely on). T o raise or lower the\nbrightness of the bulb to the maximum extent, you must move the slider\nthrough every possible setting.\nThis DimmerSwitch  class has more functionality than our LightSwitch\nclass and needs to remember more data:\nThe switch state (on or of f)\nBrightness level (0 to 10)\nAnd here are the behaviors a DimmerSwitch  object can perform:', "Turn on\nTurn of f\nRaise level\nLower level\nShow (for debugging)\nThe DimmerSwitch  class uses the standard template shown earlier in\nListing 2-2 : it starts with a class  statement and a first method named\n__init__() , then defines a number of additional methods, one for each of\nthe behaviors listed. The full code for this class is presented in Listing 2-5 .\nFile: DimmerSwitch.py\n# DimmerSwitch class  \n \nclass DimmerSwitch():  \n    def __init__(self):  \n        self.switchIsOn = False  \n        self.brightness = 0  \n \n    def turnOn(self):  \n        self.switchIsOn = True  \n \n    def turnOff(self):  \n        self.switchIsOn = False  \n \n    def raiseLevel(self):  \n        if self.brightness < 10:  \n            self.brightness = self.brightness + 1  \n \n    def lowerLevel(self):  \n        if self.brightness > 0:  \n            self.brightness = self.brightness - 1  \n \n    # Extra method for debugging  \n    def show(self):  \n        print(Switch is on?', self.switchIsOn)  \n        print('Brightness is:', self.brightness)\nListing 2-5 : The slightly mor e complicated DimmerSwitch  class", 'In this __init__()  method we have two instance variables: the familiar\nself.switchIsOn  and a new one, self.brightness , which remembers the\nbrightness level. W e assign starting values to both instance variables. All\nother methods can access the current value of each of these. In addition to\nturnOn()  and turnOff() , we include two new methods for this class:\nraiseLevel()  and lowerLevel() , which do exactly what their names\nimply . The show()  method is used during development and debugging and\njust prints the current values of the instance variables.\nThe main code in Listing 2-6  tests our class by creating a DimmerSwitch\nobject (oDimmer ), then calling the various methods.\nFile: OO_DimmerSwitch_with_T est_Code.py\n# DimmerSwitch class with test code  \n \nclass DimmerSwitch():  \n--- snipped code of DimmerSwitch class, as in Listing 2-5  --- \n \n# Main code  \noDimmer = DimmerSwitch()  \n \n# Turn switch on, and raise the level 5 times\noDimmer.turnOn()  \noDimmer.raiseLevel()  \noDimmer.raiseLevel()  \noDimmer.raiseLevel()  \noDimmer.raiseLevel()  \noDimmer.raiseLevel()  \noDimmer.show()  \n \n# Lower the level 2 times, and turn switch off  \noDimmer.lowerLevel()  \noDimmer.lowerLevel()  \noDimmer.turnOff()  \noDimmer.show()  \n \n# Turn switch on, and raise the level 3 times  \noDimmer.turnOn()  \noDimmer.raiseLevel()  \noDimmer.raiseLevel()  \noDimmer.raiseLevel()  \noDimmer.show()', 'Listing 2-6 : DimmerSwitch  class with test code\nWhen we run this code, the resulting output is:\nSwitch is on? True  \nBrightness is: 5  \nSwitch is on? False  \nBrightness is: 3  \nSwitch is on? True  \nBrightness is: 6\nThe main code creates the oDimmer  object, then makes calls to the\nvarious methods. Each time we call the show()  method, the on/of f state and\nthe brightness level are printed. The key thing to remember here is that\noDimmer  represents an object. W ith this\nmore complicated example, we’ll take a closer look at how ar guments work\nin classes.\nA television requires much more data than a light switch to represent its\nstate, and it has more behaviors. T o create a TV class, we must consider how\na user would typically use a TV and what the TV would have to remember .\nLet’s look at some of the important buttons on a typical TV remote ( Figur e\n2-4).', 'Figure 2-4 : A simplified TV remote\nFrom this, we can determine that to keep track of its state, a TV class\nwould have to maintain the following data:\nPower state (on or of f)\nMute state (is it muted?)\nList of channels available', 'Current channel setting\nCurrent volume setting\nRange of volume levels available\nAnd the actions that the TV must provide include:\nTurn the power on and of f\nRaise and lower the volume\nChange the channel up and down\nMute and unmute the sound\nGet information about the current settings\nGo to a specified channel\nThe code for our TV class is shown in Listing 2-7 . We include the\n__init__()  method for initialization, followed by a method for each of the\nbehaviors.\nFile: TV .py\n# TV class  \n \nclass TV():  \n    def __init__(self): 1 \n        self.isOn = False  \n        self.isMuted = False  \n        # Some default list of channels  \n        self.channelList = [2, 4, 5, 7, 9, 11, 20, 36, 44,  \n54, 65]   \n        self.nChannels = len(self.channelList)  \n        self.channelIndex = 0  \n        self.VOLUME_MINIMUM = 0   # constant  \n        self.VOLUME_MAXIMUM = 10  # constant  \n        self.volume = self.VOLUME_MAXIMUM //   # integer  \ndivide  \n \n    def power(self): 2 \n        self.isOn = not self.isOn   # toggle  \n \n    def volumeUp(self):  \n        if not self.isOn:  ', "            return  \n        if self.isMuted:  \n            self.isMuted = False  # changing the volume while  \nmuted unmutes the sound  \n        if self.volume < self.VOLUME_MAXIMUM:  \n            self.volume = self.volume + 1  \n \n    def volumeDown(self):  \n        if not self.isOn:  \n            return  \n        if self.isMuted:  \n            self.isMuted = False  # changing the volume while  \nmuted unmutes the sound  \n        if self.volume > self.VOLUME_MINIMUM:  \n            self.volume = self.volume - 1  \n \n    def channelUp(self): 3 \n        if not self.isOn:  \n            return  \n        self.channelIndex = self.channelIndex + 1  \n        if self.channelIndex > self.nChannels:  \n            self.channelIndex = 0  # wrap around to the first  \nchannel \n \n    def channelDown(self): 4 \n        if not self.isOn:  \n            return  \n        self.channelIndex = self.channelIndex - 1  \n        if self.channelIndex < 0:  \n            self.channelIndex = self.nChannels - 1  # wrap \naround to the top channel  \n \n    def mute(self): 5 \n        if not self.isOn:  \n            return  \n        self.isMuted = not self.isMuted  \n \n    def setChannel(self, newChannel):  \n        if newChannel in self.channelList:  \n            self.channelIndex =  \nself.channelList.index(newChannel)  \n        # if the newChannel is not in our list of channels,  \ndon't do anything  \n \n    def showInfo(self): 6 \n        print()  ", "        print('TV Status:')  \n        if self.isOn:  \n            print('    TV is: On')  \n            print('    Channel is:',  \nself.channelList[self.channelIndex])  \n            if self.isMuted:  \n                print('    Volume is:', self.volume, '(sound  \nis muted)')  \n            else:  \n                print('    Volume is:', self.volume)  \n        else:  \n            print('    TV is: Off')\nListing 2-7 : The TV class with many instance variables and methods\nThe __init__()  method 1 creates all the instance variables used in all\nthe methods and assigns reasonable starting values to each. T echnically , you\ncan create an instance variable inside any method; however , it is a good\nprogramming practice to define all instance variables in the __init__()\nmethod. This avoids the risk of an error when attempting to use an instance\nvariable in a method before it’ s been defined.\nThe power()  method 2 represents what happens when you push the\npower button on a remote. If the TV is of f, pushing the power button turns\nit on; if the TV is on, pushing the power button turns it of f. To code this\nbehavior I’ve used a toggle , which is a Boolean that’ s used to represent one\nof two states and can easily be switched between them. W ith this toggle, the\nnot operator switches the self.isOn  variable from True  to False , or from\nFalse  to True . The mute()  method code 5 does a similar thing, with the\nself.muted  variable toggling between muted and not-muted, but first has to\ncheck that the TV is on. If the TV is of f, calling the mute()  method has no\neffect.\nOne interesting thing to note is that we don’ t really keep track of the\ncurrent channel. Instead, we keep track of the index  of the current channel,\nwhich allows us to get the current channel at any time by using\nself.channelList[self.channelIndex] .\nThe channelUp()  3 and channelDown()  4 methods basically increment\nand decrement the channel index, but there is also some clever code in them\nto allow for wrap-around. If you’re currently at the last index in the channel", 'list and the user asks to go to the next channel up, the TV goes to the first\nchannel in the list. If you’re at the first index in the channel list and the user\nasks to go the next channel down, the TV goes to the last channel in the list.\nThe showInfo()  method 6 prints out the current status of the TV based\non the values of the instance variables (on/of f, current channel, current\nvolume setting, and mute setting).\nIn Listing 2-8 , we’ll create a TV object and call methods of that object.\nFile: OO_TV_with_T est_Code.py\n# TV class with test code  \n \n--- snipped code of TV class, as in Listing 2-7  --- \n \n# Main code  \noTV = TV()  # create the TV object  \n \n# Turn the TV on and show the status  \noTV.power()  \noTV.showInfo()  \n \n# Change the channel up twice, raise the volume twice, show  \nstatus \noTV.channelUp()  \noTV.channelUp()  \noTV.volumeUp()  \noTV.volumeUp()  \noTV.showInfo()  \n \n# Turn the TV off, show status, turn the TV on, show status  \noTV.power()  \noTV.showInfo()  \noTV.power()  \noTV.showInfo()  \n \n# Lower the volume, mute the sound, show status  \noTV.volumeDown()  \noTV.mute()  \noTV.showInfo()  \n \n# Change the channel to 11, mute the sound, show status  \noTV.setChannel(11)  ', 'oTV.mute()  \noTV.showInfo()\nListing 2-8 : TV class with test code\nWhen we run this code, here is what we get as output:\nTV Status:  \n    TV is: On  \n    Channel is: 2  \n    Volume is: 5  \n \nTV Status:  \n    TV is: On  \n    Channel is: 5  \n    Volume is: 7  \n \nTV Status:  \n    TV is: Off  \n \nTV Status:  \n    TV is: On  \n    Channel is: 5  \n    Volume is: 7  \n \nTV Status:  \n    TV is: On  \n    Channel is: 5  \n    Volume is: 6 (sound is muted)  \n \nTV Status:  \n    TV is: On  \n    Channel is: 11  \n    Volume is: 6\nAll of the methods are working correctly , and we get the expected output.\nPassing Arguments to a Method\nWhen calling any function, the number of ar guments must match the\nnumber of parameters listed in the matching def statement:\ndef myFunction(param1, param2, param3):  \n    # body of function  ', ' \n# call to a function:  \nmyFunction(argument1, argument2, argument3)\nThe same rule applies with methods and method calls. However , you may\nnotice that whenever we make a call to a method, it appears that we are\nspecifying one less ar gument than the number of parameters. For example,\nthe definition of the power()  method in our TV class looks like this:\ndef power(self):\nThis implies that the power()  method is expecting one value to be passed\nin and whatever is passed in will be assigned to the variable self . Yet when\nwe started by turning on the TV in Listing 2-8 , we made this call:\noTV.power()\nWhen we make the call, we don’ t explicitly pass anything inside the\nparentheses.\nThis may seem even stranger in the case of the setChannel()  method.\nThe method is written to accept two parameters:\ndef setchannel(self, newchannel):  \n    if newChannel in self.channelList:  \n        self.channelIndex =  \nself.channelList.index(newChannel)\nBut we called setChannel()  like this:\noTV.setChannel(11)\nIt appears that only one value is being passed in.\nYou might expect Python to generate an error here, due to a mismatch in\nthe number of ar guments (one) and the number of parameters (two). In\npractice, Python is doing a bit of behind-the-scenes work to make the\nsyntax easier to follow .\nLet’s examine this. Earlier , I said that to make a call to a method of an\nobject, you use the following generic syntax:', '<object> .<method> (<any arguments> )\nPython takes the <object>  you specify in the call and rearranges it to\nbecome the first ar gument. Any values in the parentheses of the method call\nare considered the subsequent ar gument(s). Thus, Python makes it appear\nthat you wrote this instead:\n<method of object> (<object> , <any arguments> )\nFigur e 2-5  shows how this works in our example code, again using the\nsetChannel()  method of the TV class.\nFigure 2-5 : Calling a method\nAlthough it looks like we’re only providing one ar gument here (for\nnewChannel ), there are really two ar guments passed in— oTV and 11—and\nthe method provides two parameters to receive these values ( self  and\nnewChannel , respectively). Python rearranges the ar guments for us when the\ncall is made. This may seem odd at first, but it will become second nature\nvery quickly . Writing the call with the object first makes it much easier for a\nprogrammer to see which object is being acted on.\nThis is a subtle but important feature. Remember that the object (in this\ncase, oTV) keeps the current settings of all of its instance variables. Passing\nthe object as the first ar gument allows the method to run with the values of\nthe instance variables of that object.\nMultiple Instances', "Every method is written with self  as the first parameter , so the self\nvariable receives the object used in each call. This has a major implication:\nit allows any method within a class to work with differ ent objects. I’ll\nexplain how this works using an example.\nIn Listing 2-9 , we’ll create two TV objects and save them in two\nvariables, oTV1  and oTV2 . This way , each TV object’ s instance variables can be manipulated\nindependently of those of any other TV object.\nInitialization Parameters\nThe ability to pass ar guments to method calls also works when instantiating\nan object. So far , when we’ve created our objects, we’ve always set their\ninstance variables to constant values. However , you’ll often want to create\ndifferent instances with dif ferent starting values. For example, imagine we\nwant to instantiate dif ferent TVs and identify them using their brand name\nand location. This way , we can dif ferentiate between a Samsung television\nin the family room and a Sony television in the bedroom. Constant values\nwould not work for us in this situation.\nTo initialize an object with dif ferent values, we add parameters to the\ndefinition of the __init__()  method, like this:\n# TV class  \n \nclass TV():  \n    def __init__(self, brand, location):  # pass in a brand  \nand location for the TV  \n        self.brand = brand  \n        self.location = location  \n        --- snipped remaining initialization of TV ---  \n        ...  ', "In all methods, parameters are local variables, so they literally go away\nwhen the method ends. For example, in the __init__()  method of the TV\nclass shown here, brand  and location  are local variables that will\ndisappear when the method ends. However , we often want to save values\nthat are passed in via parameters to use them in other methods.\nIn order to allow an object to remember initial values, the standard\napproach is to store any values passed in into instance variables. Since\ninstance variables have object scope, they can be used in other methods in\nthe class. The Python convention is that the name of the instance variable\nshould be the same as the parameter name, but prefixed with self  and a\nperiod:\ndef __init__(self, someVariableName):  \n    self.someVariableName = someVariableName\nIn the TV class, the line after the def statement tells Python to take the\nvalue of the brand  parameter and assign it to an instance variable named\nself.brand . The next line does the same thing with the location\nparameter and the instance variable self.location . After these\nassignments, we can use self.brand  and self.location  in other methods.\nUsing this approach, we can create multiple objects from the same class\nbut start each of f with dif ferent data. Here are a few\nexamples of how we might use this:\nSay we wanted to model a student in a course. W e could have a Student\nclass that has instance variables for name , emailAddress , currentGrade ,\nand so on. Each Student  object we create from this class would have its", 'own set of these instance variables, and the values given to the instance\nvariables would be dif ferent for each student.\nConsider a game where we have multiple players. A player could be\nmodeled by a Player  class with instance variables for name , points ,\nhealth , location , and so on. Each player would have the same capabilities,\nbut the methods could work dif ferently based on the dif ferent values in the\ninstance variables.\nImagine an address book. W e could create a Person  class with instance\nvariables for name , address , phoneNumber , and birthday . We could create\nas many objects from the Person  class as we want, one for each person we\nknow . The instance variables in each Person  object would contain dif ferent\nvalues. W e could then write code to search through all the Person  objects\nand retrieve information about the one or ones we are looking for .\nIn future chapters, I will explore this concept of instantiating multiple\nobjects from a single class and give you tools to help manage a collection of\nobjects.\nOOP as a Solution\nToward the end of Chapter 1 , I mentioned three problems that are inherent\nin procedural coding. Hopefully , after working through the examples in this\nchapter , you can see how object-oriented programming solves all of those\nproblems:\n. A well-written class can be easily reused in many dif ferent programs.\nClasses do not need to access global data. Instead, objects provide code and\ndata at the same level.\n. Object-oriented programming can greatly reduce the number of global\nvariables required, because a class provides a framework in which data and\ncode that acts on the data exist in one grouping. This also tends to make\ncode easier to debug.\n. Objects created from a class only have access to their own data—their set of\nthe instance variables in the class. Even when you have multiple objects\ncreated from the same class, they do not have access to each other ’s data.', 'Summary\nIn this chapter , I provided an introduction to object-oriented programming\nby demonstrating the relationship between a class and an object. The class\ndefines the shape and capabilities of an object. An object is a single\ninstance of a class that has its own set of all the data defined in the instance\nvariables of the class. Each piece of data you want an object to contain is\nstored in an instance variable, which has object scope, meaning that it is\navailable within all methods defined in the class. All objects created from\nthe same class get their own set of all the instance variables, and because\nthese may contain dif ferent values, calling the methods on dif ferent objects\ncan result in dif ferent behavior .\nI showed how you create an object from a class, typically through an\nassignment statement. After instantiating an object, you can use it to make\ncalls to any method defined in the class of that object. I also showed how\nyou can instantiate multiple objects from the same class.\nIn this chapter , the demonstration classes implemented physical objects\n(light switches, TVs). This is a good way to start understanding the\nconcepts of a class and an object. However , in future chapters, I will\nintroduce objects that do not represent physical objects.', '3\nMENTAL MODELS OF OBJECTS AND\nTHE MEANING OF “SELF”\nHopefully the new concepts and\nterminology I’ve introduced so far are\nstarting to make sense. Some people\nnew to OOP have trouble envisioning\nwhat an object is and how the methods\nof an object work with its instance variables. The\nspecifics are fairly complex, so it can be helpful to\ndevelop a mental model of how objects and classes\noperate.\nIn this chapter , I’ll present two mental models of OOP . Right up front, I\nwant to be clear that neither of these models is an exact representation of\nhow objects work in Python. Instead, these models are intended to give you\na way to think about what an object looks like and what happens when you\ncall a method. This chapter will also go into more detail about self  and\nshow how it is used to make methods work with multiple objects\ninstantiated from the same class. Throughout the rest of the book, you’ll\ngain a much deeper insight into objects and classes.\nRevisiting the DimmerSwitch Class', "In the following examples, we’ll continue with the DimmerSwitch  class\nfrom Chapter 2  (Listing 2-5). The DimmerSwitch  class already has two\ninstance variables: self.isOn  and self.brightness . These variables are created and assigned initial values in the\n__init__()  method. They are then accessed or modified in the five other\nmethods of the class.\nListing 3-1  provides some test code to create three DimmerSwitch  objects\nfrom the DimmerSwitch  class, which we’ll use in our mental models. I’ll\ncall various methods for each of the DimmerSwitch  objects.\nFile: OO_DimmerSwitch_Model1.py\n# Create first DimmerSwitch, turn it on, and raise the level  \ntwice \noDimmer1 = DimmerSwitch('Dimmer1')  \noDimmer1.turnOn()  \noDimmer1.raiseLevel()  \noDimmer1.raiseLevel()  \n \n# Create second DimmerSwitch, turn it on, and raise the level  \n3 times \noDimmer2 = DimmerSwitch('Dimmer2')  \noDimmer2.turnOn()  \noDimmer2.raiseLevel()  \noDimmer2.raiseLevel()  \noDimmer2.raiseLevel()  \n \n# Create third DimmerSwitch, using the default settings  \noDimmer3 = DimmerSwitch('Dimmer3')  \n \n# Ask each switch to show itself  \noDimmer1.show()  \noDimmer2.show()  \noDimmer3.show()\nListing 3-1 : Creating thr ee DimmerSwitch  objects and calling various\nmethods on each", 'When run with our DimmerSwitch  class, this code gives the following\noutput:\nLabel: Dimmer1  \nLight is on? In Python,\nhowever , the variable named self  does have a highly specialized and clear\nmeaning. In this section, I’ll show how self  is given a value and how the\ncode of the methods in a class work with the instance variables of any\nobject instantiated from the class.", 'NOTE\nThe variable name self  is not a keywor d in Python but is used by\nconvention—any other name could be used and the code would\nwork fine. However , using self  is a universally accepted practice in\nPython, and I will use it thr oughout this book. If you want your code\nto be understood by other Python pr ogrammers, use the name self\nas the first parameter in all methods of a class. (Other OOP\nlanguages have the same concept but use other names, such as this\nor me.)\nSuppose you write a class named SomeClass , then create an object from\nthat class, like this:\noSomeObject = SomeClass( <optional arguments> )\nThe object oSomeObject  contains a set of all the instance variables\ndefined in the class. Every method of the SomeClass  class has a definition\nthat looks like this:\ndef someMethod(self, <any other parameters> ):\nAnd here is the general form of a call to such a method:\noSomeObject.someMethod( <any other arguments> )\nAs we know , Python rearranges the ar guments in a call to a method, so\nthat the object is passed in as the first ar gument. That value is received in\nthe first parameter of the method and is put into the variable self  (Figur e\n3-4).\nFigure 3-4 : How Python rearranges arguments in a call to a method', "Therefore, whenever a method is called, self  will be set to the object in\nthe call. This means that the code of a method can operate on the instance\nvariables of any object instantiated from the class. It does so using the form:\nself.<instanceVariableName>\nThis essentially says to use the object referred to by self  and access the\ninstance variable specified by <instanceVariableName >. Since every\nmethod uses self  as the first parameter , every method in a class uses this\nsame approach.\nTo illustrate this concept, let’ s use the DimmerSwitch  class.
