Now ,\nself.brightness  refers to the brightness  instance variable of oDimmer2 .\nNo matter what object we use or which method we call, the value of the\nobject is assigned to the variable self  in the called method. Y ou should', 'think of self  as meaning the current object—the object that the method was\ncalled with. Whenever a method executes, it uses the set of instance\nvariables for the object specified in the call.\nFigure 3-6 : The program in Listing 3-3  stopped in call to oDimmer2.raiseLevel()\nSummary\nIn this chapter , I presented two dif ferent ways of thinking about objects.\nThese mental models should help in developing a basic understanding of\nwhat happens when you instantiate multiple instances of an object from a\nclass.\nThe first model showed how you can think of an object as having a set of\nall the instance variables and all the methods of a class wrapped up in a nice\nbundle.', 'The second model went into much greater detail about the\nimplementation, explaining that the code of a class exists only in one place.\nAn important takeaway is that creating new objects from a class is space-\nefficient. When you create a new instance of an object, Python allocates\nmemory to represent the instance variables defined in the class. No\nduplicates of the class’ s code are made or required.\nThe key to how methods work with multiple objects is that the first\nparameter of all methods, self , is always set to the object used in a call to\nthat method. W ith this approach, every method uses the instance variables\nfor the current object.', '4\nMANAGING MULTIPLE OBJECTS\nThis chapter will show you techniques for\nmanaging any number of objects\ninstantiated from the same class. I’ll first\ngo through an OOP implementation of the\nbank account example from Chapter 1. The\nOOP approach allows the data and code of an account to\nbe at the same level, eliminating the need to depend on\nglobal data. Then, I’ll split the program into main code\nthat provides a top-level menu and a separate Bank object\nthat manages accounts, in addition to any number of\nAccount objects. We’ll also discuss a better way of\nhandling errors using exceptions.\nBank Account Class\nOur bank account class will need, at a minimum, a name, password, and balance\nas its data. For behaviors, the user must be able to create an account, deposit and\nwithdraw money , and check their balance.\nWe’ll define and initialize the variables for the name, password, and balance,\nand build methods to implement each of the operations. W e should then be able to\ninstantiate any number of Account  objects. Like the initial class from Chapter 1 ,\nthis is a simplified Account  class that only uses integers for the balance and keeps\nthe password in cleartext. While you wouldn’ t use simplifications like these in a', "real banking application, they will allow us to concentrate on the OOP aspects\ninvolved.\nThe new code for the Account  class is presented in Listing 4-1 .\nFile: Account.py\n# Account class  \n \nclass Account():  \n 1 def __init__(self, name, balance, password):  \n        self.name = name  \n        self.balance = int(balance)  \n        self.password = password  \n    \n 2 def deposit(self, amountToDeposit, password):  \n        if password != self.password:  \n            print('Sorry, incorrect password')  \n            return None  \n \n        if amountToDeposit < 0:  \n            print('You cannot deposit a negative amount')  \n            return None  \n \n        self.balance = self.balance + amountToDeposit  \n        return self.balance  \n         \n 3 def withdraw(self, amountToWithdraw, password):  \n        if password != self.password:  \n            print('Incorrect password for this account')  \n            return None  \n \n        if amountToWithdraw < 0:  \n            print('You cannot withdraw a negative amount')  \n            return None  \n \n        if amountToWithdraw > self.balance:  \n            print('You cannot withdraw more than you have in your  \naccount')  \n            return None  \n \n        self.balance = self.balance - amountToWithdraw  \n        return self.balance  \n \n 4 def getBalance(self, password):  \n        if password != self.password:  \n            print('Sorry, incorrect password')  \n            return None  ", "        return self.balance  \n \n   # Added for debugging  \n 5 def show(self):  \n        print('       Name:', self.name)  \n        print('       Balance:', self.balance)  \n        print('       Password:', self.password)  \n        print()\nListing 4-1 : A minimal Account  class\nNOTE\nThe err or handling in Listing 4-1  is very simple. If we find an err or\ncondition, we print an err or message and r eturn a special value of None .\nLater in the chapter , I will show a better way of handling err ors.\nNotice how these methods manipulate and remember data. Data is passed into\neach method through parameters, which are local variables that only exist while\nthe method is running. Data is remembered in instance variables, which have\nobject scope and therefore remember their values across calls to dif ferent\nmethods.\nFirst we have the __init__()  method 1, with three parameters. When an object\nis created from this class, three pieces of data are required: name, balance, and\npassword. The instantiation might look like this:\noAccount = Account('Joe Schmoe', 1000, 'magic')\nWhen we instantiate the object, the values of the three ar guments are passed\ninto the __init__()  method, which in turn assigns these values to the similarly\nnamed instance variables: self.name , self.balance , and self.password . We’ll\naccess these instance variables in the other methods.\nThe deposit()  method 2 lets the user make a deposit into an account. After\ninstantiating an Account  object and saving it in oAccount , we could call the\ndeposit()  method like this:\nnewBalance = oAccount.deposit(500, 'magic')\nThis call says to deposit $500 and gives the password “magic”. The method\nperforms two validity checks on the deposit request. The first ensures that the", "password is correct by testing the password provided in the call against the\npassword set when the Account  object was created. This is a good example of\nhow the original password saved in the instance variable self.password  is used.\nThe second validity check makes sure we aren’ t depositing a negative amount\n(which would actually be a withdrawal).\nIf either of those tests fails, for now we return the special value None  to show\nthat some error occurred. If both tests pass, we increment the instance variable\nself.balance  by the amount of the deposit. Because the balance is stored in\nself.balance , it is remembered and is available for future calls. Finally , we\nreturn the new balance.\nThe withdraw()  method 3 works in a very similar way and would be called\nlike this:\noAccount.withdraw(250, 'magic')\nThe withdraw()  method checks that we’ve supplied the proper password by\nverifying it against the instance variable self.password . It also checks that we’re\nnot asking to withdraw a negative amount or more than we have in the account,\nusing the instance variable self.balance . Once those tests pass, the method\ndecrements self.balance  by the amount to withdraw . It returns the resulting\nbalance.\nTo check the balance 4, we only need to supply the proper password for the\naccount:\ncurrentBalance = oAccount.getBalance('magic')\nIf the password supplied matches the one saved in the instance variable\nself.password , the method returns the value in self.balance .\nFinally , for debugging, we added a show()  method 5 to display the current\nvalues of self.name , self.balance , and self.password  saved for the account.\nThe Account  class is our first example of representing something that is not a\nphysical object. A bank account is not something that you can see, feel, or touch.\nHowever , it fits perfectly into the world of computer objects because it has data\n(name, balance, password) and actions that work on that data (create, deposit,\nwithdraw , get balance, show).\nImporting Class Code", 'There are two ways to use a class you’ve built in your own code. As we’ve seen\nin previous chapters, the simplest way is to place all the code of the class directly\nin the main Python source file. But doing so makes it dif ficult to reuse a class.\nA second approach is to place the code of the class in a file by itself and import\nit into a program that uses it. W e’ve placed all the code for our Account  class in\nAccount.py , but if we try to run Account.py  by itself, nothing happens, because\nit’s just the definition of a class. T o use our class code, we must instantiate one or\nmore objects and make calls to the object’ s methods. As our classes become\nlarger and more complicated, saving each of them as a separate file is the\npreferred way to work with them.\nTo use our Account  class, we must build another .py file and import the code\nfrom Account.py , as we do with other built-in packages like random  and time .\nOften, Python programmers name the main program that imports other class files\nmain.py  or Main_<SomeName>.py . We must then ensure that Account.py  and the\nmain program file are in the same folder . At the beginning of the main program,\nwe bring in the Account  code by starting with an import  statement (notice that we\nleave of f the *.py file extension):\nfrom Account import *\nUsing the import  statement with an asterisk ( *) brings in the entire contents of\nthe imported file. An imported file can contain multiple classes. In this case,\nwhere possible, you should specify the specific class or classes that you want to\nimport, rather than importing the whole file. Here’ s the syntax for importing\nparticular classes:\nfrom <ExternalFile>  import <ClassName1>, <ClassName2>, ...\nThere are two benefits to importing class code:\n. The module is reusable, so if we want to use Account.py  in some other project, we\njust need to make a copy of the file and place it into that project’ s folder . Reusing\ncode in this way is a staple of object-oriented programming.\n. If your class code is included in the main program, every time you run the\nprogram, Python compiles all the code in your class (translates it into a lower -\nlevel language that is more easily runnable on your computer), even if you have\nnot made any changes to the class.\nHowever , when you run your main program with your class code imported,\nPython optimizes the compile step without you having to do anything. It creates a', 'folder named __pycache__  in the project folder , then compiles the code in your\nclass file and saves the compiled code in the __pycache__  folder with a variant of\nthe original Python filename. For example, for the Account.py file, Python will\ncreate a file using the name Account.cpython-39.pyc  (or similar , based on the\nversion of Python you are using). The .pyc extension stands for Python Compiled .\nPython only recompiles your class file if the source of the class file changes. If\nthe source of your Account.py  has not changed, Python knows it doesn\'t need to\nrecompile it and can more ef ficiently use the .pyc version of the file instead.\nCreating Some Test Code\nWe’ll test our new class with four main programs. We also create an account for Mary with account number 1 3 and\nperform some test operations on her account at 6 and 7.\nThe results are identical to the test code from Listing 4-3 . However , there is one\nhighly significant dif ference between the two test programs: now there is only the\nsingle global variable accountsList . Each account has a unique account number ,", 'which we use to access a specific account. W e have taken an important step in\nreducing the number of global variables.\nAnother important thing to note here is that we made some fairly major\nchanges to the main program, but we did not touch anything in the Account  class\nfile. OOP often allows you to hide details at dif ferent levels. If we assume that the\ncode of the Account  class takes care of details related to an individual account, we\ncan concentrate on ways to make the main code better .\nNotice also that we’re using the variable oAccount  as a temporary  variable.\nThat is, whenever we create a new Account  object, we are assigning the result to\nthe variable oAccount . Right after doing that, we append oAccount  to our list of\naccounts. W e never use the variable oAccount  in calls to any method of a specific\nAccount  object. That way , we can reuse the variable oAccount  to receive the\nvalue of the next account that is created.\nMultiple Objects with Unique Identifiers\nThe Account  objects must be individually identifiable so each user can make\ndeposits and withdrawals and get the balance of their specific account. Using a\nlist for our bank accounts works, but there’ s a serious flaw . Imagine we have five\naccounts, numbered 0, 1, 2, 3, and 4. If the person who owns account 2 decides to\nclose their account, we’d likely use a standard pop()  operation on the list to\ndelete account 2. This would cause a domino ef fect: the account that was in\nposition 3 is now in position 2, and the account that was in position 4 is now in\nposition 3. However , the users of these accounts still have their original account\nnumbers, 3 and 4. As a result, the customer who owns account 3 will now get the\ninformation for the previous account 4, and account number 4 is now an invalid\nindex.\nTo handle lar ge numbers of objects with unique identifiers, we generally use a\ndictionary . Unlike a list, a dictionary will allow us to delete accounts without\naltering the account numbers associated with them. W e build each key/value pair\nwith an account number as the key and an Account  object as the value. That way ,\nif we need to eliminate a given account, no other account is af fected. A dictionary\nof accounts would look like this:\n{0 : <object for account 0> , 1 : <object for account 1> , ... }\nWe can then easily get the associated Account  object and call a method like\nthis:', "oAccount = accountsDict[accountNumber]  \noAccount.someMethodCall()\nAlternatively , we could use the accountNumber  directly to make a call to a\nmethod of an individual Account :\naccountsDict[accountNumber].someMethodCall()\nListing 4-5  shows the test code using a dictionary of Account  objects. Again,\nwhile we’re making many changes to our test code, we’re not changing a single\nline in the Account  class. The program first\nprovides a simple menu interface. Then, when an action is chosen, it collects data\nand makes a call to a method of an Account  object. Rather than having one lar ge\nmain program that does two dif ferent tasks, we can split this code into two\nsmaller logical units, each with a clearly defined role. The menuing system\nbecomes the main code that decides what action to take, and the rest of the code", 'deals with the things a bank actually does. The bank can be modeled as an object\nthat manages other (account) objects, known as an object manager object .\nobject manager object\nAn object that maintains a list or dictionary of managed objects\n(typically of a single class) and calls methods of those objects.\nThis split can be made easily and logically: we take all the code related to the\nbank and put it into a new Bank  class. Then, at the beginning of the main\nprogram, we instantiate a single Bank  object from the new Bank  class.\nThe Bank  class will manage a list or dictionary of Account  objects. In this way ,\nthe Bank  object will be the only code that communicates directly with Account\nobjects ( Figur e 4-1 ).\nTo create this hierarchy , we need some main code that handles the highest-level\nmenuing system. In response to a choice of action, the main code will call a\nmethod of the Bank  object (for example, deposit()  or withdraw() ). The Bank\nobject will gather the information it needs (account number , password, amount to\ndeposit or withdraw), reach into its dictionary of accounts to find the matching\nuser account, and call the appropriate method for that user ’s account.\nFigure 4-1 : The main code manages a Bank object, which manages many Account  objects.\nThere are three layers in this division of labor:\n. The main code that creates and talks to a single Bank  object', '. The Bank  object that manages a dictionary of Account  objects and calls methods\nof those objects\n. The Account  objects themselves\nWith this approach, we only have a single global variable, the Bank  object. In\nfact, the main code has no idea that Account  objects even exist. Conversely , each\nAccount  object has no clue (and does not care) what the top-level user interface of\nthe program is. The Bank  object receives messages from the main code and\ncommunicates with the appropriate Account  object.\nThe key advantage of this approach is that we have broken down a much lar ger\nprogram into smaller subprograms: in this case, the main code and two classes.\nThis makes it much easier to program each piece, since the scope of work is\nsmaller and the responsibilities are clearer for each. Further , having only a single\nglobal variable ensures that lower -level code will not accidentally af fect data at\nthe global level.\nIn computer literature, the construct shown in Figur e 4-1  is often known as\ncomposition  or object composition .\ncomposition\nA logical structure in which one object manages one or more other\nobjects\nYou can think of one object as being made up of other objects. An example is\nthat a car object is made up of an engine object, a steering wheel object, some\nnumber of door objects, four wheel and tire objects, and so on. The discussion\noften centers around the relationships between objects. In this example, one\nwould say that a car “has a” steering wheel, an engine, some number of doors,\nand so on. Therefore, the car object is a composite of other objects.\nWe’ll have three separate files. The main code lives in its own file. The Bank  object removes the\nspecific account from its dictionary of accounts using a line like this:\ndel self.accountsDict[userAccountNumber]\nRecall that our definition of an object is data, plus code that acts on that data,\nover time. The ability to delete an object demonstrates the third part of our\ndefinition of an object. W e can create an object (in this case an Account  object)\nwhenever we want, not just when a program starts. In this program, we create a\nnew Account  object whenever a user decides to open an account. Our code can\nuse that object by calling its methods. W e can also delete an object at any time, in\nthis case, when a user chooses to close their account. This is an example of how\nan object (like an Account  object) has a life span, from whenever it is created it to\nwhenever it is deleted.", "Better Error Handling with Exceptions\nSo far in our Account  class, if a method detects an error (for example, if the user\ndeposits a negative amount, enters an incorrect password, withdraws a negative\namount, and so on) our placeholder solution is to return None  as a signal that\nsomething went wrong. In this section, we’ll discuss a better way of handling\nerrors by using try/except  blocks and raising exceptions.\ntry and except\nWhen a runtime error or abnormal condition occurs in a function or method from\nthe Python Standard Library , that function or method signals the error by raising\nan exception (sometimes referred to as throwing  or generating  an exception). W e\ncan detect and react to exceptions using the try/except  construct. Here is the\ngeneral form:\ntry:    \n    # some code that may cause an error (raise an exception)  \nexcept <some exception name> :  # if an exception happens  \n    # some code to handle the exception\nIf the code inside the try block works correctly and does not generate an\nexception, the except  clause is skipped, and execution continues after the except\nblock. However , if the code in the try block results in an exception, control is\npassed to the except  statement. If the exception matches the exception (or one of\nmultiple exceptions) listed in the except  statement, control is transferred to the\ncode of the except  clause. This is often referred to as catching  the exception.\nThat indented block typically contains code to report and/or recover from the\nerror .\nHere is a simple example where we ask for a number from the user and attempt\nto convert it to an integer:\nage = input('Please enter your age: ')  \ntry:  # attempt to convert to integer  \n    age = int(age)  \nexcept ValueError:  # if an exception is raised trying to convert  \n    print('Sorry, that was not a valid number')\nCalls to the Python Standard Library can generate standard exceptions such as\nTypeError , ValueError , NameError , ZeroDivisionError , and so on. In this\nexample, if the user enters letters or a floating-point number , the built-in int()", "function raises a ValueError  exception, and control is transferred to the code in\nthe except  block.\nThe raise Statement and Custom Exceptions\nIf your code detects a runtime error condition, you can use the raise  statement to\nsignal an exception. There are many forms of the raise  statement, but the\nstandard approach is to use this syntax:\nraise <ExceptionName> ('<Any string to describe the error> ')\nFor the <ExceptionName> , you have three options. First, if there is a standard\nexception that matches the error you have detected ( TypeError , ValueError ,\nNameError , ZeroDivisionError , and so on), it’ s fine to use that. Y ou can also add\nyour own description string:\nraise ValueError('You need to specify an integer')\nSecond, you can use the generic Exception  exception:\nraise Exception('The amount cannot be a floating-point number')\nHowever , this is generally frowned upon because the standard practice is to\nwrite except  statements to look for exceptions by name, and this does not provide\na specific name.\nA third choice, and perhaps the best, is to create your own custom exception.\nThis is easy to do, but involves a technique called inheritance (which we will\ndiscuss at length in Chapter 10 ). Here is all you need to create your own\nexception:\n# Define a custom exception  \nclass <CustomExceptionName> (Exception ): \n    pass\nYou supply a unique name for your exception. Y ou can then raise  your custom\nexception in your code. Creating your own exceptions means you can explicitly\ncheck for these exceptions by name in a higher level of your code. In the next\nsection, we’ll rewrite the code of our bank example so that we raise a custom\nexception in our Bank  and Account  classes and check for and report the error in\nthe main code. The main code will report the error but allow the program to\ncontinue running.", 'In the typical case, the raise  statement causes the current function or method\nto exit and transfers control back to the caller . If the caller contains an except\nclause that catches the exception, execution continues inside that except  clause.\nOtherwise, that function or method exits. This process is repeated until an except\nclause catches the exception. Control is transferred back through the sequence of\ncalls, and if no except  clause catches the exception, the program quits and Python\ndisplays the error .\nUsing Exceptions in Our Bank Program\nWe can now rewrite all three levels of our program (main, Bank , and Account ) to\nsignal errors with raise  statements and to handle errors using try/except  blocks.\nAccount Class with Exceptions\nListing 4-9  is a new version of the Account  class rewritten to use exceptions and\noptimized so that no code is repeated. W e start by defining a custom\nAbortTransaction  exception, which will be raised if we discover some error\nwhile a user is attempting to do a transaction in our bank.\nFile: BankOOP6_UsingExceptions/Account.py (modified to work with\nupcoming Bank.py)\n# Account class\n# Errors indicated by "raise" statements  \n \n# Define a custom exception  \nclass AbortTransaction(Exception): 1 \n    \'\'\'raise this exception to abort a bank transaction\'\'\'  \n    pass  \n \nclass Account():  \n    def __init__(self, name, balance, password):  \n        self.name = name  \n        self.balance = self.validateAmount(balance) 2 \n        self.password = password  \n \n    def validateAmount(self, amount):  \n        try:  \n            amount = int(amount)  \n        except ValueError:  \n            raise AbortTransaction(\'Amount must be an integer\') 3 \n        if amount <= 0:  ', "            raise AbortTransaction('Amount must be positive') 4 \n        return amount  \n \n    def checkPasswordMatch(self, password): 5 \n        if password != self.password:  \n            raise AbortTransaction('Incorrect password for this  \naccount')  \n \n    def deposit(self, amountToDeposit): 6 \n        amountToDeposit = self.validateAmount(amountToDeposit)  \n        self.balance = self.balance + amountToDeposit  \n        return self.balance  \n \n    def getBalance(self):  \n        return self.balance  \n         \n    def withdraw(self, amountToWithdraw): 7 \n        amountToWithdraw = self.validateAmount(amountToWithdraw)  \n        if amountToWithdraw > self.balance:  \n            raise AbortTransaction('You cannot withdraw more than  \nyou have in your account')  \n        self.balance = self.balance - amountToWithdraw  \n        return self.balance  \n \n    # Added for debugging  \n    def show(self):  \n        print('       Name:', self.name)  \n        print('       Balance:', self.balance)  \n        print('       Password:', self.password)\nListing 4-9 : A modified Account  class that raises exceptions\nWe start by defining our custom AbortTransaction  exception 1 so we can use\nit in this class and in other code that imports this class.\nIn the __init__()  method of the Account  class, we ensure that the amount\nprovided as the starting balance is valid by calling validateAmount()  2. This\nmethod uses a try/except  block to ensure that the starting amount can\nsuccessfully be converted to an integer . If the call to int()  fails, it raises a\nValueError  exception, which is caught in the except  clause. Rather than just\nallowing the generic ValueError  to be returned to the caller , the code of this\nexcept  block 3 executes a raise  statement, raising our AbortTransaction\nexception, and includes a more meaningful error message string. If the conversion\nto an integer succeeds, we perform another test. If the user gave a negative\namount, we also raise the AbortTransaction  exception 4, but with a dif ferent\nerror message string.", "The checkPasswordMatch()  method 5 is called by methods in the Bank  object\nto check if the password supplied by the user matches the password saved in the\nAccount . If not, we execute another raise  statement with the same exception, but\nwe supply a more descriptive error message string.\nThis allows the code of deposit()  6 and withdraw()  7 to be simplified,\nbecause these methods assume that the amount has been validated and the\npassword verified before they are invoked. There is an additional check in\nwithdraw()  to ensure that the user is not trying to withdraw more money than is\nin the account; if so, we raise the AbortTransaction  exception with an\nappropriate description.\nSince there is no code in this class to handle an AbortTransaction  exception,\nany time one is raised, control is passed back to the caller . If the caller has no\ncode to handle the exception, then control is passed back to the previous caller ,\nand so on up the stack of calls. As we’ll see, our main code will handle this\nexception.\nOptimized Bank Class\nThe full Bank  class code is available for download. Let’ s say you’re\ncoding a game and you need to have some number of bad guys, spaceships,\nbullets, zombies, or whatever else. Each such object will typically have some data\nit remembers and some actions it can perform. As long as each object does not\nrequire a unique identifier , the standard way to handle this is to create many\ninstances of the object from the class and put all the objects into a list:\nobjectList = []  # start off with an empty list  \nfor i in range(nObjects):  \n    oNewObject = MyClass()   # create a new instance  \n    objectList.append(oNewObject)  # store the object in the list\nIn our game, we represent a world as a lar ge grid, like a spreadsheet. W e want\nmonsters placed at random locations in the grid. Listing 4-12  shows the start of a\nMonster  class with its __init__()  method and a move()  method. When a\nMonster  is instantiated, it is told the number of rows and columns in the grid and\nthe maximum speed, and it chooses a random starting location and speed.\nFile: MonsterExample.py\nimport random  \n \nclass Monster()  \n    def __init__(self, nRows, nCols, maxSpeed):  \n        self.nRows = nRows  # save away  \n        self.nCols = nCols  # save away  \n        self.myRow = random.randrange(self.nRows) # chooses a  \nrandom row  \n        self.myCol = random.randrange(self.nCols) # chooses a  \nrandom col  \n        self.mySpeedX = random.randrange(-maxSpeed, maxSpeed + 1)  ', '# chooses an X speed  \n        self.mySpeedY = random.randrange(-maxSpeed, maxSpeed + 1)  \n# chooses a Y speed\n        # Set other instance variables like health, power, etc.  \n \n    def move(self):  \n        self.myRow = (self.myRow + self.mySpeedY) %  self.nRows  \n        self.myCol = (self.myCol + self.mySpeedX) % self.nCols\nListing 4-12 : A Monster  class that can be used to instantiate many Monster s\nWith this Monster  class, we can create a list of Monster  objects like this:\nN_MONSTERS = 20  \nN_ROWS = 100   # could be any size  \nN_COLS = 200   # could be any size  \nMAX_SPEED = 4  \nmonsterList = []  # start with an empty list  \nfor i in range(N_MONSTERS):  \n    oMonster = Monster(N_ROWS, N_COLS, MAX_SPEED)  # create a  \nMonster \n    monsterList.append(oMonster)  # add the Monster to our list\nThis loop will instantiate 20 Monster s, and each will know its own starting\nlocation in the grid and its individual speed. Once you have a list of objects, later\nin the program when you want each object to do the same action, you can write a\nsimple loop where you call the same method of each object in the list:\nfor objectVariable in objectVariablesList:  \n    objectVariable.someMethod()\nFor example, if we want each of our Monster  objects to move, we could use a\nloop like this:\nfor oMonster in monsterList:  \n    oMonster.move()\nSince each Monster  object remembers its location and speed, in the move()\nmethod, each Monster  can move to and remember its new location.\nThis technique of building a list of objects and calling the same method of all\nobjects in the list is extremely useful, and it’ s a standard approach to dealing with\na collection of similar objects. W e will use this approach quite often when we get\nto building games using pygame later .', 'Interface vs. Implementation\nOur earlier Account  class seems to have methods and instance variables that work\nwell. When you’re confident your code is working well, you no longer have to be\nconcerned with the details within the class. When a class does what you want it to\ndo, all you need to remember is what methods are available in the class. There are\ntwo dif ferent ways to look at a class: by focusing on what it is capable of doing\n(the interface ) and how it works internally (the implementation ).\ninterface\nThe collection of methods a class provides (and the parameters\nthat each method expects). The interface shows what  an object\ncreated from the class can do.\nimplementation\nThe actual code of the class, which shows how an object does\nwhat it does.\nIf you are the creator or maintainer of a class, you need to fully understand the\nimplementation—the code of all of the methods and how they work together to\naffect the instance variables. If you are purely writing code to use a class, you\nonly need to concern yourself with the interface—the dif ferent methods that are\navailable in the class, the values that need to be passed into each, and any value(s)\nthat are returned from the methods. If you are coding on your own (as a “one-\nperson team”), then you will be both the implementer of a class and the user of its\ninterface.\nAs long as the interface of a class does not change, the class’ s implementation\ncan change at any time. That is, if you find that a method can be implemented in a\nfaster or more ef ficient way , changing the relevant code inside the class will not\nhave any bad side ef fects on any other part of the program.\nSummary\nAn object manager object is an object that manages other objects. It does this by\nhaving one or more instance variables that are lists or dictionaries made up of', 'other objects. The object manager can call methods of any specific object or of all\nmanaged objects. This technique gives full control of all managed objects to the\nobject manager alone.\nWhen you encounter an error in a method or function, you can raise an\nexception. The raise  statement returns control to the caller . The caller can detect\na potential error by placing the call in a try block, and it can react to any such\nerror using an except  block.\nThe interface of a class is the documentation of all the methods and related\nparameters in the class. The implementation is the actual code of the class. What\nyou need to know depends on your role. The writer/maintainer of a class needs to\nunderstand the details of the code, whereas anyone who uses the class only needs\nto understand the interface that the class provides.', 'PART II\nGRAPHICAL USER INTERFACES\nWITH PYGA ME\nThese chapters introduce you to pygame, an external\npackage that adds functionality common to GUI\nprograms. I’ll provide several examples.', '5\nINTRODUCTION TO PYGAME\nThe Python language was designed to\nhandle text input and text output. It\nprovides the ability to get text from and\nsend text to the user, a file, and the\ninternet. The core language, however,\nhas no way of dealing with more modern concepts\nsuch as windows, mouse clicks, sounds, and so on.\nSo, what if you want to use Python to create\nsomething more state-of-the-art than a text-based\nprogram? First, I’ll explain how individual pixels are addressed in programs\nthat use a GUI. Then, I’ll discuss event-driven programs and how they\ndiffer from typical text-based programs. After that, we’ll code a few\nprograms that demonstrate key pygame features.\nWindow Details\nA computer screen is made up of a lar ge number of rows and columns of\nsmall dots called pixels  (from the words pictur e element ). A user interacts\nwith a GUI program through one or more windows; each window is a\nrectangular portion of the screen. Programs can control the color of any\nindividual pixel in their window(s). If you’re running multiple GUI\nprograms, each program is typically displayed in its own window . In this', 'section, I’ll discuss how you address and alter individual pixels in a\nwindow . These concepts are independent of Python; they are common to all\ncomputers and are used in all programming languages.\nThe W indow Coordinate System\nYou are probably familiar with Cartesian coordinates in a grid like Figur e\n5-1.\nFigure 5-1 : The standard Cartesian coordinate system', 'Any point in a Cartesian grid can be located by specifying its x- and y-\ncoordinates (in that order). The origin is the point specified as (0, 0) and is\nfound in the center of the grid.\nComputer window coordinates work in a similar way ( Figur e 5-2 ).\nFigure 5-2 : A computer window’ s coordinate system\nHowever , there are a few key dif ferences:\n. The origin (0, 0) point is in the upper -left corner of the window .\n. The y-axis is reversed so that y values start at zero at the top of the window\nand increase as you go down.\n. The x and y values are always integers. Each (x, y) pair specifies a single\npixel in the window . These values are always specified as relative to the\nupper -left corner of the window , not the screen. That way , the user can\nmove the window anywhere on the screen without af fecting the coordinates\nof the elements of the program displayed in the window .\nThe full computer screen has its own set of (x, y) coordinates for every\npixel and uses the same type of coordinate system, but programs rarely , if\never, need to deal with screen coordinates.', 'When we write a pygame application, we need to specify the width and\nheight of the window we want to create. W ithin the window , we can address\nany pixel using its x- and y-coordinates, as shown in Figur e 5-3 .\nFigur e 5-3  shows a black pixel at position (3, 5). That is an x-value of 3\n(note that this is actually the fourth column, since coordinates start at 0) and\na y value of 5 (actually the sixth row). Each pixel in a window is commonly\nreferred to as a point . To reference a point in a window , you would typically\nuse a Python tuple. For example, you might have an assignment statement\nlike this, with the x value first:\npixelLocation = (3, 5)', 'Figure 5-3 : A single point (a single pixel) in a computer window\nTo show an image in a window , we need to specify the coordinates of its\nstarting point—always the upper -left corner of the image—as an (x, y) pair ,\nas in Figur e 5-4 , where we draw the image at location (3, 5).', 'When working with an image, you’ll often need to deal with the\nbounding r ectangle , which is the smallest rectangle that can be made that\ncompletely surrounds all pixels of the image. A rectangle is represented in\npygame by a set of four values: x, y , width, height. The rectangle for the\nimage in Figur e 5-4  has values of 3, 5, 1 1, 7. I’ll show you how to use a\nrectangle like this in an upcoming example program. Even if your image is\nnot rectangular (for example, if it’ s a circle or an ellipse), you still have to\nconsider its bounding rectangle for positioning and collision detection.', 'Figure 5-4 : An image in a window\nPixel Colors\nLet’s explore how colors are represented on the computer screen. If you\nhave experience with a graphics program like Photoshop, you probably\nalready know how this works, but you may want a quick refresher anyway .', 'Each pixel on the screen is made up of a combination of three colors: red,\ngreen, and blue, often referred to as RGB . The color displayed in any pixel\nis composed of some amount of red, green, and blue, where the amount of\neach is specified as a value from 0, meaning none, to 255, meaning full\nintensity . Therefore, there are 256 × 256 × 256 possible combinations, or\n16,777,216 (often referred to as just “16 million”) possible colors, for each\npixel.\nColors in pygame are given as RGB values, and we write them as Python\ntuples of three numbers. Here is how we create constants for the main\ncolors:\nRED = (255, 0, 0)   # full red, no green, no blue  \nGREEN = (0, 255, 0)  # no red, full green, no blue  \nBLUE = (0, 0, 255)  # no red, no green, full blue\nHere are the definitions of a few more colors. Y ou can create a color\nusing any combination of three numbers between 0 and 255:\nBLACK = (0, 0, 0)    # no red, no green, no blue  \nWHITE = (255, 255, 255)  # full red, full green, full blue  \nDARK_GRAY = (75, 75, 75)  \nMEDIUM_GRAY = (128, 128, 128)  \nLIGHT_GRAY = (175, 175, 175)  \nTEAL = (0, 128, 128)  # no red, half-strength green, half-\nstrength blue  \nYELLOW = (255, 255, 0)  \nPURPLE = (128, 0, 128)\nIn pygame, you’ll need to specify colors when you want to fill the\nbackground of a window , draw a shape in a color , draw text in a color , and\nso on. Defining colors up front as tuple constants makes them very easy to\nspot later in code.\nEvent-Driven Programs\nIn most of the programs in the book so far , the main code has lived in a\nwhile  loop. The program stops at a call to the built-in input()  function and\nwaits for some user input to work on. Program output is typically handled\nusing calls to print() .', 'In interactive GUI programs, this model no longer works. GUIs introduce\na new model of computing known as the event-driven  model. Event-driven\nprograms don’ t rely on input()  and print() ; instead, the user interacts\nwith elements in a window at will using a keyboard and/or mouse or other\npointing device. They may be able to click various buttons or icons, make\nselections from menus, provide input in text fields, or give commands via\nclicks or key presses to control some avatar in the window .\nNOTE\nCalls to print()  can still be highly useful for debugging, when used\nto write out intermediate r esults.\nCentral to event-driven programming is the concept of an event . Events\nare dif ficult to define and are best described with examples, such as a\nmouse click and a key press (each of which is actually made up of two\nevents: mouse down and mouse up and key down and key up, respectively).\nHere is my working definition.\nevent\nSomething that happens while your program is running that\nyour program wants to or needs to respond to. Most events\nare generated by user actions.\nAn event-driven GUI program runs constantly in an infinite loop. Each\ntime through the loop, the program checks for any new events it needs to\nreact to and executes appropriate code to handle those events. Also, each\ntime through the loop, the program needs to redraw all the elements in the\nwindow to update what the user sees.\nFor example, say we have a simple GUI program that displays two\nbuttons: Bark and Meow . When clicked, the Bark button plays a sound of a\ndog barking and the Meow button plays a sound of a cat meowing ( Figur e\n5-5).', 'Figure 5-5 : A simple program with two buttons\nThe user can click these buttons in any order and at any time. T o handle\nthe user ’s actions, the program runs in a loop and constantly checks to see if\neither button has been clicked. When it receives a mouse down event on a\nbutton, the program remembers that the button has been clicked and draws\nthe depressed image of that button. When it receives a mouse up event on\nthe button, it remembers the new state and redraws the button with its\noriginal appearance, and it plays the appropriate sound. Because the main\nloop runs so quickly , the user perceives that the sound plays immediately\nafter they click the button. Each time through the loop, the program redraws\nboth buttons with an image matching each button’ s current state.\nUsing Pygame\nAt first, pygame may seem like an overwhelmingly lar ge package with\nmany dif ferent calls available. Although it is lar ge, there’ s actually not a lot\nthat you need to understand to get a small program up and running. T o\nintroduce pygame, I’ll first give you a template that you can use for all\npygame programs you create. Then I’ll build on that template, adding key\npieces of functionality little by little.\nIn the following sections, I’ll show you how to:\nBring up a blank window .\nShow an image.\nDetect a mouse click.\nDetect both single and continuous key presses.\nCreate a simple animation.\nPlay sound ef fects and background sounds.\nDraw shapes.', 'In the next chapter , we’ll continue the discussion of pygame and you’ll\nsee how to:\nAnimate many objects.\nBuild and react to a button.\nCreate a text display field.\nBringing Up a Blank W indow\nAs I said earlier , pygame programs run constantly in a loop, checking for\nevents. It might help to think of your program as an animation, where each\npass through the main loop is one frame. The user may click on something\nduring any frame, and your program must not only respond to that input but\nalso keep track of everything it needs to draw in the window . This number defines the\nmaximum number of times the program will loop (and therefore redraw the\nwindow) per second. Our value of 30 is fairly typical. If the amount of work\ndone in our main loop is excessive, the program might run slower than this\nvalue, but it will never run faster . Check for and handle events; commonly referred to as the event loop .\nIn this section, we call pygame.event.get()  to get a list of the events that\nhappened since the last time we checked (the last time the main loop ran),\nthen iterate through the list of events. Clear the window .\nOn each iteration through the main loop, our program must redraw\neverything in the window , which means we need to clear it first. W e could\nalso draw a background picture, but we’ll hold of f on that for now .\n. Draw all window elements.\nHere we’ll place code to draw everything we want to show in our window .\nIn this sample program there is nothing to draw .\nIn real programs, things are drawn in the order they appear in the code, in\nlayers from backmost to frontmost. For example, assume we want to draw\ntwo partially overlapping circles, A and B. If we draw A first, A will appear\nbehind B, and portions of A will be obscured by B. If we draw B first and\nthen A, the opposite happens, and we see A in front of B. Slow things down a bit.', 'Computers are very fast, and if the loop continued to the next iteration right\naway without pausing, the program might run faster than the designated\nframe rate. When they stop pressing the key ,\nthe movement stops.\nThe other change is that this approach allows you to check for multiple\nkeys being down at the same time. For example, if the user presses and\nholds the left and down arrow keys, the ball will move diagonally down and\nto the left. Y ou can check for as many keys being held down as you wish.\nHowever , the number of simultaneous  key presses that can be detected is\nlimited by the operating system, the keyboard hardware, and many other\nfactors. The typical limit is around four keys, but your mileage may vary .\nCreating a Location-Based Animation\nNext, we’ll build a location-based animation. This code will allow us to\nmove an image diagonally and then have it appear to bounce of f the edges\nof the window . This was a favorite technique of screensavers on old CR T-\nbased monitors, to avoid burning in a static image.\nWe’ll change the location of our image slightly in every frame. W e’ll also\ncheck if the result of that movement would place any part of the image\noutside one of the window boundaries and, if so, reverse the movement in\nthat direction. For example, if the image was moving down and would cross\nthe bottom of the window , we would reverse the direction and make the\nimage start moving up.\nWe’ll again use the same starting template. Listing 5-6  gives the full\nsource code.\nFile:\nPygameDemo4_OneBallBounce/PygameOneBallBounceXY .py\n# pygame demo 4(a) - one image, bounce around the window  \nusing (x, y)  coords \n ', '# 1 - Import packages  \nimport pygame  \nfrom pygame.locals import *  \nimport sys  \nimport random  \n \n# 2 - Define constants  \nBLACK = (0, 0, 0)  \nWINDOW_WIDTH = 640  \nWINDOW_HEIGHT = 480  \nFRAMES_PER_SECOND = 30  \nBALL_WIDTH_HEIGHT = 100  \nN_PIXELS_PER_FRAME = 3  \n \n# 3 - Initialize the world  \npygame.init()  \nwindow = pygame.display.set_mode((WINDOW_WIDTH,  \nWINDOW_HEIGHT))  \nclock = pygame.time.Clock()  \n \n# 4 - Load assets: image(s), sound(s), etc.  \nballImage = pygame.image.load(\'images/ball.png\')  \n \n# 5 - Initialize variables\nMAX_WIDTH = WINDOW_WIDTH - BALL_WIDTH_HEIGHT  \nMAX_HEIGHT = WINDOW_HEIGHT - BALL_WIDTH_HEIGHT  \n1 ballX = random.randrange(MAX_WIDTH)  \nballY = random.randrange(MAX_HEIGHT)  \nxSpeed = N_PIXELS_PER_FRAME  \nySpeed = N_PIXELS_PER_FRAME  \n  \n# 6 - Loop forever  \nwhile True:  \n \n    # 7 - Check for and handle events  \n    for event in pygame.event.get():  \n        # Clicked the close button? Quit pygame and end the  \nprogram \n        if event.type == pygame.QUIT:  \n            pygame.quit()  \n            sys.exit()  \n     \n    # 8 - Do any "per frame" actions  \n  2 if (ballX < 0) or (ballX >= MAX_WIDTH):  \n        xSpeed = -xSpeed  # reverse X direction  \n \n    if (ballY < 0) or (ballY >= MAX_HEIGHT):  ', "        ySpeed = -ySpeed  # reverse Y direction  \n \n    # Update the ball's location, using the speed in two  \ndirections  \n  3 ballX = ballX + xSpeed  \n    ballY = ballY + ySpeed  \n \n    # 9 - Clear the window before drawing it again  \n    window.fill(BLACK)  \n     \n    # 10 - Draw the window elements  \n    window.blit(ballImage, (ballX, ballY))  \n \n    # 11 - Update the window  \n    pygame.display.update()  \n \n    # 12 - Slow things down a bit  \n    clock.tick(FRAMES_PER_SECOND)   \nListing 5-6 : A location-based animation, bouncing a ball ar ound the\nwindow\nWe start by creating and initializing the two variables xSpeed  and ySpeed\n1, which determine how far and in what direction the image should move in\neach frame. W e initialize both variables to the number of pixels to move per\nframe (3), so the image will start by moving three pixels to the right (the\npositive x direction) and three pixels down (the positive y direction).\nIn the key part of the program, we handle the x- and y-coordinates\nseparately 2. First, we check to see if the x-coordinate of the ball is less than\nzero, meaning that part of the image is of f the left edge, or past the\nMAX_WIDTH  pixel and so ef fectively of f the right edge. If either of these is the\ncase, we reverse the sign of the speed in the x direction, meaning it will go\nin the opposite direction. For example, if the ball was moving to the right\nand went of f the right edge, we would change the value of xSpeed  from 3 to\n–3 to cause the ball to start moving to the left, and vice versa.\nThen we do a similar check for the y-coordinate to make the ball bounce\noff the top or bottom edge, as needed.\nFinally , we update the position of the ball by adding the xSpeed  to the\nballX  coordinate and adding the ySpeed  to the ballY  coordinate 3. This", 'positions the ball at a new location on both axes.\nAt the bottom of the main loop, we draw the ball. Since we’re updating\nthe values of ballX  and ballY  in every frame, the ball appears to animate\nsmoothly . Try it out. Whenever the ball reaches any edge, it seems to\nbounce of f.\nUsing Pygame rects\nNext I’ll present a dif ferent way to achieve the same result. Rather than\nkeeping track of the current x- and y-coordinates of the ball in separate\nvariables, we’ll use the rect  of the ball, update the rect  every frame, and\ncheck if performing the update would cause any part of the rect  to move\noutside an edge of the window . This results in fewer variables, and because\nwe’ll start by making a call to get the rect  of an image, it will work with\nimages of any size.\nWhen you create a rect  object, in addition to remembering the left ,\ntop, width , and height  as attributes of the rectangle, that object also\ncalculates and maintains a number of other attributes for you. Y ou can\naccess any of these attributes directly by name using dot syntax , as shown\nin Table 5-1 . (I’ll provide more detail on this in Chapter 8 .)', 'Table 5-1 : Direct Access to Attributes of a rect\nAttribute Description\n<rect>.x The x-coordinate of the left edge of the rect\n<rect>.y The y-coordinate of the top edge of the rect\n<rect>.left The x-coordinate of the left edge of the rect (same as <rect>.x)\n<rect>.top The y-coordinate of the top edge of the rect (same as <rect>.y)\n<rect>.right The x-coordinate of the right edge of the rect\n<rect>.bottom The y-coordinate of the bottom edge of the rect\n<rect>.topleft A two-integer tuple: the coordinates of the upper-left corner of the rect\n<rect>.bottomle\nftA two-integer tuple: the coordinates of the lower-left corner of the rect\n<rect>.topright A two-integer tuple: the coordinates of the upper-right corner of the rect\n<rect>.bottomri\nghtA two-integer tuple: the coordinates of the lower-right corner of the rect\n<rect>.midtop A two-integer tuple: the coordinates of the midpoint of the top edge of  \nthe rect\n<rect>.midleft A two-integer tuple: the coordinates of the midpoint of the left edge of  \nthe rect\n<rect>.midbotto\nmA two-integer tuple: the coordinates of the midpoint of the bottom edge  \nof the rect\n<rect>.midright A two-integer tuple: the coordinates of the midpoint of the right edge of  \nthe rect\n<rect>.center A two-integer tuple: the coordinates at the center of the rect\n<rect>.centerx The x-coordinate of the center of the width of the rect\n<rect>.centery The y-coordinate of the center of the height of the rect\n<rect>.size A two-integer tuple: the (width, height) of the rect\n<rect>.width The width of the rect\n<rect>.height The height of the rect\n<rect>.w The width of the rect (same as <rect>.width )\n<rect>.h The height of the rect (same as <rect>.height )\nA pygame rect  also can be thought of, and accessed as, a list of four\nelements. Specifically , you can use an index to get or set any individual part\nof a rect . For instance, using the ballRect , the individual elements can be\naccessed as:\nballRect[0]  is the x value (but you could also use ballRect.left )', "ballRect[1]  is the y value (but you could also use ballRect.top )\nballRect[2]  is the width (but you could also use ballRect.width)\nballRect[3]  is the height (but you could also use ballRect.height )\nListing 5-7  is an alternative version of our bouncing ball program that\nmaintains all the information about the ball in a rectangle object.\nFile:\nPygameDemo4_OneBallBounce/PygameOneBallBounceRects.p\ny\n# pygame demo 4(b) - one image, bounce around the window  \nusing rects  \n \n# 1 - Import packages  \nimport pygame  \nfrom pygame.locals import *  \nimport sys  \nimport random  \n \n# 2 - Define constants  \nBLACK = (0, 0, 0)  \nWINDOW_WIDTH = 640  \nWINDOW_HEIGHT = 480  \nFRAMES_PER_SECOND = 30  \nN_PIXELS_PER_FRAME = 3  \n \n# 3 - Initialize the world  \npygame.init()  \nwindow = pygame.display.set_mode((WINDOW_WIDTH,  \nWINDOW_HEIGHT))  \nclock = pygame.time.Clock()  \n  \n# 4 - Load assets: image(s), sound(s), etc.  \nballImage = pygame.image.load('images/ball.png')  \n \n# 5 - Initialize variables\n1 ballRect = ballImage.get_rect()  \nMAX_WIDTH = WINDOW_WIDTH - ballRect.width  \nMAX_HEIGHT = WINDOW_HEIGHT - ballRect.height  \nballRect.left = random.randrange(MAX_WIDTH)  \nballRect.top = random.randrange(MAX_HEIGHT)  \nxSpeed = N_PIXELS_PER_FRAME  \nySpeed = N_PIXELS_PER_FRAME  ", ' \n# 6 - Loop forever  \nwhile True:  \n \n    # 7 - Check for and handle events  \n    for event in pygame.event.get():  \n        # Clicked the close button? The resulting program works exactly the same as\nthe original. The important lesson here is how you can use and manipulate\nattributes of a rect  object.', 'After loading the image of the ball, we call the get_rect()  method 1 to\nget the bounding rectangle of the image. The standard behavior of a button consists of the user using\ntheir mouse to click down on the button image and then releasing it.\nButtons typically consist of at least two images: one to represent the up\nor normal state of the button and another to represent the down  or pressed', 'state of the button. The sequence of a click can be broken down into the\nfollowing steps:\n. User moves the mouse pointer over the button\n. User presses the mouse button down\n. Program reacts by changing the image to the down state\n. User releases the mouse button\n. Program reacts by showing the up image of the button\n. Program performs some action based on the button click\nGood GUIs also allow the user to click down on a button, temporarily\nroll of f the button, changing the button to the up state, and then, with the\nmouse button still down, roll back over the image so the button changes\nback to the down image. If the user clicks down on a button but then rolls\nthe mouse of f and lifts up on the mouse button, that is not considered a\nclick. This means the program takes action only when the user presses\ndown and releases while the mouse is positioned over the image of a button.\nBuilding a Button Class\nThe button behavior should be common and consistent for all buttons used\nin a GUI, so we’ll build a class that takes care of the behavior details. Once\nwe’ve built a simple button class, we can instantiate any number of buttons\nand they’ll all work exactly the same way .\nLet’s consider what behaviors our button class must support. W e’ll need\nmethods to:\nLoad the images of the up and down states, then initialize any instance\nvariables needed to track the button’ s state.\nTell the button about all events that the main program has detected and\ncheck whether there are any that the button needs to react to.\nDraw the current image representing the button.\nListing 6-4  presents the code of a SimpleButton  class. (W e’ll build a\nmore complicated button class in Chapter 7 .) This class has three methods,', '__init__() , handleEvent() , and draw() , that implement the behaviors\nmentioned. The code of the handleEvent()  method does get a little tricky ,\nbut once you have it working, it’ s incredibly easy to use. Feel free to work\nyour way through it, but know that the implementation of the code is not\nthat relevant. The important thing here is to understand the purpose and\nusage of the dif ferent methods.\nFile: PygameDemo7_SimpleButton/SimpleButton.py\n# SimpleButton class\n#\n# Uses a "state machine" approach\n# \n \nimport pygame  \nfrom pygame.locals import *  \n \nclass SimpleButton():  \n    # Used to track the state of the button  \n    STATE_IDLE = \'idle\' # button is up, mouse not over button  \n    STATE_ARMED = \'armed\' # button is down, mouse over button  \n    STATE_DISARMED = \'disarmed\' # clicked down on button,  \nrolled off  \n         \n    def __init__(self, window, loc, up, down): 1  \n        self.window = window  \n        self.loc = loc  \n        self.surfaceUp = pygame.image.load(up)  \n        self.surfaceDown = pygame.image.load(down)  \n \n        # Get the rect of the button (used to see if the  \nmouse is over the button)  \n        self.rect = self.surfaceUp.get_rect()  \n        self.rect[0] = loc[0]  \n        self.rect[1] = loc[1]  \n \n        self.state = SimpleButton.STATE_IDLE  \n \n    def handleEvent(self, eventObj): 2  \n        # This method will return True if user clicks the  \nbutton.\n        # Normally returns False.  \n \n        if eventObj.type not in (MOUSEMOTION, MOUSEBUTTONUP,  ', "MOUSEBUTTONDOWN): 3  \n            # The button only cares about mouse-related  \nevents \n            return False  \n \n        eventPointInButtonRect =  \nself.rect.collidepoint(eventObj.pos)  \n \n        if self.state == SimpleButton.STATE_IDLE:  \n            if (eventObj.type == MOUSEBUTTONDOWN) and  \neventPointInButtonRect:  \n                self.state = SimpleButton.STATE_ARMED  \n \n        elif self.state == SimpleButton.STATE_ARMED:  \n            if (eventObj.type == MOUSEBUTTONUP) and  \neventPointInButtonRect:  \n                self.state = SimpleButton.STATE_IDLE  \n                return True  # clicked!  \n \n            if (eventObj.type == MOUSEMOTION) and (not  \neventPointInButtonRect):  \n                self.state = SimpleButton.STATE_DISARMED  \n \n        elif self.state == SimpleButton.STATE_DISARMED:  \n            if eventPointInButtonRect:  \n                self.state = SimpleButton.STATE_ARMED  \n            elif eventObj.type == MOUSEBUTTONUP:  \n                self.state = SimpleButton.STATE_IDLE  \n \n        return False  \n \n    def draw(self): 4  \n        # Draw the button's current appearance to the window  \n        if self.state == SimpleButton.STATE_ARMED:  \n            self.window.blit(self.surfaceDown, self.loc)  \n \n        else:  # IDLE or DISARMED  \n            self.window.blit(self.surfaceUp, self.loc)\nListing 6-4 : The SimpleButton  class\nThe __init__()  method begins by saving all values passed in into\ninstance variables 1 to use in other methods. It then initializes a few more\ninstance variables.", "Whenever the main program detects any event, it calls the\nhandleEvent()  method 2. This method first checks that the event is one of\nMOUSEMOTION , MOUSEBUTTONUP , or MOUSEBUTTONDOWN  3. The rest of the\nmethod is implemented as a state machine , a technique that I will go into\nmore detail about in Chapter 15 . The code is a little complicated, and you\nshould feel free to study how it works, but for now note that it uses the\ninstance variable self.state  (over the course of multiple calls) to detect if\nthe user has clicked on the button. The handleEvent()  method returns True\nwhen the user completes a mouse click by pressing down on the button,\nthen later releasing on the same button. In all other cases, handleEvent()\nreturns False .\nFinally , the draw()  method uses the state of the object’ s instance variable\nself.state  to decide which image (up or down) to draw 4.\nMain Code Using a SimpleButton\nTo use a SimpleButton  in the main code, we first instantiate one from the\nSimpleButton  class before the main loop starts with a line like this:\noButton = SimpleButton(window, (150, 30),  \n                       'images/buttonUp.png',  \n                       'images/buttonDown.png')\nThis line creates a SimpleButton  object, specifying a location to draw it\n(as usual, the coordinates are for the top-left corner of the bounding\nrectangle) and providing the paths to both the up and down images of the\nbutton. In the main loop, any time any event happens we need to call the\nhandleEvent()  method to see if the user has clicked the button. If the user\nclicks the button, the program should perform some action. Also in the\nmain loop, we need to call the draw()  method to make the button show in\nthe window .\nWe’ll build a small test program, which will generate a user interface like\nFigur e 6-2 , to incorporate one instance of a SimpleButton .", 'Figure 6-2 : The user interface of a program with a single instance of a SimpleButton\nWhenever the user completes a click on the button, the program outputs a\nline of text in the shell saying that the button has been clicked. Listing 6-5\ncontains the main program code.\nFile: PygameDemo7_SimpleButton/Main_SimpleButton.py\n# Pygame demo 7 - SimpleButton test  \n \n--- snip ---\n# 5 - Initialize variables\n# Create an instance of a SimpleButton\n1 oButton = SimpleButton(window, (150, 30),  \n                       \'images/buttonUp.png\',  \n                       \'images/buttonDown.png\')  \n \n# 6 - Loop forever  \nwhile True:  \n \n    # 7 - Check for and handle events  \n    for event in pygame.event.get():  \n        if event.type == pygame.QUIT:  \n            pygame.quit()  \n            sys.exit()  \n \n        # Pass the event to the button, see if it has been  \nclicked on  \n      2 if oButton.handleEvent(event):  \n          3 print(\'User has clicked the button\')  \n \n    # 8 - Do any "per frame" actions\n    \n    # 9 - Clear the window  \n    window.fill(GRAY)  \n     \n    # 10 - Draw all window elements  \n  4 oButton.draw() # draw the button  ', ' \n    # 11 - Update the window  \n    pygame.display.update()  \n \n    # 12 - Slow things down a bit  \n    clock.tick(FRAMES_PER_SECOND)   \nListing 6-5 : The main pr ogram that cr eates and r eacts to a SimpleButton\nAgain, we start with the standard pygame template from Chapter 5 .\nBefore the main loop, we create an instance of our SimpleButton 1,\nspecifying a window to draw into, a location, a path to the up image, and a\npath to the down image.\nEvery time through the main loop, we need to react to events detected in\nthe main program. W e simply modify our main code to instantiate three SimpleButton\nobjects instead of one.\nFile:\nPygameDemo7_SimpleButton/Main_SimpleButton3Buttons.py\noButtonA = SimpleButton(window, (25, 30),  \n                        'images/buttonAUp.png',  \n                        'images/buttonADown.png')  \noButtonB = SimpleButton(window, (150, 30),  \n                        'images/buttonBUp.png',  \n                        'images/buttonBDown.png')  \noButtonC = SimpleButton(window, (275, 30),  \n                        'images/buttonCUp.png',  \n                        'images/buttonCDown.png')\nWe now need to call the handleEvent()  method of all three buttons:\n        # Pass the event to each button, see if one has been  \nclicked \n        if oButtonA.handleEvent(event):  \n            print('User clicked button A.')  \n        elif oButtonB.handleEvent(event):  \n            print('User clicked button B.')  \n        elif oButtonC.handleEvent(event):  \n            print('User clicked button C.')\nFinally , we tell each button to draw itself:\n    oButtonA.draw()  \n    oButtonB.draw()  \n    oButtonC.draw()\nWhen you run the program, you’ll see a window with three buttons.\nClicking any of the buttons prints a message showing the name of the\nbutton that was clicked.\nThe key idea here is that since we are using three instances of the same\nSimpleButton  class, the behavior of each button will be identical. An\nimportant benefit of this approach is that any change to the code in the\nSimpleButton  class will af fect all buttons instantiated from the class. The", "main program does not need to worry about any details of the inner\nworkings of the button code, needing only to call the handleEvent()\nmethod of each button in the main loop. Each button will return True  or\nFalse  to say that it has or has not been clicked.\nBuilding a Reusable Object-Oriented Text\nDisplay\nThere are two dif ferent types of text in a pygame program: display text and\ninput text. Display text is output from your program, equivalent to a call to\nthe print()  function, except it’ s displayed in a pygame window . Input text\nis string input from the user , equivalent to a call to input() . In this section,\nI’ll discuss display text. W e’ll look at how to deal with input text in the next\nchapter .\nSteps to Display T ext\nDisplaying text in a window is a fairly complicated process in pygame\nbecause it’ s not simply displayed as a string in the shell, but requires you to\nchoose a location, fonts and sizes, and other attributes. For example, you\nmight use code like the following:\npygame.font.init()  \n \nmyFont = pygame.font.SysFont('Comic Sans MS', 30)  \ntextSurface = myfont.render('Some text', True, (0, 0, 0))  \nwindow.blit(textSurface, (10, 10))\nWe start by initializing the font system within pygame; we do this before\nthe main loop starts. Then we tell pygame to load a particular font from the\nsystem by name. Here, we request Comic Sans with a font size of 30.\nThe next step is the key one: we use that font to render  our text, which\ncreates a graphical image of the text, called a surface  in pygame. W e supply\nthe text we want to output, a Boolean that says whether we want our text to\nbe anti-aliased, and a color in RGB format. Here, (0, 0, 0)  indicates that\nwe want our text to be black. Finally , using blit() , we draw the image of\nthe text into the window at some (x, y) location.", 'This code works well to show the provided text in the window at the\ngiven location. However , if the text doesn’ t change, there will be a lot of\nwasted work done re-creating the textSurface  on each iteration through\nthe main loop. There are also a lot of details to remember , and you must get\nthem all correct to draw the text properly . We can hide most of this\ncomplexity by building a class.\nCreating a SimpleT ext Class\nThe idea is to build a set of methods that take care of font loading and text\nrendering in pygame, meaning we no longer have to remember the details\nof the implementation. Listing 6-6  contains a new class called SimpleText\nthat does this work.\nFile: PygameDemo8_SimpleT extDisplay/SimpleT ext.py\n# SimpleText class  \n \nimport pygame  \nfrom pygame.locals import *  \n \nclass SimpleText():  \n     \n  1 def __init__(self, window, loc, value, textColor):  \n      2 pygame.font.init()  \n        self.window = window  \n        self.loc = loc  \n      3 self.font = pygame.font.SysFont(None, 30)  \n        self.textColor = textColor  \n        self.text = None # so that the call to setText below  \nwill \n                         # force the creation of the text  \nimage \n        self.setValue(value) # set the initial text for  \ndrawing \n \n  4 def setValue(self, newText):   \n        if self.text == newText:  \n            return  # nothing to change  \n \n        self.text = newText  # save the new text  \n        self.textSurface = self.font.render(self.text, True,  ', 'self.textColor)  \n \n  5 def draw(self):  \n        self.window.blit(self.textSurface, self.loc)\nListing 6-6 : The SimpleText  class for displaying text\nYou can think of a SimpleText  object as a field in the window where you\nwant text to be displayed. Y ou can use one to display unchanging label text\nor to display text that changes throughout a program.\nThe SimpleText  class has only three methods. The __init__()  method 1\nexpects the window to draw into, the location at which to draw the text in\nthe window , any initial text you want to see displayed in the field, and a text\ncolor . Calling pygame.font.init()  2 starts up pygame’ s font system. The\ncall in the first instantiated SimpleText  object actually does the\ninitialization; any additional SimpleText  objects will also make this call,\nbut since fonts have already been initialized, the call returns immediately .\nWe create a new Font  object with pygame.font.SysFont()  3. Rather than\nproviding a specific font name, None  indicates that we will use whatever the\nstandard system font is.\nThe setValue()  method renders an image of the text to display and saves\nthat image in the self.textSurface  instance variable 4. As the program\nruns, any time you want to change the text that’ s displayed, you call the\nsetValue()  method, passing in the new text to display . The setValue()\nmethod has an optimization, too: it remembers the last text that it rendered,\nand before doing anything else, it checks if the new text is the same as the\nprevious text. If the text has not changed, there is nothing to do and the\nmethod just returns. If there is new text, it renders the new text into a\nsurface to be drawn.\nThe draw()  method 5 draws the image contained in the\nself.textSurface  instance variable into the window at the given location.\nThis method should be called in every frame.\nThere are multiple advantages to this approach:\nThe class hides all the details of pygame’ s rendering of text, so the user of\nthis class never needs to know what pygame-specific calls are needed to', 'show text.\nEach SimpleText  object remembers the window that it draws into, the\nlocation where the text should be placed, and the text color . Therefore, you\nonly need to specify these values once, when you instantiate a SimpleText\nobject, typically before the main loop starts.\nEach SimpleText  object is also optimized to remember both the text that it\nwas last told to draw and the image ( self.textSurface ) that it made from\nthe current text. It only needs to render a new surface when the text\nchanges.\nTo show multiple pieces of text in a window , you only need to instantiate\nmultiple SimpleText  objects. This is a key concept of object-oriented\nprogramming.\nDemo Ball with SimpleText and SimpleButton\nTo cap this of f, we’ll modify Listing 6-2  to use the SimpleText  and\nSimpleButton  classes. W e’ll build a more full-featured text\nobject that has reasonable default values for each of these attributes, but\nallows you to override those defaults.\nInterface vs. Implementation', "The SimpleButton  and SimpleText  examples bring up the important topic\nof interface versus implementation. As mentioned in Chapter 4 , the\ninterface refers to how something is used, while the implementation refers\nto how something works (internally).\nIn an OOP environment, the interface is the set of methods in a class and\ntheir related parameters—also known as the application pr ogramming\ninterface (API) . The implementation is the actual code of all the methods in\nthe class.\nAn external package such as pygame will most likely come with\ndocumentation of the API that explains the calls that are available and the\narguments you are expected to pass with each call. The full pygame API\ndocumentation is available at https://www .pygame.or g/docs/ .\nWhen you write code that makes calls to pygame, you don’ t need to\nworry about the implementation of the methods you are using. For example,\nwhen you make a call to blit()  to draw image, you really don’ t care how\nblit()  does what it does; you just need to know what  the call does and\nwhat ar guments need to be passed in. On the other side, you can trust that\nthe implementer(s) who wrote the blit()  method have thought extensively\nabout how to make blit()  work most ef ficiently .\nIn the programming world, we often wear two hats as both the\nimplementer and the application developer , so we need to make an ef fort to\ndesign APIs that not only make sense in the current situation, but also are\ngeneral enough to be used by future programs of our own and by programs\nwritten by other people. Our SimpleButton  and SimpleText  classes are\ngood examples, as they are written in a general way so that they can be\nreused easily . I’ll talk more about interface versus implementation in\nChapter 8 , when we look at encapsulation.\nCallbacks\nWhen using a SimpleButton  object, we handle checking for and reacting to\na button click like this:\nif oButton.handleEvent(event):  \n    print('The button was clicked')", "This approach to handling events works well with the SimpleButton\nclass. However , some other Python packages and many other programming\nlanguages handle events in a dif ferent way: with a callback .\ncallback\nA function or method of an object that is called when a\nparticular action, event, or condition happens.\nAn easy way to understand this is to think about the 1984 hit movie\nGhostbusters . The tagline for the movie is “Who you gonna call?” In the\nmovie, the Ghostbusters ran an ad on TV that told people that if they saw a\nghost (that’ s the event to look for), they should call the Ghostbusters (the\ncallback) to get rid of it. Upon receiving the call, the Ghostbusters take the\nappropriate actions to eliminate the ghost.\nAs an example, consider a button object that is initialized to have a\ncallback. When the user clicks the button, the button will call the callback\nfunction or method. That function or method executes whatever code is\nneeded to react to the button click.\nCreating a Callback\nTo set up a callback, when you create an object or call one of an object’ s\nmethods, you pass the name of a function or a method of an object to be\ncalled. As an example, there is a standard GUI package for Python called\ntkinter . The code needed to create a button with this package is very\ndifferent from what I have shown—here’ s an example:\n    import tkinter  \n             \n    def myFunction():  \n        print('myCallBackFunction was called')  \n \n    oButton = tkinter.Button(text='Click me',  \ncommand=myFunction)  ", 'When you create a button with tkinter , you must pass in a function (or a\nmethod of an object), which will be called back when the user clicks the\nbutton. Here, we are passing myFunction  as the function to be called back.\n(This call is using keyword parameters, which will be discussed at length in\nChapter 7 .) The tkinter  button remembers that function as the callback,\nand when the user clicks the resulting button, it calls the function\nmyFunction() .\nYou can also use a callback when you initiate some action that may take\nsome time. Instead of waiting for the action to finish and causing the\nprogram appear to freeze for a period of time, you provide a callback to be\ncalled when the action is completed. For example, imagine that you want to\nmake a request across the internet. Rather than making a call and waiting\nfor that call to return data, which may take a long time, there are packages\nthat allow you to use the approach of making the call and setting a callback.\nThat way , the program can continue running, and the user is not locked out\nof it. This often involves multiple Python threads and is beyond the scope\nof this book, but the technique of using a callback is the general way that it\nis done.\nUsing a Callback with SimpleButton\nTo demonstrate this concept, we’ll make a minor modification to the\nSimpleButton  class to allow it to accept a callback. As an additional\noptional parameter , the caller can provide a function or method of an object\nto be called back when a click on a SimpleButton  object happens.
