We’ll do so with\npygwidgets, a library of GUI widgets.\nThis chapter explains how standard widgets such as images, buttons, and\ninput or output fields can be built as classes and how client code uses them.\nBuilding each element as a class allows programmers to incorporate\nmultiple instances of each element when creating a GUI. Before we get\nstarted building these GUI widgets, however , I first need to discuss one\nmore Python feature: passing data in a call to a function or method.\nPassing Arguments into a Function or\nMethod', 'The ar guments in a call to a function and the parameters defined in the\nfunction have a one-to-one relationship, so that the value of the first\nargument is given to the first parameter , the value of the second ar gument is\ngiven to the second parameter , and so on.\nFigur e 7-1 , duplicated from Chapter 3 , shows that the same is true when\nyou make a call to a method of an object. W e can see that the first\nparameter , which is always self , is set to the object in the call.\nFigure 7-1 : How arguments passed into a method match up with its parameters\nHowever , Python (and some other languages) allows you to make some\nof the ar guments optional. If an optional ar gument isn’ t provided in a call,\nwe can provide a default value to use in the function or method instead. I’ll\nexplain by means of a real-world analogy .\nIf you order a hambur ger at a Bur ger King restaurant, your bur ger will\ncome with ketchup, mustard, and pickles. But Bur ger King is famous for\nsaying, “Y ou can have it your way .” If you want some other combination of\ncondiments, you must say what you want (or don’ t want) when you make\nyour order .\nWe’ll start by writing an orderBurgers()  function that simulates making\na bur ger order in the regular way we’ve been defining functions, without\nimplementing default values:\ndef orderBurgers(nBurgers, ketchup, mustard, pickles):\nYou must specify the number of hambur gers you want to order , but\nideally , if you want the defaults of True  for adding ketchup, mustard, and\npickles, you shouldn’ t need to pass in any more ar guments. So, to order two\nhambur gers with the standard defaults, you might think your call should\nlook like this:', "orderBurgers(2)  # with ketchup, mustard, and pickles\nHowever , in Python, this will trigger an error because there is a mismatch\nbetween the number of ar guments in the call and the number of parameters\nspecified in the function:\nTypeError: orderBurgers() missing 3 required positional  \narguments: 'ketchup', 'mustard', and 'pickles'\nLet’s see how Python allows us to set up optional parameters that can be\ngiven default values if nothing is specified.\nPositional and Keyword Parameters\nPython has two dif ferent types of parameters: positional parameters and\nkeyword parameters. Positional parameters  are the type that we’re already\nfamiliar with, where each ar gument in a call has a matching parameter in\nthe function or method definition.\nA keywor d parameter allows you to specify a default value. Y ou write a\nkeyword parameter as a variable name, an equal sign, and a default value,\nlike this:\ndef someFunction( <keywordParameter> =<default value> ):\nYou can have multiple keyword parameters, each with a name and a\ndefault value.\nA function or method can have both positional parameters and keyword\nparameters, in which case you must specify all positional parameters befor e\nany keyword parameters:\ndef someOtherFunction(positionalParam1, positionalParam2, ...  \n          <keywordParameter1> =<default value 1> ,  \n          <keywordParameter2> =<default value 2> , ...):\nLet’s rewrite orderBurgers()  to use one positional parameter and three\nkeyword parameters with default values, like this:", 'def orderBurgers(nBurgers, ketchup=True, mustard=True,  \npickles=True):\nWhen we make a call to this function, nBurgers  is a positional parameter\nand therefore must be specified as an ar gument in every call. The other\nthree are keyword parameters. If no values are passed for ketchup ,\nmustard , and pickles , the function will use the default value of True  for\neach of those parameter variables. Now we can order two bur gers with all\nthe condiments like this:\norderBurgers(2)\nIf we want something other than a default value, we can specify the name\nof the keyword parameter and a dif ferent value in our call. For example, if\nwe only want ketchup on our two bur gers, we can make the call this way:\norderBurgers(2, mustard=False, pickles=False)\nWhen the function runs, the values of the mustard  and pickles  variables\nare set to False . Since we did not specify a value for ketchup , it is given\nthe default of True .\nYou can also make the call specifying all ar guments positionally ,\nincluding those written as keyword parameters. Python will use the ordering\nof your ar guments to assign each parameter the correct value:\norderBurgers(2, True, False, False)\nIn this call, we are again specifying two bur gers with ketchup, no\nmustard, and no pickles.\nAdditional Notes on Keyword Parameters\nLet’s quickly go over a few conventions and tips for using keyword\nparameters. As a Python convention, when you use keyword parameters\nand keywords with ar guments, the equal sign between the keyword and the\nvalue should not have spaces around it, to show that these are not typical\nassignment statements. These lines are properly formatted:', "def orderBurgers(nBurgers, ketchup=True, mustard=True,  \npickles=True):  \n \norderBurgers(2, mustard=False)\nThese lines will also work fine, but they don’ t follow the formatting\nconvention and are less readable:\ndef orderBurgers(nBurgers, ketchup = True, mustard = True,  \npickles = True):  \n \norderBurgers(2, mustard = False)\nWhen calling a function that has both positional parameters and keyword\nparameters, you must provide values for all the positional parameters first,\nbefore any optional keyword parameters.\nKeyword ar guments in calls can be specified in any order . Calls to our\norderBurgers()  function could be made in various ways, such as:\norderBurgers(2, mustard=False, pickles=False)  # only ketchup\nor:\norderBurgers(2, pickles=False, mustard=False, ketchup=False)   \n# plain\nAll keyword parameters will be given the appropriate values,\nindependent of the order of the ar guments.\nWhile all the default values in the orderBurgers()  example were\nBoolean values, a keyword parameter can have a default value of any data\ntype. For example, we could write a function to allow a customer to make\nan ice cream order like this:\ndef orderIceCream(flavor, nScoops=1, coneOrCup='cone',  \nsprinkles=False):\nThe caller must specify a flavor , but by default will get one scoop in a\ncone with no sprinkles. The caller could override these defaults with\ndifferent keyword values.", "Using None as a Default V alue\nIt’s sometimes helpful to know whether the caller passed in a value for a\nkeyword parameter or not. As a third\nparameter , the caller can optionally pass in a single desired topping. This may be a very subtle\nuse of keyword parameters, but it will be very useful in our upcoming\ndiscussion.\nChoosing Keywords and Default V alues\nUsing default values makes calling functions and methods simpler , but\nthere is a downside. Y our choice of each keyword for keyword parameters\nis very important. Once programmers start making calls that override\ndefault values, it’ s very dif ficult to change the name of a keyword\nparameter because that name must be changed in all calls to the function or\nmethod in lockstep. Otherwise, code that was working will break. For more\nwidely distributed code, this can potentially cause a great deal of pain to\nprogrammers using your code. Bottom line, don’ t change the name of a\nkeyword parameter unless it is absolutely necessary . So, choose wisely!\nIt’s also very important to use default values that should suit the widest\npossible range of users. (On a personal note, I hate mustard! Whenever I go\nto Bur ger King, I have to remember to specify no mustard or I’ll get what I\nconsider to be an inedible hambur ger. After importing, you can instantiate\nobjects from its classes and call the methods of those objects.\nThe most current documentation of pygwidgets  is at\nhttps://pygwidgets.r eadthedocs.io/en/latest/ . W e began by discussing default\nvalues for parameters in methods, and I explained that a keyword parameter\nallows for a default value to be used if no matching ar gument value is\nspecified in a call.\nI then introduced you to the pygwidgets  module, which contains a\nnumber of prebuilt GUI widget classes, and showed you how to use several\nof these. Second, you can reuse the code by creating as many instances\nof a class as you need. Y our classes can provide basic functionality by\nincluding keyword parameters with well-chosen default values. However ,\nthe default values can easily be overwritten to allow for customization.\nYou can publish the interfaces of your classes for other programmers\n(and yourself) to take advantage of in dif ferent projects. Good\ndocumentation and consistency go a long way toward making these types of\nclasses highly usable.', 'PART III\nENCAPSULATION, POLYMORPHISM,\nAND INHERITANCE\nThe three main tenets of object-oriented\nprogramming are encapsulation, polymorphism, and\ninheritance. The next three chapters will explain each\nof these in turn, describing the underlying concepts\nand showing examples of how they are implemented\nin Python. In order for a programming language to\ncall itself an OOP language, it must support all three\nof these central requirements. (If you’re ever asked in\nan interview about the requirements for a language to\nbe object-oriented, here’s an easy way to remember\nthem: it’s as easy as PIE!)\nChapter 8  explains encapsulation: hiding the details and keeping\neverything in one place.\nChapter 9  discusses polymorphism: how multiple classes can have\nmethods with the same names.\nChapter 10  covers inheritance: building on code that already exists.\nFinally , Chapter 1 1 goes into detail on a number of topics (mostly dealing\nwith memory management) that do not logically fit into the previous three\nchapters but are useful and important to OOP .', '8\nENCAPSULATION\nThe first of the three main tenets of\nobject-oriented programming is\nencapsulation. This word might conjure\nup an image of a space capsule, a cell\nwall, or a medicine gelcap, where the\nprecious cargo inside is protected from the outside\nenvironment. In programming, encapsulation has a\nsimilar but even more detailed meaning: hiding\ninternal details of state and behavior from any\nexternal code and having all code in one place.\nIn this chapter , we’ll see how encapsulation works with functions, then\nwith methods of objects. I’ll discuss dif ferent interpretations of\nencapsulation: using direct access versus using getters and setters. I’ll show\nhow Python allows you to mark an instance variable as private, indicating\nthat it should not be accessed by code external to a class, and I’ll touch on\nthe Python property decorator . Finally , I’ll discuss the concept of\nabstraction in the design of classes.\nEncapsulation with Functions', 'Functions are a prime example of encapsulation because when you call a\nfunction, you generally don’ t care how the function works internally . A\nwell-written function contains a series of steps that make up a lar ger single\ntask, which you do care about. The name of the function should describe the\naction that its code embodies. Consider the built-in len()  function from the\nPython Standard Library , used to find the number of characters in a string\nor elements in a list. Y ou pass in a string or list, and it returns the count.\nWhen you write code that calls this function, you don’ t care how len()\ndoes what it does. Y ou don’ t stop to think about whether the code of the\nfunction contains two lines or two thousand lines, whether it uses one local\nvariable or a hundred. Y ou just need to know what ar gument to pass in and\nhow to use the result that’ s returned.\nThe same is true of functions that you write, like this function that\ncalculates and returns the average of a list of numbers:\ndef calculateAverage(numbersList):  \n    total = 0.0  \n    for number in numbersList:  \n        total = total + number  \n    nElements = len(numbersList)  \n    average = total / nElements  \n    return average\nOnce you’ve tested a function like this and found that it works, you no\nlonger have to worry about the details of the implementation. Y ou only need\nto know what ar gument(s) to send into the function and what it returns.\nHowever , if one day you find that there is a much simpler or faster\nalgorithm to calculate an average, you could rewrite the function in a new\nway. As long as the interface (the inputs and outputs) does not change, there\nis no need to change any calls to the function. This type of modularization\nmakes the code more maintainable.\nEncapsulation with Objects\nUnlike variables used in regular functions, instance variables in objects\npersist across dif ferent method calls. T o keep the remaining discussion', 'clear , I’ll introduce a new term: client . (I don’ t want to use the term user\nhere since that typically refers to the human user of the final program.)\nclient\nAny software that creates an object from a class and makes\ncalls to the methods of that object.\nWe must also consider the duality of inside  versus outside  an object or\nclass. When you’re working inside a class (writing the code of the methods\nin a class), you need to concern yourself with how the dif ferent methods of\nthe class share the instance variables. Y ou consider the ef ficiency of your\nalgorithms. Y ou think about what the interface should look like: what\nmethods you should provide, what the parameters for each are, and what\nshould be used as default values. In short, you are concerned with the\ndesign and implementation of the methods.\nFrom the outside, as a client programmer , you need to know the interface\nof the class. Y ou’re concerned with what the class methods do, what\narguments should be passed in, and what data is passed back from each\nmethod.\nA class therefore provides encapsulation by:\nHiding all details of implementation in its methods and instance variables\nProviding all the functionality a client needs from an object through its\ninterface (the methods defined in the class)\nObjects Own Their Data\nIn object-oriented programming, we say that the data inside an object is\nowned  by the object. OOP programmers generally agree that, as a good\ndesign principle, client code should only be concerned with the interface of\na class and should not care about the implementation of the methods.\nConsider the example of a simple Person  class in Listing 8-1 .\nclass Person():  \n ', "    def __init__(self, name, salary):  \n        self.name = name  \n        self.salary = salary\nListing 8-1 : Data ownership in the Person  class\nThe values of the instance variables\nself.name  and self.salary  are set whenever we instantiate new Person\nobjects, like this:\noPerson1 = Person('Joe Schmoe', 90000)  \noPerson2 = Person('Jane Smith', 99000)\nEach Person  object owns its own set of the two instance variables.\nInterpretations of Encapsulation\nHere is where things get a little controversial. Dif ferent programmers have\ndifferent views about the accessibility of an instance variable. Python\nprovides for a loose interpretation of encapsulation by allowing direct\naccess to instance variables using simple dot syntax. Client code can legally\naccess an instance variable of an object by name using the syntax\n<object> .<instanceVariableName> .\nHowever , a strict  interpretation of encapsulation says that client software\nshould never be able to retrieve or change the value of an instance variable\ndirectly . Instead, the only way that a client can retrieve or change a value\nheld in an object is to use a method provided by the class for this purpose.\nLet’s look at both approaches.\nDirect Access and Why Y ou Should Avoid It\nAs mentioned, Python does allow direct access to instance variables. Listing\n8-2 instantiates the same two objects from the Person  class in Listing 8-1  as\nin the previous section, but then accesses their self.salary  instance\nvariables directly .", "File:\nPersonGettersSettersAndDirectAccess/Main_PersonDirectAcce\nss.py\n# Person example main program using direct access  \n \nfrom Person import *  \n \noPerson1 = Person('Joe Schmoe', 90000)  \noPerson2 = Person('Jane Smith', 99000)  \n \n# Get the values of the salary variable directly\n1 print(oPerson1.salary)  \nprint(oPerson2.salary)  \n \n# Change the salary variable directly\n2 oPerson1.salary = 100000  \noPerson2.salary = 111111  \n \n# Get the updated salaries and print again  \nprint(oPerson1.salary)  \nprint(oPerson2.salary)\nListing 8-2 : Example main code using dir ect access to an instance\nvariable\nPython allows you to write code like this that reaches into an object to\ndirectly get 1 and set 2 any instance variable using standard dot syntax.\nMost Python programmers feel that this technique is perfectly acceptable.\nIn fact, Guido van Rossum (the creator of Python) famously said in\nreference to this issue, “W e are all adults here,” meaning that programmers\nshould know what they are doing, and the risks involved, when they attempt\nto access instance variables directly .\nHowever , I strongly believe that directly accessing an instance variable of\nan object is an extremely dangerous practice, as it breaks the core idea of\nencapsulation. T o illustrate why this is the case, let’ s take a look at a few\nexample scenarios where direct access can be problematic.\nChanging the Name of an Instance V ariable", 'The first problem with direct access is that changing the name of an\ninstance variable will break any client code that uses the original name\ndirectly . This can happen when the developer of a class decides that the\ninitial choice of the name of the variable was not optimal, for reasons such\nas the following:\nThe name doesn’ t describe the data it represents clearly enough.\nThe variable is a Boolean, and they want to swap what True  and False\nrepresent by renaming the variable (for example, closed  to open , allowed\nto disallowed , active  to disabled ).\nThere was a spelling or capitalization mistake in the original name.\nThe variable was originally a Boolean, but they later realize that they need\nto represent more than two values.\nIn any of these cases, if the developer changes the name of an instance\nvariable in the class from self.<originalName>  to self.<newName> , then\nany client software that uses the original name directly will break.\nChanging an Instance V ariable into a Calculation\nA second situation where direct access is problematic is when the code of a\nclass needs to change to meet new requirements. Suppose that when writing\na class, you use an instance variable to represent a piece of data, but the\nfunctionality changes so that you need an algorithm to compute a value\ninstead. T ake our Account  class from Chapter 4 , for example. T o make our\nbank accounts more realistic, we might want to add an interest rate. Y ou\nmight think this is a simple matter of adding an instance variable for the\ninterest rate named self.interestRate . Then, using the direct access\napproach, client software could access this value of an Account  object\nusing:\noAccount.interestRate\nThis would work, for a while. But later the bank might decide on a new\npolicy—say that the interest rate will depend on the amount of money in the\naccount. The interest rate might be calculated like this:', 'def calculateInterestRate(self):  \n    # Assuming self.balance has been set in another method  \n    if self.balance < 1000:  \n        self.interestRate = 1.0  \n     elif self.balance < 5000:  \n        self.interestRate = 1.5  \n    else:  \n        self.interestRate = 2.0\nRather than just relying on a single interest rate value in\nself.interestRate , the calculateInterestRate()  method determines the\ncurrent rate based on the account balance.\nAny client software that directly accesses oAccount.interestRate  and\nuses the value of the instance variable might then get an outdated value,\ndepending on the last time calculateInterestRate()  was called. And any\nclient software that sets a new interestRate  may find that the new value is\nmysteriously changed by some other code that calls\ncalculateInterestRate()  or when the bank account owner makes a\ndeposit or withdrawal.\nIf, however , the interest calculation method was named\ngetInterestRate()  and client software called that instead, the interest rate\nwould always be calculated on the fly and there would be no potential error .\nValidating Data\nThe third reason to avoid direct access when setting a value is that client\ncode can too easily set an instance variable to an invalid value. A better\napproach is to call a method in the class, whose job is to set the value. As\nthe developer , you can include validation code in that method to ensure that\nthe value being set is appropriate. Consider the code in Listing 8-3 , whose\npurpose is to manage the members of a club.\nFile: V alidatingData_ClubExample/Club.py\n# Club class  \n \nclass Club():  \n \n    def __init__(self, clubName, maxMembers):  ', "        self.clubName = clubName 1 \n        self.maxMembers = maxMembers  \n        self.membersList = []  \n \n    def addMember(self, name): 2\n        # Make sure that there is enough room left  \n        if len(self.membersList) < self.maxMembers:  \n            self.membersList.append(name)  \n            print('OK.', name, 'has been added to the',  \nself.clubName, 'club')  \n        else:  \n            print('Sorry, but we cannot add', name, 'to the',  \nself.clubName, 'club.')  \n            print('This club already has the maximum of',  \nself.maxMembers, 'members.')  \n \n    def report(self): 3 \n        print()  \n        print('Here are the', len(self.membersList), 'members  \nof the', self.clubName,  \n              'club:')  \n        for name in self.membersList:  \n            print('   ' + name)  \n        print()\nListing 8-3 : An example of a Club  class\nThe Club  code keeps track of the name of the club, the maximum number\nof members, and the list of members, all in instance variables 1. Once\ninstantiated, you can call methods to add a member to the club 2 and to\nreport the members of the club 3. (We could easily add more methods to\nremove members, change names, and so on, but these two are good enough\nthe make the point.)\nHere is some test code that uses the Club  class.\nFile: V alidatingData_ClubExample/Main_Club.py\n# Club example main program  \n \nfrom Club import *  \n \n# Create a club with at most 5 members  ", "oProgrammingClub = Club('Programming', 5)  \n \noProgrammingClub.addMember('Joe Schmoe')  \noProgrammingClub.addMember('Cindy Lou Hoo')  \noProgrammingClub.addMember('Dino Richmond')  \noProgrammingClub.addMember('Susie Sweetness')  \noProgrammingClub.addMember('Fred Farkle')  \noProgrammingClub.report()\nWe create a Programming club that allows a maximum of five members\nand then we add five members. The code runs well and reports the members\nadded to the club:\nOK. Joe Schmoe has been added to the Programming club  \nOK. Cindy Lou Hoo has been added to the Programming club  \nOK. Dino Richmond has been added to the Programming club  \nOK. Susie Sweetness has been added to the Programming club  \nOK. Fred Farkle has been added to the Programming club\nNow let’ s try to add a sixth member:\n# Attempt to add additional member  \noProgrammingClub.addMember('Iwanna Join')\nThis attempt to add a member is rejected, and we see an appropriate error\nmessage:\nSorry, but we cannot add Iwanna Join to the Programming club.  \nThis club already has the maximum of 5 members.\nThe code of addMember()  does all the validation needed to ensure that a\ncall to add a new member works correctly or generates an error message.\nHowever , with direct access, a client could change the fundamental nature\nof the Club  class. For example, a client could maliciously or accidentally\nchange the maximum number of members:\noProgrammingClub.maxMembers = 300\nFurther , suppose you know that the Club  class represents the members as\na list, and you know the name of the instance variable that represents the", "members. In that case you can write client code to add to the list of\nmembers directly , without making the method call, like so:\noProgrammingClub.memberList.append('Iwanna Join')\nThis line would push the membership over the intended limit because it\navoids the code that ensures that the request to add the member is valid.\nClient code using direct access could even cause an error inside the Club\nobject. For example, the instance variable self.maxMembers  is intended to\nbe an integer . Using direct access, client code could change its value to a\nstring. Any subsequent call to addMember()  would crash at the first line of\nthat method, where it attempts to compare the length of the list of members\nagainst the maximum number of members, because Python cannot compare\nan integer to a string.\nAllowing direct access to instance variables from outside an object can\nbe dangerous, bypassing safeguards that were designed to protect the data\nof an object.\nStrict Interpretation with Getters and Setters\nThe strict approach to encapsulation says that client code never  accesses an\ninstance variable directly . If a class wants to allow client software to access\nthe information held inside an object, the standard approach is to include a\ngetter  and a setter  method in the class.\ngetter\nA method that retrieves data from an object instantiated from\na class.\nsetter\nA method that assigns data into an object instantiated from a\nclass.", 'Getter and setter methods are designed to allow writers of client software\nto get data from and set data in an object, without needing explicit\nknowledge of the implementation of a class—specifically , without having\nto know or use the name of any instance variable. The Person  class code in\nListing 8-1  has an instance variable self.salary . In Listing 8-4  we add a\ngetter and a setter to the Person  class to allow the caller to get and set the\nsalary , without providing direct access to the Person ’s self.salary\ninstance variable.\nFile: PersonGettersSettersAndDirectAccess/Person.py\nclass Person():  \n    def __init__(self, name, salary):  \n        self.name = name  \n        self.salary = salary  \n \n    # Allow the caller to retrieve the salary  \n  1 def getSalary(self):   \n        return self.salary  \n \n    # Allow the caller to set a new salary  \n  2 def setSalary(self, salary):  \n        self.salary = salary\nListing 8-4 : An example of a Person  class with a getter and a setter\nThe get 1 and set 2 portions of these method names are not required but\nare used by convention. Y ou generally follow these words with a\ndescription of the data being accessed, in this case Salary . While it is\ntypical to use the name of the instance variable being accessed, this is also\nnot a requirement.\nListing 8-5  shows some test code that instantiates two Person  objects,\nthen gets and sets their salaries using these getter and setter methods.\nFile:\nPersonGettersSettersAndDirectAccess/Main_PersonGetterSette\nr.py', "# Person example main program using getters and setters  \n \nfrom Person import *  \n \n1 oPerson1 = Person('Joe Schmoe', 90000)  \noPerson2 = Person('Jane Smith', 99000)  \n \n# Get the salaries using getter and print\n2 print(oPerson1.getSalary())  \nprint(oPerson2.getSalary())  \n \n# Change the salaries using setter\n3 oPerson1.setSalary(100000)  \noPerson2.setSalary(111111)  \n \n# Get the salaries and print again  \nprint(oPerson1.getSalary())  \nprint(oPerson2.getSalary())\nListing 8-5 : Example main code using getter and setter methods\nFirst we create two Person  objects from the Person  class 1. Then we use\nthe getter and setter methods to retrieve 2 and change 3 the salaries in the\nPerson  objects.\nGetters and setters provide a formal way to get and set values in an\nobject. They enforce a layer of protection that only allows access to\ninstance variables if the class writer wants to allow it.\nNOTE\nSome Python literatur e uses the terms accessor  for a getter method\nand mutator  for the setter method. These ar e just differ ent names for\nthe same things. I will use the mor e generic terms getter  and setter .\nSafe Direct Access\nThere are certain circumstances where it seems reasonable to access\ninstance variables directly: when it is absolutely clear what the instance\nvariable means, little or no validation of the data is needed, and there is no", 'chance that the name will ever change. A good example of this is the Rect\n(rectangle) class in the pygame package. A rectangle in pygame is defined\nusing four values—x, y , width, and height—like this:\noRectangle = pygame.Rect(10, 20, 300, 300)\nAfter creating that rectangle object, using oRectangle.x , oRectangle.y ,\noRectangle.width , and oRectangle.height  directly as variables seems\nacceptable.\nMaking Instance Variables More Private\nIn Python, all instance variables are public (that is, can be accessed by code\nexternal to the class). But what if you want to allow access to some of your\nclass’ s instance variables, but not all of them? Some OOP languages allow\nyou to explicitly mark certain instance variables as public  or private , but\nPython doesn’ t have those keywords. However , there are two ways that\nprogrammers who develop classes in Python can indicate that their instance\nvariables and methods are intended to be private.\nImplicitly Private\nTo mark an instance variable as one that should never be accessed\nexternally , by convention you start the name of your instance variable with\none leading underscore:\nself._name  \nself._socialSecurityNumber  \nself._dontTouchThis\nInstance variables with names like these are intended to represent private\ndata, and client software should never attempt to access them directly . The\ncode may still work if the instance variables are accessed, but it is not\nguaranteed.\nThe same convention is used for method names:\ndef _internalMethod(self):  \n ', "def _dontCallMeFromClientSoftware(self):\nAgain, this is only a convention; there is no enforcement. If any client\nsoftware makes a call to a method with a name beginning with an\nunderscore, Python will allow it, but there is a good chance that doing so\nwill lead to unexpected errors.\nMore Explicitly Private\nPython does allow for a more explicit level of privatization. T o disallow\nclient software from directly accessing your data, you create an instance\nvariable name that starts with two underscores.\nSuppose we create a class named PrivatePerson  with an instance\nvariable self.__privateData  that should never be accessed from outside\nan object:\n# PrivatePerson class  \n \nclass PrivatePerson():  \n \n    def __init__(self, name, privateData):  \n        self.name = name  \n      1 self.__privateData = privateData  \n \n    def getName(self):  \n        return self.name  \n \n    def setName(self, name):  \n        self.name = name\nWe can then create a PrivatePerson  object, passing in some data that we\nwish to keep private 1. Attempting to access the __privateData  instance\nvariable directly from client software, like this:\nusersPrivateData = oPrivatePerson.__privateData\nwill generate an error:\nAttributeError: 'PrivatePerson' object has no attribute  \n'__privateData'", 'Similarly , if you create a method name that starts with two underscores,\nany attempt by client software to call the method will generate an error .\nPython provides this ability by performing name mangling . Behind the\nscenes, Python changes any name that starts with two underscores by\nprepending it with an underscore and the name of the class, so __<name>\nbecomes _<className> __<name> . For example, in the PrivatePerson\nclass, Python will change self.__privateData  to\nself._PrivatePerson__privateData . Therefore, if a client tries to use the\nname oPrivatePerson.__privateData , that name won’ t be recognized.\nThis is a subtle change designed as a deterrent to using direct access, but\nyou should note that it doesn’ t absolutely guarantee privacy . If the client\nprogrammer knows how this works, they can still access the instance\nvariable with <object> ._<className> __<name>  (or, in our example,\noPrivatePerson._PrivatePerson__privateData ).\nDecorators and @property\nAt a high level, a decorator is a method that takes another method as an\nargument and extends the way the original method works. (Decorators can\nalso be functions that decorate functions or methods, but I’ll concentrate on\nmethods.) Decorators are an advanced topic and are generally beyond the\nscope of this book. However , there is a set of built-in decorators that\nprovide a compromise between direct access and the use of getters and\nsetters in a class.\nA decorator is written as a line that starts with the @ symbol followed by\na decorator name and is placed directly before the def statement of a\nmethod. This applies the decorator to a method to add to its behavior:\n@<decorator>  \ndef <someMethod> (self, <parameters> )  \nWe’ll use two built-in decorators and apply them to two methods in a\nclass to implement a property .', 'property\nAn attribute of a class that appears to client code to be an\ninstance variable, but instead causes a method to be called\nwhen it is accessed.\nA property allows class developers to use indirection, the way that a\nmagician uses misdirection—the audience thinks they are seeing one thing,\nwhile behind the scenes something very dif ferent is happening. When\nwriting a class to use property decorators, the developer writes a getter and\na setter method and adds a distinct built-in decorator to each one. The first\nmethod is a getter and is preceded with the built-in @property  decorator .\nThe name of the method defines a name of a property to be used by client\ncode. The second method is a setter and is preceded with the @<name of\nthe property> .setter  decorator . Here is a minimal sample class:\nclass Example():  \n    def __init__(self, startingValue):  \n        self._x = startingValue  \n \n    @property  \n    def x(self):  # this is the decorated getter method  \n        return self._x  \n \n    @x.setter  \n    def x(self, value):  # this is the decorated setter  \nmethod \n        self._x = value\nIn the Example  class, x is the name of the property . After the standard\n__init__()  method, the unusual thing is that we have two methods that\nboth have the same name: the name of the property . The first method is a\ngetter , while the second is a setter . The setter method is optional, and if it’ s\nnot present, the property will be read-only .\nGiven the Example  class, here is some sample client code:\noExample = Example(10)  \nprint(oExample.x)  \noExample.x = 20', "In this code we create an instance of the Example  class, make a call to\nprint() , and execute a simple assignment. From the client’ s point of view ,\nthis code is highly readable. When we write oExample.x , it looks like we\nare using direct access to an instance variable. However , when client code\naccesses the value of an object’ s property (on the right side of an\nassignment statement or as an ar gument in a call to a function or method),\nPython translates it into a call to the getter method of the object. When an\nobject dot property appears on the left side of an assignment statement,\nPython calls the setter method. The getter and setter methods af fect the real\ninstance variable, self._x .\nHere is a more realistic example that should help make this clear . Listing\n8-6 shows a Student  class that includes a property grade , properly\ndecorated getter and setter methods, and a private instance variable\n__grade .\nFile: PropertyDecorator/Student.py\n# Using a property to (indirectly) access data in an object  \n \nclass Student():  \n \n    def __init__(self, name, startingGrade=0):  \n        self.__name = name  \n        self.grade = startingGrade 1 \n \n    @property 2 \n    def grade(self): 3 \n        return self.__grade  \n \n    @grade.setter 4 \n    def grade(self, newGrade): 5 \n        try:  \n            newGrade = int(newGrade)  \n        except (TypeError, ValueError) as e:  \n            raise type(e)('New grade: ' + str(newGrade) + ',  \nis an invalid type.')  \n        if (newGrade < 0) or (newGrade > 100):  \n            raise ValueError('New grade: ' + str(newGrade) +  \n', must be between 0 and 100.')  \n        self.__grade = newGrade", "Listing 8-6 : The Student  class with pr operty decorators\nThe __init__()  method has a little trick to it, so let’ s examine the other\nmethods first. Notice we have two methods with the name grade() .\nPreceding the definition of the first grade()  method, we add an @property\ndecorator 2. This defines the name grade  as a property of any object created\nfrom this class. The first method 3 is a getter that just returns the value of\nthe current grade, kept in the private self.__grade  instance variable, but\ncould include any code that might be needed to calculate a value and return\nit.\nPreceding the second grade()  method is an @grade.setter  decorator 4.\nThis second method 5 accepts a new value as a parameter , does a number of\nchecks to ensure that value is valid, then sets the new value into\nself.__grade .\nThe __init__()  method first stores the student’ s name in an instance\nvariable. The next line 1 seems straightforward but is a little unusual. As we\nhave seen, we typically store the values of parameters into instance\nvariables. Therefore, we might be tempted to write this line as:\nself.__grade = startingGrade\nBut instead, we are storing the starting grade into the property grade .\nSince grade  is a property , Python translates this assignment statement into a\ncall to the setter method 5, which has the advantage of validating the input\nbefore storing the value in the instance variable self.__grade .\nListing 8-7  provides some test code that uses the Student  class.\nFile: PropertyDecorator/Main_Property .py\n# Main Student property example\n1 oStudent1= Student('Joe Schmoe')  \noStudent2= Student ('Jane Smith')  \n \n# Get the students' grades using the 'grade' property and  \nprint\n2 print(oStudent1.grade)  \nprint(oStudent2.grade)  ", "print() \n \n# Set new values using the 'grade' property\n3 oStudent1.grade = 85  \noStudent2.grade = 92  \n \n4 print(oStudent1.grade)  \nprint(oStudent2.grade)\nListing 8-7 : The main code that cr eates Student  objects and accesses a\nproperty\nIn the test code, we first create two Student  objects 1 and print the grade\nof each 2. It looks like we’re reaching into each object directly to get the\ngrade values, but since grade  is a property Python turns these lines into\ncalls to the getter method and returns the value of the private instance\nvariable self.__grade  for each object.\nWe then set new grade values for each Student  object 3. Here it looks\nlike we’re setting values directly into each object’ s data, but again, because\ngrade  is a property , Python turns these lines into calls to the setter method.\nThat method validates each value before doing the assignment. The test\ncode ends by printing the new values of the grades 4.\nWhen we run the test code, we get this output, as we expect:\n0 \n0 \n \n85 \n92\nUsing the @property  and @<property_name> .setter  decorators gives\nyou the best of both the direct access and getter -and-setter worlds. Client\nsoftware can be written in a way that appears  to access instance variables\ndirectly , but as the class programmer , your decorated methods get and set\nthe actual instance variables owned by the object and even allow for\nvalidation of inputs. This approach supports encapsulation because the\nclient code is not accessing an instance variable directly .", 'While this technique is used by many professional Python developers, I\npersonally find it a little ambiguous, because when I read other developers’\ncode that uses this approach, it is not immediately apparent whether it’ s\nusing direct accesses to instance variables or using properties that Python\ntranslates into calls to decorated methods. I prefer to use standard getter and\nsetter methods and will use them in the rest of this book.\nEncapsulation in pygwidgets Classes\nThe definition of encapsulation at the start of this chapter focused on two\nareas: hiding internal details and having all related code in one place. All\nthe classes in pygwidgets  were designed with these considerations in mind.\nAs examples, consider the TextButton  and CustomButton  classes.\nThe methods of these two classes encapsulate all the functionality of GUI\nbuttons. While the source code of these classes is available, there is no need\nfor a client programmer to look at it to use them ef fectively . There’ s also no\nneed for client code to attempt to access any of their instance variables: all\nbutton functionality is available through calling the methods of these\nclasses. This adheres to the strict interpretation of encapsulation, meaning\nthat the only way that client software should access an object’ s data is by\ncalling that object’ s methods. A client programmer can think of these\nclasses as black boxes, since there is no reason to look at how they\naccomplish their tasks.\nNOTE\nA whole black box testing  industry has developed ar ound the idea of\na test pr ogrammer being given a class to test without being allowed\nto see the code of the class. The tester is only supplied with\ndocumentation of the interfaces, and with that writes code that tests\nall the interfaces under many differ ent cases to ensur e that all\nmethods work as described. The set of tests not only ensur es that the\ncode and documentation match, but is used again whenever code is\nadded or modified in the class to ensur e that the changes have not\nbroken anything.', 'A Story from the Real World\nA number of years ago, I was involved in the design and development of a\nvery lar ge educational project that was built in an environment called\nDirector  from Macromedia (later Adobe), using the object-oriented Lingo\nlanguage. Director was designed to be extended through XTRAs  that could\nadd functionality , similar to the way plug-ins are added to browsers. These\nXTRAs were developed and sold by a number of third-party vendors. In the\ndesign, we planned on storing navigational and other course-related\ninformation in a database. I looked at all the dif ferent database XTRAs that\nwere available and purchased a particular XTRA, which I’ll call XTRA1.\nEach XTRA came with documentation of its API, which showed how to\nmake queries to the database using Structured Query Language (SQL). I\ndecided to create a Database  class that incorporated all the functionality of\naccessing the database using XTRA1’ s API. That way , all code that\ncommunicated with the XTRA directly was in the Database  class. Figur e 8-\n1 shows the overall architecture.\nFigure 8-1 : The architecture of accessing a database using an object and an XTRA\nWhen the program started, it created a single instance of the Database\nclass. The main code was a client of the Database  object. Whenever the\nmain code wanted information from the database, rather than formatting a\nSQL query itself, it called a method of the Database  object, supplying', 'details about what information it wanted. The methods in the Database\nobject translated each request into a SQL query made to XTRA1 to get data\nfrom the database. That way , only the code of the Database  object knew\nhow to access the XTRA using its API.\nThe program worked well, and the customers enjoyed using the product.\nBut every once in a while, we would run into errors in the data we got back\nfrom the database. I contacted the XTRA1 developer and gave many easily\nreproducible examples of the problems. Unfortunately , the developer never\naddressed these issues.\nBecause of the lack of response, we eventually decided to purchase a\ndifferent database XTRA, XTRA2, to serve this purpose. XTRA2 worked\nin a similar way but had some subtle dif ferences in how it was initialized\nand required some minor changes in the way that SQL queries were built.\nBecause the Database  class encapsulated all the details of\ncommunicating with the XTRA, we were able to make all the necessary\nchanges to work with XTRA2 only in the Database  class. W e did not\nchange a single line in the main program (the client code).\nIn this case, I was both the Database  class developer and the client\nsoftware developer . If my client code had used the names of instance\nvariables in the class, I would have had to crawl through the program,\nmodifying each line of relevant code. Using encapsulation with a class\nsaved me from countless hours of reworking and testing.\nAs a follow-up to the story , while XTRA2 worked well, that company\neventually went out of business and I had to go through the same process\nagain. Once again, because of encapsulation, only the code of the Database\nclass was modified to work with XTRA3.\nAbstraction\nAbstraction is another OOP concept closely related to encapsulation; many\ndevelopers consider it to be the fourth tenet of OOP .\nWhereas encapsulation is about implementation, hiding the details of the\ncode and data that make up a class, abstraction  is about the client’ s view of\na class. It’ s about the perception of a class from the outside.', 'abstraction\nHandling complexity by hiding unnecessary details.\nEssentially , abstraction is a reminder to make sure that the user ’s view of\na system is as simple as possible.\nAbstraction is extremely common in consumer products. Many people\nuse TVs, computers, microwave ovens, cars, and so on every day . We\nbecome comfortable with the user interface that these products extend to us.\nThrough their controls, they provide an abstraction of their functionality .\nYou press the accelerator pedal in a car to make it go forward. W ith a\nmicrowave, you set an amount of time and press Start to heat up some food.\nBut few of us really know how these products work internally .\nHere’ s an example of abstraction from the world of computer science. In\nprogramming, a stack  is a mechanism for remembering data in a last in,\nfirst out  (LIFO)  order . Think of a pile of plates, where clean plates are\nadded to the top and users take one from the top when they need a plate. A\nstack has two standard operations: push  adds an item to the top of the stack,\nand pop removes the topmost item from the stack.\nA stack is particularly useful whenever your program does any\nnavigation because it can be used to leave a trail of breadcrumbs for finding\nyour way back. This is how programming languages keep track of the\nexecution of function and method calls in code: when you call a function or\nmethod, the return point is pushed onto a stack, and when the function or\nmethod returns, the place to return to is discovered by popping the most\nrecent information of f the top of the stack. In this way , code can make as\nmany levels of calls as you need, and it always unwinds correctly .\nAs an abstraction, suppose a client program required the functionality of\na stack that would be simple to create and would provide the ability to push\nand pop information. If this were written as a class, the client code would\ncreate a stack like this:\noStack = Stack()\nA client would add information by calling a push()  method like this:', "oStack.push( <someData> )\nAnd it would retrieve the most recent data by calling a pop()  method like\nthis:\n<someVariable>  = oStack.pop()\nThe client would not need to know or care how these methods were\nimplemented or how the data was stored. The implementation of the Stack\nwould be handled completely by the methods of the Stack .\nWhile the client code could view a Stack  class as a black box, writing\nsuch a class in Python is fairly trivial. Listing 8-8  shows how it could be\nimplemented.\nFile: Stack/Stack.py\n# Stack class  \n \nclass Stack():  \n    ''' Stack class implements a last in first out LIFO  \nalgorithm'''  \n    def __init__(self, startingStackAsList=None):  \n        if startingStackAsList is None:  \n          1 self.dataList = [ ]  \n        else:  \n            self.dataList = startingStackAsList[:] # make a  \ncopy \n         \n  2 def push(self, item):        \n        self.dataList.append(item)  \n \n  3 def pop(self):  \n        if len(self.dataList) == 0:  \n            raise IndexError  \n        element = self.dataList.pop()  \n        return element  \n \n  4 def peek(self):  \n        # Retrieve the top item, without removing it  \n        item = self.dataList[-1]  \n        return item  ", " \n  5 def getSize(self):  \n        nElements = len(self.dataList)  \n        return nElements  \n \n  6 def show(self):  \n        # Show the stack in a vertical orientation  \n        print('Stack is:')  \n        for value in reversed(self.dataList):  \n            print('   ', value)\nListing 8-8 : A stack as a Python class\nThe Stack  class keeps track of all the data using a list instance variable\nnamed self.dataList  1. The client doesn’ t need to know this level of\ndetail, but push()  2 just adds an item to the internal list using the Python\nappend()  operation, while pop()  3 pops the last element from the internal\nlist. Because it is easy to do, this implementation of the Stack  class also\nimplements three additional methods:\npeek()  4 allows the caller to obtain the data at the top of the stack without\nremoving it from the stack.\ngetSize()  5 returns the number of items on the stack.\nshow()  6 prints the contents of the stack in the way that the client thinks of\na stack: the data is displayed vertically , with the most recent data pushed\nshown at the top. This can be helpful in debugging client code that involves\nmultiple calls to push()  and pop() .\nThis was an extremely simple example, but as you gain more experience\nwriting classes, your classes will typically become more complex. Along\nthe way , you may find cleaner and more ef ficient ways of writing some\nmethods and perhaps rewrite them. Because objects provide both\nencapsulation and abstraction, as the writer of a class, you should feel free\nto modify its code and data, as long as the published interfaces do not\nchange. Changes to the implementation of methods should have no ill\neffects on client software but rather should allow you to make\nimprovements without af fecting any client code. In fact, if you find ways to\nmake your code more ef ficient and publish a new version, client code may\nappear to speed up, with zero changes required to that code.", 'A property is an excellent example of abstraction. As you saw earlier ,\nwith properties the client programmer can use a syntax that makes their\nintent clear (to get and set a value in an object). The implementation in the\nmethods that are called as a result can be much more complicated, but is\ntotally hidden from the client code.\nSummary\nEncapsulation is the first major tenet of object-oriented programming,\nallowing classes to hide their implementation and data from client code and\nensuring that a class provides all the functionality that a client needs in a\nsingle place.\nA key concept of OOP is that objects own their data, and that’ s why I\nrecommend that you provide getter and setter methods if you want client\ncode to access the data held in an instance variable. Python does allow\ndirect access to instance variables using dot syntax, but I strongly\nencourage you to stay away from this syntax for the reasons laid out in this\nchapter .\nThere are conventions for marking instance variables and methods as\nprivate, using a leading underscore or double underscore depending on the\nlevel of privatization you require. As a compromise, Python also allows the\nuse of the @property  decorator . This makes it appear as if the client code is\nable to access an instance variable directly , while behind the scenes Python\nturns such references into calls to the decorated getter and setter methods in\nthe class.\nThe pygwidgets  package provides many good examples of\nencapsulation. As a client programmer , you see a class from the outside and\nwork with the interfaces that the class provides. As a class designer ,\nabstraction—handling complexity by hiding details—helps you design a\ngood interface by considering the interface of the class from the client’ s\npoint of view . However , in Python, you often have the source code available\nso that you can look at the implementation if you wish.', '9\nPOLYMORPHISM\nThis chapter is about the second major\ntenet of OOP: polymorphism. Its\ncomponent parts are from the Greek: the\nprefix poly means “much” or “many,”\nand morphism means “shape,” “form,”\nor “structure.”\nSo, polymorphism  essentially means many forms . I’m not talking about a\nStar T rek–style shape-shifting alien—in fact, it’ s quite the opposite. Rather\nthan one thing taking on many shapes, polymorphism in OOP is about how\nmultiple classes can have methods with the exact same names. This will\neventually give us a highly intuitive way to act on a collection of objects,\nindependent of what class each came from.\nOOP programmers often use the term “send a message” when we talk\nabout client code calling a method of an object. What the object should do\nwhen it receives the message is up to the object. W ith polymorphism, we can\nsend the same message to multiple objects, and each object will react\ndifferently depending on what it’ s designed to do and the data available to it.\nIn this chapter , I’ll discuss how this ability allows you to build packages of\nclasses that are easily extensible and predictable. W e’ll also use\npolymorphism with operators to make the same operators perform dif ferent\noperations depending on the data types they are working with. Finally , I’ll\nshow you how to use the print()  function to get valuable debugging\ninformation from objects.', 'Sending Messages to Real-World Objects\nLet’s look at polymorphism in the real world, using cars as an example. All\ncars have an accelerator pedal. When the driver presses that pedal, they’re\nsending the “accelerate” message to the car . The car they’re driving could\nhave an internal combustion engine or an electric motor , or be a hybrid. Each\nof these types of cars has its own implementation of what happens when it\nreceives the accelerate message, and each behaves accordingly .\nPolymorphism allows for easier adoption of new technology . If someone\nwere to develop a nuclear -powered car , the user interface of the car would\nremain the same—the driver would still press the accelerator pedal to send\nthe same message—but a very dif ferent mechanism would make the nuclear -\npowered car go faster .\nAs another real-world example, imagine you enter a lar ge room with a\nbank of light switches that control a variety of dif ferent lights. The underlying mechanisms that cause\nincandescent, fluorescent, and LED bulbs to emit light are wildly dif ferent,\nbut each achieves the user ’s intended goal.\nA Classic Example of Polymorphism in\nProgramming\nIn terms of OOP , polymorphism is about how client code can call a method\nwith the exact same name in dif ferent objects, and each object will do\nwhatever it needs to do to implement the meaning of that method for that\nobject.\nThe classic example of polymorphism is to consider code that represents\ndifferent types of pets. Listing 9-4  shows the Triangle  class that creates randomly\nsized right triangles whose edges are parallel to the x- and y-axes, with the\nright angle in the upper -left corner .\nFile: Shapes/Circle.py\n# Circle class  \n \nimport pygame  \nimport random  \nimport math  \n \n# Set up the colors  \nRED = (255, 0, 0)  \nGREEN = (0, 255, 0)  \nBLUE = (0, 0, 255)  \n \nclass Circle():  \n \n    def __init__(self, window, maxWidth, maxHeight):  \n        self.window = window  \n \n        self.color = random.choice((RED, GREEN, BLUE))  \n        self.x = random.randrange(1, maxWidth - 100)  \n        self.y = random.randrange(25, maxHeight - 100)  \n        self.radius = random.randrange(10, 50)  \n        self.centerX = self.x + self.radius  \n        self.centerY = self.y + self.radius  \n        self.rect = pygame.Rect(self.x, self.y,  ', "                                self.radius * 2, self.radius *  \n2) \n        self.shapeType = 'Circle'  \n         \n  1 def clickedInside(self, mousePoint):  \n        distance = math.sqrt(((mousePoint[0] - 100)  \n        self.y = random.randrange(25, maxHeight - 100)  \n        self.rect = pygame.Rect(self.x, self.y,  \n                                self.width, self.height)  \n        self.shapeType = 'Triangle'  \n         \n  4 def clickedInside(self, mousePoint):  \n        inRect = self.rect.collidepoint(mousePoint)  \n        if not inRect:  \n            return False  \n \n        # Do some math to see if the point is inside the  \ntriangle  \n        xOffset = mousePoint[0] - self.x  \n        yOffset = mousePoint[1] - self.y  \n        if xOffset == 0:  \n            return True  \n \n        # Calculate the slope (rise over run)  \n        pointSlopeFromYIntercept = (yOffset - self.height) /  \nxOffset \n        if pointSlopeFromYIntercept < self.triangleSlope:  \n            return True  \n        else:  \n            return False  \n \n    def getType(self):  \n        return self.shapeType  \n \n  5 def getArea(self):  \n        theArea = .5 * self.width * self.height  \n        return theArea  \n \n  6 def draw(self):  \n        pygame.draw.polygon(self.window, self.color,  \n                            ((self.x, self.y + self.height),  \n                            (self.x, self.y),  \n                            (self.x + self.width, self.y)))\nListing 9-4 : The Triangle  class\nTo understand the polymorphism at work here, let’ s look at the codeof the\nclickedInside()  method for each shape. The clickedInside()  method of", 'the Square  class was very simple: a check to see if the mouse click occurred\ninside the rectangle of the Square . The details of the calculations for\nclickedInside()  in the Circle  and Triangle  classes are not particularly\nimportant, but they clearly are doing dif ferent calculations. Reverse order to check last drawn shape first  \n            for oShape in reversed(shapesList): 3 \n                if oShape.clickedInside(event.pos): 4 \n                    area = oShape.getArea() 5 \n                    area = str(area)  \n                    theType = oShape.getType()  \n                    newText = 'Clicked on a ' + theType + '  \nwhose area is' + area)  \n                    oStatusLine.setValue(newText)  \n                    break # For example, to add the ability\nfor our program to include ellipses, we would build an Ellipse  class that\nimplements the getArea() , clickedInside() , draw() , and getType()\nmethods. (The code of the clickedInside()  method might be\nmathematically complicated for an ellipse!)\nOnce we’ve written the code of the Ellipse  class, the only change we\nneed to make to the setup code is to add Ellipse  to the tuple of shape classes', 'to choose from. The code in the main loop that does the checking for clicks,\ngets the area of the shape, and so on will not need to change at all.\nThis example demonstrates two important features of polymorphism:\nPolymorphism extends the concept of abstraction discussed in Chapter 8  to a\ncollection of classes. If multiple classes have the same interfaces for their\nmethods, the client programmer can ignore the implementation of those\nmethods in all the classes.\nPolymorphism can make client programming easier . If a client programmer\nis already familiar with the interfaces provided by one or more classes, then\ncalling the methods of another polymorphic class should as be simple as\nfollowing the pattern.\npygwidgets Exhibits Polymorphism\nAll the classes in pygwidgets  were designed to use polymorphism, and they\nall implement two common methods. The first is the handleEvent()  method\nwe first used in Chapter 6 , which takes an event object as a parameter . Each\nclass must contain its own code in this method to handle any event that\npygame may generate. Each time through the main loop, client programs\nneed to call the handleEvent()  method for every instance of every object\ninstantiated from pygwidgets .\nSecond is the draw()  method, which draws images to the window . A\ntypical drawing portion of a program that uses pygwidgets  might look like\nthis:\ninputTextA.draw()  \ninputTextB.draw()  \ndisplayTextA.draw()  \ndisplayTextB.draw()  \nrestartButton.draw()  \ncheckBoxA.draw()  \ncheckBoxB.draw()  \nradioCustom1.draw()  \nradioCustom2.draw()  \nradioCustom3.draw()  \ncheckBoxC.draw()  \nradioDefault1.draw()  \nradioDefault2.draw()  ', 'radioDefault3.draw()  \nstatusButton.draw()\nFrom the client’ s point of view , each line just calls the draw()  method and\npasses in nothing. From an internal point of view , the code to implement\neach of these methods is very dif ferent. The draw()  method of the\nTextButton  class is completely dif ferent from that of the InputText  class,\nfor instance.\nAdditionally , all widgets that manage a value contain a setValue()  and\noptionally a getValue()  method. For example, to get the text the user enters\ninto an InputText  widget, you call the getValue()  getter method. Radio\nbutton and checkbox widgets also have a getValue()  method to get their\ncurrent values. T o put new text into a DisplayText  widget, you call the\nsetValue()  setter method, passing in the new text. Radio button and\ncheckbox widgets can be set with a call to their setValue()  method.\nPolymorphism allows client programmers to feel comfortable with a\ncollection of classes. When clients see a pattern, like using methods named\nhandleEvent()  and draw() , it makes it easy for them to predict how to use a\nnew class in the same collection.\nAs of this writing, the pygwidgets  package does not provide either a\nhorizontal or a vertical Slider  class widget to allow a user to easily select\nfrom a range of numbers. If I were to add these widgets, they would certainly\ncontain the following: a handleEvent()  method, where all user interaction\nwould take place; a getValue()  and a setValue()  method to get and set a\ncurrent value for the Slider ; and a draw()  method.\nPolymorphism for Operators\nPython also exhibits polymorphism with operators. Consider the following\nexample with the + operator:\nvalue1 = 4  \nvalue2 = 5  \nresult = value1 + value2  \nprint(result)\nwhich prints:', "9\nThe + operator here clearly means “add” in a mathematic sense because\nboth variables contain integer values. But now consider this second example:\nvalue1 = 'Joe'  \nvalue2 = 'Schmoe'  \nresult = value1 + value2  \nprint(result)\nwhich prints:\nJoeSchmoe\nThe line result = value1 + value2  is exactly the same as in the first\nexample, but it performs a completely dif ferent operation. W ith string values,\nthe + operator performs a string concatenation. The same operator was used,\nbut a dif ferent action was performed.\nThis technique of having multiple meanings for an operator is commonly\nknown as operator overloading . For some classes, the ability to overload\noperators adds highly useful features and greatly improves the readability of\nclient code.\nMagic Methods\nPython reserves method names with the unusual form of two underscores,\nsome name, and two underscores for a particular purpose:\n__<someName> __()\nThese are of ficially called special methods  but are more commonly\nreferred to by Python programmers as magic methods . Many of these are\nalready defined, such as __init__() , which is called whenever you\ninstantiate an object from a class, but all other names in this style are\navailable for future expansion. These are known as “magic” methods because\nPython calls them behind the scenes whenever it detects an operator , a\nspecial function call, or some other special circumstance. They are not\nintended to be called by client code directly .", "NOTE\nBecause the names of these magic methods ar e difficult to pr onounce\n—for example, __init__()  is read as “underscor e underscor e init\nunderscor e underscor e”—Python pr ogrammers often r efer to these\nas dunder  methods (a shortened version of double underscore ). This\nmethod would be r eferr ed to as “dunder init.”\nContinuing with the previous examples, we’ll look at how this works with\nthe + operator . The built-in data types (integer , float, string, Boolean, and so\non) are actually implemented as classes in Python. W e can see this by testing\nwith the built-in isinstance()  function, which takes an object and a class\nand returns True  if the object was instantiated from the class or False  if not.\nThese lines will both report True :\nprint(isinstance(123, int))  \nprint(isinstance('some string', str))\nThe classes for the built-in data types contain a set of magic methods,\nincluding ones for the basic math operators. When Python detects the +\noperator with integers, it calls the magic method named __add__()  in the\nbuilt-in integer class, which performs integer addition. When Python sees the\nsame operator used with strings, it calls the __add__()  method in the string\nclass, which performs string concatenation.\nThis mechanism is generalized so that when Python encounters a  +\noperator when working with objects instantiated from your class, it will call\nthe __add__()  method if one is present in your class. Therefore, as the class\ndeveloper , you can write code to invent a new meaning for this operator .\nEach operator maps to a specific magic method name. While there are\nmany types of magic methods, let’ s start with those related to the comparison\noperators.\nComparison Operator Magic Methods\nConsider our Square  class from Listing 9-2 . You want client software to be\nable to compare two Square  objects to see if they are equal. It is up to you to\ndecide what “equal” means when comparing objects. For instance, you might", "define it as two objects being the same color and at the same location and\nwith the same size. As a simple example, we’ll define two Square  objects as\nequal if they just have the same side length. This is easily implemented by\ncomparing the self.heightAndWidth  instance variables of the two objects\nand returning a Boolean. Y ou could write your own equals()  method, and\nclient software could then call it like this:\nif oSquare1.equals(oSquare2):\nThis would work fine. However , it would be more natural for client\nsoftware to use the standard == comparison operator:\nif oSquare1 == oSquare2:\nWritten this way , Python translates the == operator into a call to a magic\nmethod of the first object. In this case, Python will attempt to call a magic\nmethod named __eq__()  in the Square  class. Table 9-1  shows the magic\nmethods for all the comparison operators.\nTable 9-1 : Comparison Operator Symbols, Meanings, and Magic Method Names\nSymbol Meaning Magic method name\n== Equal to __eq__()\n!= Not equal to __ne__()\n< Less than __lt__()\n> Greater than __gt__()\n<= Less than or equal to __le__()\n>= Greater than or equal to __ge__()\nTo allow the == comparison operator to check for equality between two\nSquare  objects, you would write a method like this in the Square  class:\ndef __eq__(self, oOtherSquare):  \n    if not isinstance(oOtherSquare, Square):  \n        raise TypeError('Second object was not a Square')  \n    if self.heightAndWidth == oOtherSquare.heightAndWidth:  \n        return True  # match \n    else:  \n        return False  # not a match", 'When Python detects an == comparison where the first object is a Square ,\nit calls this method in the Square  class. Since Python is a loosely typed\nlanguage (it doesn’ t require you to define variable types), the second\nparameter could be of any data type. However , in order for the comparison to\nwork correctly , the second parameter must also be a Square  object. W e\nperform a check using the isinstance()  function, which works with\nprogrammer -defined classes the same way it works with built-in classes. If\nthe second object is not a Square , we raise an exception.\nWe then compare the heightAndWidth  of the current object ( self ) with\nthe heightAndWidth  of a second object ( oOtherSquare ). This is a case where\nusing direct access to the instance variables of two objects is perfectly\nacceptable, because both objects are of the same type, and therefore they\nmust contain the same instance variables.\nA Rectangle Class with Magic Methods\nTo expand, we’ll build a program that draws a number of rectangle shapes\nusing a Rectangle  class. The user will be able to click on any two rectangles,\nand the program will report if the rectangles have the same area or if the area\nof the first one is lar ger or smaller than the area of the second rectangle.\nWe’ll use the ==, <, and > operators and expect the result to be a Boolean\nTrue  or False  for each comparison. Listing 9-6  contains the code of the\nRectangle  class, which implements magic methods for these operators.\nFile: MagicMethods/Rectangle/Rectangle.py\n# Rectangle class  \n \nimport pygame  \nimport random  \n \n# Set up the colors  \nRED = (255, 0, 0)  \nGREEN = (0, 255, 0)  \nBLUE = (0, 0, 255)  \n \nclass Rectangle():  \n \n    def __init__(self, window):  \n        self.window = window  \n        self.width = random.choice((20, 30, 40))  ', "        self.height = random.choice((20, 30, 40))  \n        self.color = random.choice((RED, GREEN, BLUE))  \n        self.x = random.randrange(0, 400)  \n        self.y = random.randrange(0, 400)  \n        self.rect = pygame.Rect(self.x, self.y, self.width,  \nself.height)  \n        self.area = self.width * self.height  \n \n    def clickedInside(self, mousePoint):  \n        clicked = self.rect.collidepoint(mousePoint)  \n        return clicked  \n \n    # Magic method called when you compare\n    # two Rectangle objects with the == operator  \n     def __eq__ (self, oOtherRectangle): 1 \n        if not isinstance(oOtherRectangle, Rectangle):  \n            raise TypeError('Second object was not a  \nRectangle')  \n        if self.area == oOtherRectangle.area:  \n            return True  \n        else:  \n            return False  \n \n    # Magic method called when you compare\n    # two Rectangle objects with the < operator  \n     def __lt__(self, oOtherRectangle): 2 \n        if not isinstance(oOtherRectangle, Rectangle):  \n            raise TypeError('Second object was not a  \nRectangle')  \n        if self.area < oOtherRectangle.area:  \n            return True  \n        else:  \n            return False  \n \n    # Magic method called when you compare \n    # two Rectangle objects with the > operator  \n     def __gt__(self, oOtherRectangle): 3 \n        if not isinstance(oOtherRectangle, Rectangle):  \n            raise TypeError('Second object was not a  \nRectangle')  \n        if self.area > oOtherRectangle.area:  \n            return True  \n        else:  \n            return False  \n \n    def getArea(self):  \n        return self.area  ", " \n    def draw(self):  \n        pygame.draw.rect(self.window, self.color, (self.x,  \nself.y, self.width, self.height))\nListing 9-6 : The Rectangle  class\nThe methods __eq__()  1, __lt__()  2, and __gt__()  3 allow client code\nto use standard comparison operators between Rectangle  objects. T o\ncompare two rectangles for equality , you would write:\n    if oRectangle1 == oRectangle2:\nWhen this line runs, the __eq__()  method of the first object is called, and\nthe second object is passed in as the second parameter . The function returns\neither True  or False . Similarly , to compare for less than, you would write a\nline like this:\n    if oRectangle1 < oRectangle2:\nThe __lt__()  method then checks for the area of the first rectangle being\nless than the area of the second rectangle. If client code used the > operator to\ncompare two rectangles, the __gt__()  method would be called.\nMain Program Using Magic Methods\nListing 9-7  shows the code of the main program that tests the magic methods.\nFile: MagicMethods/Rectangle/Main_RectangleExample.py\nimport pygame  \nimport sys  \nfrom pygame.locals import *  \nfrom Rectangle import *  \n \n# Set up the constants  \nWHITE = (255, 255, 255)  \nWINDOW_WIDTH = 640  \nWINDOW_HEIGHT = 480  \nFRAMES_PER_SECOND = 30  \nN_RECTANGLES = 10  \nFIRST_RECTANGLE = 'first'  ", "SECOND_RECTANGLE = 'second'  \n \n# Set up the window  \npygame.init()  \nwindow = pygame.display.set_mode((WINDOW_WIDTH,  \nWINDOW_HEIGHT), 0, 32)  \nclock = pygame.time.Clock()  \n \nrectanglesList = []  \nfor i in range(0, N_RECTANGLES):  \n    oRectangle = Rectangle(window)  \n    rectanglesList.append(oRectangle)  \n \nwhichRectangle = FIRST_RECTANGLE  \n \n# Main loop  \nwhile True:  \n    for event in pygame.event.get():  \n        if event.type == QUIT:  \n            pygame.quit()  \n            sys.exit()  \n \n        if event.type == MOUSEBUTTONDOWN:  \n            for oRectangle in rectanglesList:  \n                if oRectangle.clickedInside(event.pos):  \n                    print('Clicked on', whichRectangle,  \n'rectangle.')  \n \n                    if whichRectangle == FIRST_RECTANGLE:  \n                        oFirstRectangle = oRectangle 1 \n                        whichRectangle = SECOND_RECTANGLE  \n \n                    elif whichRectangle == SECOND_RECTANGLE:  \n                        oSecondRectangle2 = oRectangle 2\n                        # User has chosen 2 rectangles, let's  \ncompare \n                        if oFirstRectangle ==  \noSecondRectangle: 3 \n                            print('Rectangles are the same  \nsize.') \n                        elif oFirstRectangle <  \noSecondRectangle: 4 \n                            print('First rectangle is smaller  \nthan second rectangle.')  \n                        else:  # must be larger 5  \n                            print('First rectangle is larger  \nthan second rectangle.')  ", '                        whichRectangle = FIRST_RECTANGLE  \n \n    # Clear the window and draw all rectangles  \n    window.fill(WHITE)  \n    for oRectangle in rectanglesList: 6  \n        oRectangle.draw()  \n \n    pygame.display.update()  \n \n    clock.tick(FRAMES_PER_SECOND)\nListing 9-7 : The main pr ogram that draws and then compar es Rectangle\nobjects\nThe user of the program clicks on pairs of rectangles to compare their\nsizes. W e store the selected rectangles in two variables 1 2.\nWe check for equality using the == operator 3, which resolves to calling\nthe __eq__()  method of the Rectangle  class. If the rectangles are the same\nsize, we print out an appropriate message. If not, we check if the first\nrectangle is smaller than the second using the < operator 4, which results in a\ncall to the __lt__()  method.
