In this section, we’ll build a class that\nuses math operator magic methods to operate on vectors. There are a number\nof math operations that can be performed on vectors. Figur e 9-2  shows an\nexample of adding two vectors.', 'Figure 9-2 : Vector addition in Cartesian coordinates\nAdding two vectors results in a new vector whose x value is the sum of the\nx values of the two added vectors and whose y value is the sum of the y\nvalues of the two added vectors. In Figur e 9-2 , we add the vector (3, 2) and\nthe vector (1, 3) to create a vector (4, 5).\nTwo vectors are considered equal if their x values are the same and their y\nvalues are the same. A vector ’s size is computed to be the hypotenuse of the', "right triangle with one side of length x and the second side of length y . We\ncan use the Pythagorean theorem to compute the length and use the lengths\nto compare two vectors’ sizes.\nListing 9-8  is a Vector  class that illustrates the appropriate magic methods\nfor doing math and comparisons between two Vector  objects. (Each of these\nmethods has additional code using a call to isinstance()  to ensure that the\nsecond object is a Vector . These checks are included in the downloadable\nfile, but I have omitted them here to save space.)\nFile: MagicMethods/V ectors/V ector .py\n# Vector class  \n \nimport math  \n \nclass Vector():  \n    '''The Vector class represents two values as a vector, \n       allows for many math calculations'''     \n    def __init__(self, x, y):  \n        self.x = x  \n        self.y = y  \n \n  1 def __add__(self, oOther):  # called for + operator  \n        return Vector(self.x + oOther.x, self.y + oOther.y)  \n \n    def __sub__(self, oOther):  # called for - operator  \n        return Vector(self.x - oOther.x, self.y - oOther.y)  \n \n  2 def __mul__(self, oOther):  # called for * operator\n        # Special code to allow for multiplying by a vector or  \na scalar  \n        if isInstance(oOther, Vector):  # multiply two vectors  \n            return Vector((self.x * oOther.x), (self.y *  \noOther.y))  \n        elif isinstance(oOther, (int, float)):  # multiply by  \na scalar  \n            return Vector((self.x * oOther), (self.y *  \noOther))  \n        else:  \n            raise TypeError('Second value must be a vector or  \nscalar')  \n \n    def __abs__(self):  \n        return math.sqrt((self.x ** 2) + (self.y ** 2))  ", ' \n    def __eq__(self, oOther):  # called for == operator  \n        return (self.x == oOther.x) and (self.y == oOther.y)  \n \n    def __ne__(self, oOther):  # called for != operator  \n        return not (self == oOther)  # calls __eq__ method  \n \n    def __lt__(self, oOther):  # called for < operator  \n        if abs(self) < abs(oOther):  # calls __abs__ method  \n            return True  \n        else:  \n            return False  \n         \n    def __gt__(self, oOther):  # called for > operator  \n        if abs(self) > abs(oOther):  # calls __abs__ method\n            return True  \n        else:  \n            return False\nListing 9-8 : The Vector  class that implements a number of magic methods\nThis class implements arithmetic and comparison operators as magic\nmethods. Client code would use standard symbols for math and comparison\nbetween two Vector  objects. For example, the addition of vectors in Figur e\n9-2 could be handled like this:\noVector1 = Vector(3, 2)  \noVector2 = Vector(1, 3)  \noNewVector = oVector1 + oVector2  # use the + operator to add  \nvectors\nWhen the third line runs, the __add__()  method 1is called to add the two\nVector  objects, resulting in the creation of a new Vector  object. There is a\nspecial check in the __mul__()  method 2 that allows the * operator to either\nmultiply two Vector s or multiply one Vector  by a scalar value, depending\non the type of the second value.\nCreating a String Representation of Values in\nan Object', "A standard approach to debugging is to add calls to print()  to write out the\nvalues of variables at certain points in your program:\nprint('My variable is', myVariable)\nHowever , if you try to use print()  to help you debug the contents of an\nobject, the results are not particularly helpful. For example, here we create a\nVector  object and print it:\noVector = Vector(3, 4)   \nprint('My vector is', oVector)\nThis is what is printed:\n<Vector object at 0x10361b518>\nThis tells us that we have an object instantiated from the Vector  class and\nshows the memory address of that object. However , in most cases, what we\nreally want to know are the values of the instance variables in the object at\nthat moment. Luckily , we can use magic methods for that.\nThere are two magic methods that can be useful in getting information (as\nstrings) from an object:\nThe __str__()  method is used to create a string representation of an object\nthat can be read easily by humans. If client code makes a call to the str()\nbuilt-in function and passes in an object, Python will call the magic method\n__str__()  if it is present in that class.\nThe __repr__()  method is used to create an unambiguous, possibly\nmachine-readable string representation of the object. If client code makes a\ncall to the repr()  built-in function and passes in an object, Python will\nattempt to call the magic method __repr__()  in that class, if present.\nI’ll show the __str__()  method, as it’ s more generally used for simple\ndebugging. When you call the print()  function, Python calls the built-in\nstr()  function to convert each ar gument into a string. For any ar gument that\ndoes not have a __str__()  method, this function formats a string that\ncontains the type of the object, the words “object at,” and the memory\naddress, then returns the resulting string. That’ s why we see the earlier output\ncontaining the memory address.", "Instead, you can write your own version of __str__()  and have it produce\nwhatever string you want to help debug the code of your class. The general\napproach is to build a string that contains the values of any instance variables\nthat you want to see and return that string to be printed. For example, we can\nadd the following method to the Vector  class from Listing 9-8  to get\ninformation about any Vector  object:\nclass Vector():  \n    --- snipped all previous methods ---  \n    def __str__(self):  \n        return 'This vector has the value (' + str(self.x) +  \n', ' + str(self.y) + ')'\nIf you instantiate a Vector , you can then call the print()  function and\npass in a Vector  object:\noVector = Vector(10, 7)  \nprint(oVector)\nRather than just printing the memory address of the Vector object, you\nwill get a nicely formatted report of the values of the two instance variables\ncontained in the object:\nThis vector has the value (10, 7)\nThe main code in Listing 9-9  creates a few Vector  objects, does some\nvector math, and prints out the results of some Vector  calculations.\nFile: V ectors/Main_V ectors.py\n# Vector test code  \n \nfrom Vector import *  \n \nv1 = Vector(3, 4)  \nv2 = Vector(2, 2)  \nv3 = Vector(3, 4)  \n \n# These lines print Boolean or numeric values  \nprint(v1 == v2)  \nprint(v1 == v3)  \nprint(v1 < v2)   ", "print(v1 > v2)  \nprint(abs(v1))   \nprint(abs(v2))   \nprint()  \n \n# These lines print Vectors (calls the __str__() method)  \nprint('Vector 1:', v1)  \nprint('Vector 2:', v2)  \nprint('Vector 1 + Vector 2:', v1 + v2)   \nprint('Vector 1 - Each Fraction  object is\nmade up of a numerator (top part) and a denominator (bottom part). The\nclass keeps track of a fraction by storing the separate parts in instance\nvariables, along with the fraction’ s approximate decimal value. The methods\nallow the caller to get the reduced value of the fraction, print the fraction\nalong with its floating-point value, compare two fractions for equality , and\nadd two Fraction  objects.\nFile: MagicMethods/Fraction.py\n# Fraction class  \n \nimport math  \n \nclass Fraction():  \n    def __init__(self, numerator, denominator): 1 \n        if not isinstance(numerator, int):  \n            raise TypeError('Numerator', numerator, 'must be  \nan integer')  \n        if not isinstance(denominator, int):  \n            raise TypeError('Denominator', denominator, 'must  \nbe an integer')  \n        self.numerator = numerator  \n        self.denominator = denominator  \n         \n        # Use the math package to find the greatest common  \ndivisor \n        greatestCommonDivisor = math.gcd(self.numerator,  \nself.denominator)  \n        if greatestCommonDivisor > 1:  \n            self.numerator = self.numerator //  \ngreatestCommonDivisor  \n            self.denominator = self.denominator //  \ngreatestCommonDivisor  \n        self.value = self.numerator / self.denominator  \n \n        # Normalize the sign of the numerator and denominator  \n        self.numerator = int(math.copysign(1.0, self.value)) *  \nabs(self.numerator)  \n        self.denominator = abs(self.denominator)  \n \n    def getValue(self): 2 \n        return self.value  ", " \n    def __str__(self): 3\n        '''Create a string representation of the fraction'''  \n        output = '  Fraction: ' + str(self.numerator) + '/' +  \n\\ \n                 str(self.denominator) + '\\n' + \\  \n                 '  Value: ' + str(self.value) + '\\n'  \n        return output      \n     \n    def __add__(self, oOtherFraction): 4\n        ''' Add two Fraction objects'''  \n        if not isinstance(oOtherFraction, Fraction):  \n            raise TypeError('Second value in attempt to add is  \nnot a Fraction')  \n        # Use the math package to find the least common  \nmultiple  \n        newDenominator = math.lcm(self.denominator,  \noOtherFraction.denominator)  \n         \n        multiplicationFactor = newDenominator //  \nself.denominator  \n        equivalentNumerator = self.numerator *  \nmultiplicationFactor  \n      \n        otherMultiplicationFactor = newDenominator //  \noOtherFraction.denominator  \n        oOtherFractionEquivalentNumerator =  \n                oOtherFraction.numerator *  \notherMultiplicationFactor  \n \n        newNumerator = equivalentNumerator +  \noOtherFractionEquivalentNumerator  \n \n        oAddedFraction = Fraction(newNumerator,  \nnewDenominator)  \n        return oAddedFraction  \n \n    def __eq__(self, oOtherFraction): 5\n        '''Test for equality '''  \n        if not isinstance(oOtherFraction, Fraction):  \n            return False  # not comparing to a fraction  \n        if (self.numerator == oOtherFraction.numerator) and \\  \n           (self.denominator == oOtherFraction.denominator):  \n            return True  \n        else:  \n            return False", "Listing 9-10 : The Fraction  class that implements a number of magic\nmethods\nWhen you create a Fraction  object, you pass in a numerator and a\ndenominator 1, and the __init__()  method immediately calculates the\nreduced fraction and its floating-point value. At any time, client code can call\nthe getValue()  method to retrieve that value 2. False  \n \nFraction3  \n  Fraction: -1/4  \n  Value: -0.25  \n \nFraction4  \n  Fraction: -1/4  \n  Value: -0.25  \n \nAre fractions 3 and 4 equal? True  \n \nSum of 5/2 and 500/2  \n  Fraction: 5/1  \n  Value: 5.0\nSummary\nThis chapter was about the key OOP concept of polymorphism. Stated\nsimply , polymorphism is the ability for multiple classes to implement\nmethods with the same names. Each class contains specific code to do\nwhatever needs to be done for objects instantiated from that class. As a\ndemonstration program, I showed how you could create a number of\ndifferent shape classes, each of which had an __init__() , getArea() ,", 'clickedInside() , and draw()  method. The code of each version of these\nmethods was specific to the type of the shape.\nAs you saw , there are two key advantages to using polymorphism. First, it\nextends the concept of abstraction to a collection of classes, allowing the\nclient programmer to ignore the implementation. Second, it allows for a\nsystem of classes that work in similar ways, making a system predictable to\nclient programmers.\nI also discussed the idea of polymorphism in operators, explaining how the\nsame operator could do dif ferent operations with dif ferent types of data. I\nshowed how Python’ s magic methods are used to make this happen and how\nyou can build methods to implement these operators in your own classes. T o\ndemonstrate the use of arithmetic and comparison operator magic methods, I\nshowed a Vector  class and a Fraction  class. I also showed how you can use\nthe __str__()  method to help in debugging the content of an object.', '10\nINHERITANCE\nThe third tenet of OOP is inheritance,\nwhich is a mechanism for deriving a\nnew class from an existing class. Rather\nthan starting from scratch and\npotentially duplicating code, inheritance\nallows a programmer to write code for a new class\nthat extends or differentiates it from an existing class.\nLet’s begin with a real-world example that demonstrates what inheritance\nis basically about. Y ou’re attending culinary school. One of your lessons\ninvolves an exhaustive demonstration of making hambur gers. Y ou learn\neverything that there is to know about the dif ferent cuts of meat, the\ngrinding of the meat, the best types of buns, the best lettuce, tomato, and\ncondiments—just about everything you could imagine. Y ou also learn about\nthe best way to cook the hambur ger, how long to cook it, when and how\noften to flip it over , and so on.\nThe next lesson in the curriculum is about cheesebur gers. The instructor\ncould  start from scratch and go through all the material about hambur gers\nagain. But instead, they assume that you’ve retained the knowledge from\nthe previous lesson and so already know everything there is to know about\ncreating a great hambur ger. This lesson therefore focuses on what types of\ncheese to use, when to add it, how much to use, and so on.', 'The point of the story is that there is no need to “reinvent the wheel”;\ninstead, you can simply add on to what you already know .\nInheritance in Object-Oriented Programming\nInheritance in OOP is the ability to create a class that builds on ( extends ) an\nexisting class. When creating lar ge programs, you will often use classes that\nprovide very useful general capabilities. Y ou’ll sometimes want to build a\nclass that’ s similar to a class that already exists, but does some things\nslightly dif ferently . Inheritance allows you to do just that, creating a new\nclass that includes all the methods and instance variables of an existing\nclass, but adds new and dif ferent functionality .\nInheritance is an extremely powerful concept. When classes are set up\ncorrectly , using inheritance can seem  simple. However , being able to design\nclasses to use it in a clear manner is a skill that’ s difficult to master . As an\nimplementer , inheritance takes a great deal of practice to use properly and\nefficiently .\nWith inheritance, we talk about the relationship between two classes,\ntypically referred to as the base class and subclass .\nBase class\nThe class that is inherited from; it serves as a starting point\nfor the subclass.\nSubclass\nThe class that is doing the inheriting; it enhances the base\nclass.\nWhile these are the most common terms used to describe the two classes\nin Python, you may also hear them referred to in other ways, such as:\nSuper class  and subclass', 'Base class  and derived  class\nParent class  and child  class\nFigur e 10-1  is a standard diagram that shows this relationship.\nFigure 10-1 : A subclass inherits from a base class.\nA subclass inherits all of the methods and instance variables defined in a\nbase class.\nFigur e 10-2  provides a dif ferent, perhaps more accurate, way to think of\nthe relationship between the two classes.\nFigure 10-2 : A base class is incorporated into a subclass.\nAs the implementer , you can think of the base class as being incorporated\ninto the subclass. That is, the base class actually becomes part of the lar ger\nsubclass. As a client of a subclass, you think about the subclass as a single\nunit and do not need to know that the base class is there at all.\nWhen discussing inheritance, we often say there is an is a relationship\nbetween a subclass and a base class. For example, a student is a person, an\norange is a fruit, a car is a vehicle, and so on. The subclass is a specialized\nversion of the base class that inherits all the properties and behavior of the\nbase class, but also provides additional details and functionality .', 'Most importantly , a subclass extends a base class in either or both of the\nfollowing ways (which will be explained soon):\nA subclass can redefine  a method that’ s defined in the base class. That is, a\nsubclass can provide a method with same name as one in the base class but\nwith dif ferent functionality . This is called overriding  a method. When client\ncode makes a call to an overridden method, the method in the subclass is\ncalled. (However , the code of the method in the subclass can still call the\nmethod of the same name in the base class.)\nA subclass can add new methods and instance variables that do not appear\nin the base class.\nOne way to think of a subclass is with the phrase coding by differ ence.\nSince the subclass inherits all the instance variables and methods of the\nbase class, it does not need to repeat all of that code; the subclass only\nneeds to contain code that dif ferentiates it from the base class. The code of\nthe subclass therefore only contains new instance variables (and their\ninitialization), overriding methods, and/or new methods not found in the\nbase class.\nImplementing Inheritance\nThe syntax of inheritance in Python is simple and elegant. The base class\ndoes not need to know  that it is being used as a base class. Only the subclass\nneeds to indicate that it wants to inherit from a base class. Here is the\ngeneral syntax:\nclass <BaseClassName> (): \n    # BaseClass methods  \n \nclass <SubClassName> (<BaseClassName> ): \n    # SubClass methods\nIn the class  statement of the subclass, within the parentheses you specify\nthe name of the base class it should inherit from. In this case, we want the\nsubclass <SubClassName>  to inherit from the base class <BaseClassName> .\n(Programmers will often use the word subclass  as a verb, as in “Let’ s', "subclass ClassA  to build ClassB .”) Here’ s an example with real class\nnames:\nclass Widget():  \n    # Widget's methods  \n \nclass WidgetWithFrills(Widget):  \n    # WidgetWithFrills's methods\nThe Widget  class will provide general functionality . The\nWidgetWithFrills  class will include everything from the Widget  class and\ndefine any additional methods and instance variables it wants with more\nspecific capabilities.\nEmployee and Manager Example\nI’ll start with an extremely simple example to make the key concepts clear ,\nthen move on to some more practical examples.\nBase Class: Employee\nListing 10-1  defines a base class called Employee .\nFile:\nEmployeeManagerInheritance/EmployeeManagerInheritance.py\n#  Employee Manager inheritance\n#\n# Define the Employee class, which we will use as a base  \nclass \n \nclass Employee():  \n    def __init__(self, name, title, ratePerHour=None):  \n        self.name = name  \n        self.title = title  \n        if ratePerHour is not None:  \n            ratePerHour = float(ratePerHour)  \n        self.ratePerHour = ratePerHour  \n \n    def getName(self):  \n        return self.name  ", ' \n    def getTitle(self):  \n        return self.title  \n \n    def payPerYear(self):  \n        # 52 weeks * 5 days a week * 8 hours per day  \n        pay = 52 * 5 * 8 * self.ratePerHour  \n        return pay\nListing 10-1 : The Employee  class, which will be used as a base class\nThe Employee  class has the methods __init__() , getName() ,\ngetTitle() , and payPerYear() . It also has three instance variables,\nself.name , self.title , and self.ratePerHour , which are set in the\n__init__()  method. W e retrieve the name and title using getter methods.\nThese employees are paid per hour , so self.payPerYear()  does a\ncalculation to determine the annual pay based on the hourly rate.\nEverything in this class should be familiar to you; there is nothing new\nhere. Y ou can instantiate an Employee  object by itself, and it will work fine.\nSubclass: Manager\nFor the Manager  class, we consider the dif ferences between a manager and\nan employee: the manager is a salaried employee who has a number of\ndirect reports. If this manager does a good job, they get a 10 percent bonus\nfor the year . The Manager  class can extend the Employee  class, since the\nmanager is an employee but has additional capabilities and responsibilities.\nListing 10-2  shows the code of our Manager  class. It only needs to\ncontain code that is dif ferent from the Employee  class, so you’ll see that it\ndoesn’ t have a getName()  or getTitle()  method. Any calls to those\nmethods with a Manager  object will be handled by the methods in the\nEmployee  class.\nFile:\nEmployeeManagerInheritance/EmployeeManagerInheritance.py\n# Define a Manager subclass that inherits from Employee  \n \n1 class Manager(Employee):  ', "    def __init__(self, name, title, salary,  \nreportsList=None):  \n      2 self.salary = float(salary)  \n        if reportsList is None:  \n            reportsList = []  \n        self.reportsList = reportsList  \n      3 super().__init__(name, title)  \n \n  4 def getReports(self):  \n        return self.reportsList  \n \n  5 def payPerYear(self, giveBonus=False):  \n        pay = self.salary  \n        if giveBonus:  \n            pay = pay + (.10 * self.salary)  # add a bonus of  \n10% \n          6 print(self.name, 'gets a bonus for good work')  \n        return pay        \nListing 10-2 : The Manager  class, implemented as a subclass of the\nEmployee  class\nIn the class  statement 1, you can see that this class inherits from the\nEmployee  class because Employee  is inside the parentheses after the name\nManager .\nThe __init__()  method of the Employee  class expects a name, a title,\nand an optional rate per hour . A manager is a salaried employee and\nmanages a number of employees, so the __init__()  method of the Manager\nclass expects a name, a title, a salary , and a list of employees. Adhering to\nthe principle of coding by dif ference, the __init__()  method starts by\ninitializing anything the __init__()  method of the Employee  class doesn’ t\ndo. Therefore, we save the salary  and reportsList  in similarly named\ninstance variables 2.\nNext we want to call the __init__()  method of the Employee  base class\n3. Here, I am calling the built-in function super() , which asks Python to\nfigure out which class is the base class (often referred to as the super class )\nand call that class’ s __init__()  method. It also adjusts the ar guments to\ninclude self  as the first ar gument in this call. Therefore, you can think of\nthis line as translating to:", 'Employee.__init__(self, name, title)\nIn fact, coding that line this way would work perfectly well; using the\ncall to super()  is simply a much cleaner way to write the call without\nhaving to specify the name of the base class.\nThe ef fect is that the new Manager  class’ s __init__()  method initializes\nthe two instance variables ( self.salary  and self.reportsList ) that are\ndifferent from those in the Employee  class, and the Employee  class’ s\n__init__()  method initializes the self.name  and self.title  instance\nvariables that are common to any Employee  or Manager  object that is\ncreated. For a Manager  who has a salary , self.ratePerHour  is set to None .\nNOTE\nOlder versions of Python r equir ed you to write this code in yet a\nthird way , so you may see this in older pr ograms and\ndocumentation:\nsuper(Employee, self).__init__(name, salary)\nThis also does the exact same thing. However , the newer syntax\nwith the simple call to super() is much easier to r emember . Using\nsuper() also makes it less err or-prone if you decide that you want\nto change the name of your base class.\nThe Manager  class has an added getter method, getReports()  4, that\nallows client code to retrieve a list of Employee s who report to the Manager .\nThe payPerYear()  method 5 calculates and returns the Manager ’s pay .\nNotice that both the Employee  and the Manager  classes have a method\nnamed payPerYear() . If you call the payPerYear()  method using an\ninstance of Employee , the Employee  class’ s method will run and calculate\nthe pay based on the hourly rate. If you call the payPerYear()  method with\nan instance of Manager , the Manager  class’ s method will run and do a\ndifferent calculation. The payPerYear()  method in the Manager  class\noverrides  the method by the same name in the base class. Overriding a', "method in a subclass specializes the subclass to dif ferentiate it from the\nbase class. The overriding method must have the exact same name as the\nmethod that it overrides (although it may have a dif ferent list of\nparameters). In the overriding method, you can:\nCompletely replace the overridden method in the base class. W e see this in\nthe payPerYear()  method of the Manager  class.\nDo some work on its own and call the inherited or overridden method of the\nsame name in the base class. W e see this in the __init__()  method of the\nManager  class.\nThe actual content of the overriding method depends on the situation. If\nthe client makes a call to a method that does not exist in a subclass, the\nmethod call will be sent on to the base class. For example, notice that there\nis no method named getName()  in the Manager  class, but it does exist in the\nEmployee  base class. If a client calls getName()  on an instance of Manager ,\nthat call is handled by the base class, Employee .\nThe payPerYear()  method of the Manager  class contains this code:\n        if giveBonus:  \n            pay = pay + (.10 * self.salary)  # add a bonus of  \n10% \n          6 print(self.name, 'gets a bonus for good work')\nThe instance variable self.name  was defined in the Employee  class, but\nthe Manager  class has no previous mention of it. This demonstrates that\ninstance variables defined in a base class are available for use in methods of\na subclass. Here we are calculating the pay for a manager , which works\ncorrectly because payPerYear()  has access to instance variables defined\ninside its own class ( self.salary ) and instance variables defined in the\nbase class (printing using self.name  6).\nTest Code\nLet’s test our Employee  and Manager  objects and call methods of each.\nFile:\nEmployeeManagerInheritance/EmployeeManagerInheritance.py", "# Create objects  \noEmployee1 = Employee('Joe Schmoe', 'Pizza Maker', 16)  \noEmployee2 = Employee('Chris Smith', 'Cashier', 14)  \noManager = Manager('Sue Jones', 'Pizza Restaurant Manager',  \n                   55000, [oEmployee1, oEmployee2])  \n \n# Call methods of the Employee objects  \nprint('Employee name:', oEmployee1.getName())  \nprint('Employee salary:',  \n'{:,.2f}'.format(oEmployee1.payPerYear()))  \nprint('Employee name:', oEmployee2.getName())  \nprint('Employee salary:',  \n'{:,.2f}'.format(oEmployee2.payPerYear()))  \nprint() \n \n# Call methods of the Manager object  \nmanagerName = oManager.getName()  \nprint('Manager name:', managerName)  \n \n# Give the manager a bonus  \nprint('Manager salary:',  \n'{:,.2f}'.format(oManager.payPerYear(True)))  \nprint(managerName, '(' + oManager.getTitle() + ')', 'direct  \nreports:')  \nreportsList = oManager.getReports()  \nfor oEmployee in reportsList:  \n    print('   ', oEmployee.getName(),  \n          '(' + oEmployee.getTitle() + ')')\nWhen we run this code, we see the following output, as we would expect:\nEmployee name: Joe Schmoe  \nEmployee salary: 33,280.00  \nEmployee name: Chris Smith  \nEmployee salary: 29,120.00  \n \nManager name: Sue Jones  \nSue Jones gets a bonus for good work  \nManager salary: 60,500.00  \nSue Jones (Pizza Restaurant Manager) direct reports:  \n    Joe Schmoe (Pizza Maker)  \n    Chris Smith (Cashier)\nThe Client’s View of a Subclass", 'The discussion so far has been focused on the details of implementation.\nBut classes can look dif ferent depending on whether you are the developer\nof a class or are writing code to use a class. Let’ s change focus and take a\nlook at inheritance from the client’ s point of view . As far as client code is\nconcerned, a subclass has all the functionality of the base class, plus\nanything defined in the subclass itself. It may help to think about the\nresulting collection of methods as layers of paint on a wall. When a client\nlooks at the Employee  class, the client sees all the methods defined in that\nclass ( Figur e 10-3 ).\nFigure 10-3 : What a client would see looking at the interface of the Employee  class\nWhen we introduce the Manager  class that inherits from the Employee\nclass, it’ s like adding paint to touch up the places where we want to add or\nchange methods. For methods that we don’ t want to change, we just leave\nthe old layer of paint ( Figur e 10-4 ).', 'Figure 10-4 : What a client would see looking at the interface of the Manager  class\nAs the developer , we know that the Manager  class inherits from the\nEmployee  class and overrides some methods. As the client, we just see five\nmethods. The client doesn’ t need to know that some methods are\nimplemented in the Manager  class and others come from the inherited\nEmployee  class.\nReal-World Examples of Inheritance\nLet’s take a look at two real-world examples of inheritance. First, I’ll show\nyou how to build an input field that only allows you to enter numbers. I’ll\nthen build an output field that formats monetary values.\nInputNumber\nIn this first example, we’ll create an input field that allows the user to input\nonly numerical data. As a general user interface design principle, it’ s much\nbetter to restrict the input to allow only for correctly formatted data while\nthe user is entering the data, rather than allowing any input and checking its\ncorrectness later . Entering letters or other symbols in this input field, or\nattempting to enter multiple decimal points or multiple minus signs, should\nnot be allowed.\nThe pygwidgets  package contains an InputText  class that allows the\nuser to input any characters. W e’ll write an InputNumber  class to allow only', "valid numbers as input. The new InputNumber  class will inherit much of its\ncode from InputText . We’ll only need to override three methods of\nInputText : __init__() , handleEvent() , and getValue() . Listing 10-3\nshows the InputNumber  class that overrides these methods.\nFile: MoneyExamples/InputNumber .py\n# InputNumber class - allows the user to enter only numbers\n#\n# Demo of inheritance  \n \nimport pygame  \nfrom pygame.locals import *  \nimport pygwidgets  \n \nBLACK = (0, 0, 0)  \nWHITE = (255, 255, 255)  \n# Tuple of legal editing keys  \nLEGAL_KEYS_TUPLE = (pygame.K_RIGHT, pygame.K_LEFT,  \npygame.K_HOME,  \n                    pygame.K_END, pygame.K_DELETE,  \npygame.K_BACKSPACE,  \n                    pygame.K_RETURN, pygame.K_KP_ENTER)  \n# Legal keys to be typed  \nLEGAL_UNICODE_CHARS = ('0123456789.-')  \n \n#\n#  InputNumber inherits from InputText\n# \nclass InputNumber(pygwidgets.InputText):  \n \n    def __init__(self, window, loc, value='', fontName=None,  \n1 \n                 fontSize=24, width=200, textColor=BLACK,  \n                 backgroundColor=WHITE, focusColor=BLACK,  \n                 initialFocus=False, nickName=None,  \ncallback=None,  \n                 mask=None, keepFocusOnSubmit=False,  \n                 allowFloatingNumber=True,  \nallowNegativeNumber=True):  \n        self.allowFloatingNumber = allowFloatingNumber  \n        self.allowNegativeNumber = allowNegativeNumber  \n \n        # Call the __init__ method of our base class  \n        super().__init__(window, loc, value, fontName,  ", "fontSize, 2 \n                         width, textColor, backgroundColor,  \n                         focusColor, initialFocus, nickName,  \ncallback,  \n                         mask, keepFocusOnSubmit)  \n \n    # Override handleEvent so we can filter for proper keys\n    def handleEvent(self, event): 3 \n        if (event.type == pygame.KEYDOWN):   \n            # If it's not an editing or numeric key, ignore  \nit\n            # Unicode value is only present on key down  \n            allowableKey = (event.key in LEGAL_KEYS_TUPLE) or  \n                           (event.unicode in  \nLEGAL_UNICODE_CHARS))  \n            if not allowableKey:  \n                return False  \n \n            if event.unicode == '-':  # user typed a minus  \nsign\n                if not self.allowNegativeNumber:  \n                    # If no negatives, don't pass it through  \n                    return False  \n                if self.cursorPosition > 0:  \n                    return False # can't put minus sign after  \n1st char\n                if '-' in self.text:  \n                    return False  # can't enter a second  \nminus sign  \n \n            if event.unicode == '.':  \n                if not self.allowFloatingNumber:  \n                    # If no floats, don't pass the period  \nthrough \n                    return False   \n                if '.' in self.text:  \n                    return False  # can't enter a second  \nperiod \n \n        # Allow the key to go through to the base class  \n        result = super().handleEvent(event)   \n        return result  \n \n    def getValue(self): 4 \n        userString = super().getValue()  \n        try:  \n            if self.allowFloatingNumber:  ", "                returnValue = float(userString)  \n            else:  \n                returnValue = int(userString)  \n        except ValueError:  \n            raise ValueError('Entry is not a number, needs to  \nhave at least one digit.')  \n \n        return returnValue\nListing 10-3 : InputNumber  only allows the user to enter numeric data.\nThe __init__()  method allows for the same parameters as the\nInputText  base class, plus a few more 1. It adds two Booleans:\nallowFloatingNumber  to determine if the user should be allowed to enter\nfloating-point numbers and allowNegativeNumber  to determine if the user\ncan enter a number starting with a minus sign. Both default to True , so the\ndefault case allows the user to enter a floating-point number and both\npositive and negative numbers. Y ou could use these to restrict the user to,\nfor example, only entering a positive integer value by setting both to False .\nThe __init__()  method saves the values of these two additional\nparameters in instance variables, then calls the __init__()  method of the\nbase class using the call to super()  2.\nThe significant code is in the handleEvent()  method 3, which restricts\nthe allowed keys to a small subset: the numbers zero through nine, the\nminus sign, a period (decimal point), ENTER , and a few editing keys.\nWhen the user presses a key , this method is called and a KEYDOWN  or KEYUP\nevent is passed in. The code first ensures that the key pressed is in the\nrestricted set. If the user enters a key not in that set (for example, any\nletter), we return False  to indicate that nothing important has happened in\nthis widget, and that key is ignored.\nThe handleEvent()  method then does a few more checks to ensure that\nthe number being entered is legal (for example, doesn’ t have two periods,\nonly has one minus sign and, so on). Whenever a valid key press is\ndetected, the code calls the handleEvent()  method of the InputText  base\nclass to do whatever it needs to do with that key (display or edit the field).\nWhen the user presses RETURN  or ENTER , client code calls the\ngetValue()  method 4 to get the user ’s entry . The getValue()  method in", "this class calls getValue()  in the InputText  class to get the string from the\nfield, then attempts to convert that string to a number . If that conversion\nfails, it raises an exception.\nBy overriding methods, we have built a very powerful new reusable class\nthat extends the functionality of the InputText  class, without changing a\nsingle line in the base class. InputText  will continue to function as a class\nby itself, without any changes to its functionality whatsoever .\nDisplayMoney\nAs a second real-world example, we’ll create a field to display an amount\nof money . To make this general, we’ll display the amount with a chosen\ncurrency symbol, place that currency symbol to the left or the right of the\ntext (as appropriate), and format the number by adding commas between\nevery three digits, followed by a period and then two decimal digits. For\nexample, we would like to be able to display 1234.56 US dollars as\n$1,234.56.\nThe pygwidgets  package already has a DisplayText  class. W e can\ninstantiate an object from that class using the following interface:\ndef __init__(self, window, loc=(0, 0), value='',  \n             fontName=None, fontSize=18, width=None,  \nheight=None,  \n             textColor=PYGWIDGETS_BLACK,  \nbackgroundColor=None,  \n             justified='left', nickname=None):\nLet’s assume that we have some code that creates a DisplayText  object\nnamed oSomeDisplayText  using the appropriate ar guments. Any time that\nwe want to update the text in a DisplayText  object, we must call its\nsetValue()  method, like this:\n    oSomeDisplayText.setValue('1234.56')\nThe functionality of displaying a number (as a string) with a\nDisplayText  object already exists. W e want to create a new class named\nDisplayMoney  that is similar to DisplayText  but adds functionality , so\nwe’ll inherit from DisplayText .", "Our DisplayMoney  class will have an enhanced version of the\nsetValue()  method that overrides the base class’ s setValue()  method. The\nDisplayMoney  version will add the desired formatting, by adding a currency\nsymbol, adding commas, optionally truncating to two decimal digits, and so\non. At the end, the method will call the inherited setValue()  method of the\nDisplayText  base class and pass in a string version of the formatted text to\ndisplay in the window .\nWe’ll also add some additional setup parameters in the __init__()\nmethod to allow client code to:\nChoose the currency symbol (defaults to $)\nPlace the currency symbol on the left or right (defaults to left)\nShow or hide two decimal places (defaults to show)\nListing 10-4  shows the code of our new DisplayMoney  class.\nFile: MoneyExamples/DisplayMoney .py\n# DisplayMoney class - displays a number as an amount of  \nmoney\n#\n#  Demo of inheritance  \n \nimport pygwidgets  \n \nBLACK = (0, 0, 0)  \n \n#\n#  DisplayMoney class inherits from DisplayText class\n#\n1 class DisplayMoney(pygwidgets.DisplayText):  \n \n  2 def __init__(self, window, loc, value=None,  \n                 fontName=None, fontSize=24, width=150,  \nheight=None,  \n                 textColor=BLACK, backgroundColor=None,  \n                 justified='left', value=None,  \ncurrencySymbol='$',  \n                 currencySymbolOnLeft=True, showCents=True):  \n \n      3 self.currencySymbol = currencySymbol  ", "        self.currencySymbolOnLeft = currencySymbolOnLeft  \n        self.showCents = showCents  \n        if value is None:  \n            value = 0.00  \n \n        # Call the __init__ method of our base class  \n      4 super().__init__(window, loc, value,  \n                         fontName, fontSize, width, height,  \n                         textColor, backgroundColor,  \njustified)  \n \n  5 def setValue(self, money):  \n        if money == '':  \n            money = 0.00  \n \n        money = float(money)  \n \n        if self.showCents:   \n            money = '{:,.2f}'.format(money)  \n        else:   \n            money = '{:,.0f}'.format(money)  \n \n        if self.currencySymbolOnLeft:  \n            theText = self.currencySymbol + money  \n        else:  \n            theText = money + self.currencySymbol  \n \n        # Call the setValue method of our base class  \n      6 super().setValue(theText)\nListing 10-4 : DisplayMoney  displays a number formatted as a monetary\nvalue.\nIn the class definition, we explicitly inherit from\npygwidgets.DisplayText  1. The DisplayMoney  class only contains two\nmethods: __init__()  and setValue() . These two methods override the\nmethods with the same names in the base class.\nA client instantiates a DisplayMoney  object like this:\noDisplayMoney = DisplayMoney(widow, (100, 100), 1234.56)", 'With this line, the __init__()  method in DisplayMoney  2 will run and\noverride the __init__()  method in the base class. This method does some\ninitialization, including saving any client preferences for the currency\nsymbol, the side on which to show the symbol, and whether or not we\nshould show cents, all in instance variables 3. The method ends with a call\nto the __init__()  method of the base class, DisplayText  4 (which it finds\nby calling super() ), and passes on the data required by that method.\nLater , the client makes a call like this to show a value:\noDisplayMoney.setValue(12233.44)\nThe setValue()  method 5 in the DisplayMoney  class runs to create a\nversion of the amount of money formatted as a currency value. The method\nends by calling the inherited setValue()  method in the DisplayText  class\n6 to set the new text to display .\nWhen a call is made to any other method with an instance of\nDisplayMoney , the version residing in DisplayText  will run. Most\nimportantly , every time through the loop, the client code should call\noDisplayMoney.draw() , which draws the field in the window . Since\nDisplayMoney  does not have a draw()  method, that call will go to the\nDisplayText  base class, which does have a draw()  method.\nExample Usage\nFigur e 10-5  shows the output of an example program that takes advantage\nof both the InputNumber  and DisplayMoney  classes. The user enters a\nnumber into an InputNumber  field. When the user presses OK or ENTER ,\nthat value will be displayed in the two DisplayMoney  fields. The first field\nshows the number with decimal places, and the second rounds to the closest\ndollar using dif ferent initial settings.\nListing 10-5  contains the full code of the main program. Notice that the\ncode creates a single InputNumber  object and two DisplayMoney  objects.', 'Figure 10-5 : A client program where the user enters an amount into an InputNumber  field\nand the amount is displayed in two DisplayMoney  fields\nFile: MoneyExamples/Main_MoneyExample.py\n#  Money example\n#\n#  Demonstrates overriding inherited DisplayText and  \nInputText methods  \n \n# 1 - At the moment, a\nclient could instantiate a generic Shape  object, which is too generic to have\nits own getArea()  method. Further , all classes that inherit from the Shape\nclass (like Square , Circle , and Triangle ) must  implement\nclickedInside() , getArea() , and draw() . To solve both of these\nproblems, I’ll introduce the concepts of an abstract class  and an abstract\nmethod .\nabstract class\nA class that is not intended to be instantiated directly , but only\nto be used as a base class by one or more subclasses. (In\nsome other languages, an abstract class is referred to as a\nvirtual class .)\nabstract method\nA method that must  be overridden in every subclass.\nOften, a base class cannot correctly implement an abstract method\nbecause it cannot know the detailed data it should operate on, or it may not\nbe possible to implement a general algorithm. Instead, all subclasses need to\nimplement their own version of the abstract method.\nIn our shapes example, we want the Shape  class to be an abstract class so\nno client code can instantiate a Shape  object. Further , our Shape  class\nshould indicate that all its subclasses need to implement the\nclickedInside() , getArea() , and draw()  methods.\nPython does not have a keyword to designate a class or method as\nabstract. However , the Python Standard Library contains the abc module,\nshort for abstract base class , which is designed to help developers build\nabstract base classes and methods.\nLet’s take a look at what we need to do to build an abstract class with\nabstract methods. T o begin, we need to import two things from the abc\nmodule:', 'from abc import ABC, abstractmethod\nNext, we need to indicate that the class we want to act as an abstract base\nclass should inherit from the ABC class, which we do by putting ABC inside\nparentheses after the class name:\nclass <classWeWantToDesignateAsAbstract> (ABC):\nWe then must use the special decorator @abstractmethod  before any\nmethods that must be overwritten by all subclasses:\n@abstractmethod  \ndef <someMethodThatMustBeOverwritten> (self, ...):\nListing 10-10  shows how we can mark our Shape  class as an abstract base\nclass and indicate its abstract methods.\nFile: InheritedShapes/Shape.py\n# Shape class\n#\n# To be used as a base class for other classes  \n \nimport random  \nfrom abc import ABC, abstractmethod  \n \n# Set up the colors  \nRED = (255, 0, 0)  \nGREEN = (0, 255, 0)  \nBLUE = (0, 0, 255)  \n \n1 class Shape(ABC):  # identifies this as an abstract base class  \n \n  2 def __init__(self, window, shapeType, maxWidth,  \nmaxHeight):  \n        self.window = window  \n        self.shapeType = shapeType  \n        self.color = random.choice((RED, GREEN, BLUE))  \n        self.x = random.randrange(1, maxWidth - 100)  \n        self.y = random.randrange(25, maxHeight - 100)  \n \n  3 def getType(self):  ', "        return self.shapeType  \n \n  4 @abstractmethod  \n    def clickedInside(self, mousePoint):  \n        raise NotImplementedError  \n \n  5 @abstractmethod  \n    def getArea(self):  \n        raise NotImplementedError  \n \n  6 @abstractmethod  \n    def draw(self):  \n        raise NotImplementedError\nListing 10-10 : The Shape  base class that inherits fr om ABC with abstract\nmethods\nThe Shape  class inherits from the ABC class 1, telling Python to prevent\nclient code from instantiating a Shape  object directly . Any attempt to do so\nresults in the following error message:\nTypeError: Can't instantiate abstract class Shape with  \nabstract methods clickedInside, draw, getArea\nThe __init__()  2 and getType()  3 methods contain code that will be\nshared by all subclasses of Shape .\nThe clickedInside()  4, getArea()  5, and draw()  6 methods are all\npreceded by the @abstractmethod  decorator . This decorator indicates that\nthese methods must  be overwritten by all subclasses of Shape . Since these\nmethods in this abstract class will never run, the implementation here\nconsists only of raise NotImplementedError  to further emphasize that the\nmethod doesn’ t do anything.\nLet’s extend the shape demonstration program to add a new Rectangle\nclass, as shown in Listing 10-1 1. Adding a draw()\nmethod to the Rectangle  class (with appropriate code to draw the\nrectangle) fixes the error .\nHow pygwidgets Uses Inheritance\nThe pygwidgets  module uses inheritance to share common code. For\nexample, consider the two button classes we discussed in Chapter 7 :\nTextButton  and CustomButton . The TextButton  class requires a string to\nbe used as a label on the button, while the CustomButton  class requires you\nto supply your own art. That’ s because the two classes inherit from a common\nbase class, named PygWidgetsButton  (Figur e 10-7 ).\nPygWidgetsButton  is an abstract class. Client code is not supposed to\ncreate an instance of it, and attempting to do so will generate an error\nmessage.", "Figure 10-7 : The pygwidgets  TextButton  and CustomButton  classes both inherit from\nPygWidgetsButton .\nInstead, PygWidgetsButton  is subclassed by the TextButton  and\nCustomButton  classes. Those classes each provide the single method,\n__init__() , which will do whatever is needed to initialize their type of\nbutton. Each will then pass on identical ar guments to the __init__()\nmethod of the base class, PygWidgetsButton .\nThe TextButton  class is used to build a text-based button with minimal\nart. This is helpful when trying to get programs up and running quickly .\nHere is the interface for creating a TextButton  object:\ndef __init__(self, window, loc, text, width=None, height=40,  \n             textColor=PYGWIDGETS_BLACK,  \n             upColor= PYGWIDGETS_NORMAL_GRAY,  \n             overColor= PYGWIDGETS_OVER_GRAY,  \n             downColor=PYGWIDGETS_DOWN_GRAY,  \n             fontName=None, fontSize=20, soundOnClick=None,  \n             enterToActivate=False, callBack=None,  \nnickname=None)\nWhile many of the parameters default to reasonable values, the caller\nmust provide a value for text , which will appear on the button. The\n__init__()  method itself creates “surfaces” (images) for the button that are\nused in displaying a standard button. The code to create a typical\nTextButton  object looks like this:\noButton = pygwidgets.TextButton(window, (50, 50), 'Text  \nButton')\nWhen drawn, the user sees a button that looks like Figur e 10-8 .", 'Figure 10-8 : An example of a typical TextButton\nThe CustomButton  class is used to build a button using artwork the client\nsupplies. Here is the interface for creating a CustomButton :\ndef __init__(self, window, loc, up, down=None, over=None,  \n             disabled=None, soundOnClick=None,  \n             nickname=None, enterToActivate=False):\nThe key dif ference is that this version of the __init__()  method requires\nthe caller to supply a value for the up parameter (remember , a button has\nfour images: up, down, disabled, and over). Y ou can optionally also supply\ndown, over , and disabled images. For any image that is not supplied,\nCustomButton  makes a copy of the up image of the button and uses that.\nThe last line of the __init__()  methods for both the TextButton  and\nCustomButton  classes is a call to the __init__()  method of the common\nbase class, PygWidgetsButton . Both calls pass in four images for the\nbutton, along with other ar guments:\nsuper().__init__(window, loc, surfaceUp, surfaceOver,  \n                 surfaceDown, surfaceDisabled, buttonRect,  \n                 soundOnClick, nickname, enterToActivate,  \ncallBack)\nFrom the client’ s point of view , you see two completely dif ferent classes\nwith many methods (most of which are identical). But from the\nimplementer ’s point of view , you can now see how inheritance allowed us\nto override the single __init__()  method in the base class to provide client\nprogrammers with two similar , but very useful, ways of creating buttons.\nThe two classes share everything other than the __init__()  method.\nTherefore, the way that the buttons function, and the method calls that are\navailable ( handleEvent() , draw() , disable() , enable() , and so on), must\nbe identical.\nThere are a number of benefits to this kind of inheritance. First, it\nprovides consistency for both the client code and the end user: TextButton', 'and CustomButton  objects work the same way . It also makes bugs easier to\nfix—fixing a bug in a base class means you have then fixed the bug in all\nsubclasses that inherit from it. Finally , if you add functionality in the base\nclass, it is available immediately in all classes that inherit from the base\nclass.\nClass Hierarchy\nAny class can be used as a base class, even a subclass that already inherits\nfrom another base class. This kind of relationship, known as a class\nhierar chy, is depicted in Figur e 10-9 .\nFigure 10-9 : A class hierarchy\nIn this figure, class C inherits from class B, which inherits from class A.\nTherefore, class C is a subclass and class B is a base class, but class B is also\na subclass of class A. So, class B serves in both roles. In cases like this, class\nC inherits not only all the methods and instance variables in class B, but also\nall the methods and instance variables in class A. This type of hierarchy can\nbe very useful when building more and more specific classes. Class A can\nbe very general, class B more detailed, and class C even more specific.\nFigur e 10-10  provides a dif ferent way to think about the relationships in\na class hierarchy .', 'Figure 10-10 : A different way of portraying a class hierarchy\nHere, the client sees only class C, but this class is made up of all the\nmethods and instance variables defined collectively in classes C, B, and A.\nThe pygwidgets  package uses a class hierarchy for all widgets. The first\nclass in pygwidgets  is the abstract class PygWidget , which supplies basic\nfunctionality to all widgets in the package. Its code consists of methods that\nallow for showing and hiding, enabling and disabling, getting and setting\nthe location, and getting the nickname (internal name) of any widget.\nThere are other classes in pygwidgets  that are used as abstract classes,\nincluding the aforementioned PygWidgetsButton , which is the base class of\nboth TextButton  and CustomButton . Figur e 10-1 1 should help make this\nrelationship clear .\nFigure 10-1 1: Class hierarchy in pygwidgets\nAs you can see, the PygWidgetsButton  class is both a subclass of\nPygWidget  and a base class for TextButton  and CustomButton .', 'The Difficulty of Programming with\nInheritance\nWhen developing using inheritance, it can be dif ficult to understand what to\nput where. Y ou’re constantly asking yourself questions like: Should this\ninstance variable be in the base class? Is there enough common code in\nsubclasses to create a method in the base class? What are appropriate\nparameters for a method in a subclass? What are appropriate parameters and\ndefaults to be used in a base class that expects to be overridden or called\nfrom a subclass?\nAttempting to understand the interactions among all the variables and\nmethods in a hierarchy of classes can be an extremely dif ficult, tricky , and\nfrustrating task. This is especially true when reading the code of a class\nhierarchy developed by another programmer . To fully understand what’ s\ngoing on, you often have to become familiar with the code in the base\nclasses all the way up the hierarchy .\nFor example, imagine a hierarchy in which class D is a subclass of C,\nwhich is a subclass of B, which is a subclass of the base class A. In class D,\nyou may encounter code that branches based on the value of an instance\nvariable, but that variable might never be set in the code of class D. In cases\nlike this, you must look for the instance variable in the code of class C. If it\nis not found there, then you must look in the code of class B, and so on.\nWhen designing a class hierarchy , perhaps the best way to avoid this\nproblem is to only call methods and use instance variables inherited from\none layer up in the hierarchy . In our example, code in class D should only\nmake calls to methods in class C, while class C should only make calls to\nmethods in class B, and so on. This is a simplistic version of the Law of\nDemeter . Stated simply , you (meaning objects) should only talk to your\nimmediate friends (nearby objects) and never talk to strangers (distant\nobjects). A detailed discussion is beyond the scope of this book, but there\nare many references available on the internet.\nAnother approach, which we first talked about in Chapter 4 , is to take\nadvantage of composition , where an object instantiates one or more other\nobjects. The key dif ference is that inheritance is used to model an “is a”\nrelationship, whereas composition uses a “has a” relationship. For example,', 'if we wanted to have a spinbox widget (an editable text number field with\nan up and a down arrow), we could build a SpinBox  class that instantiates a\nDisplayNumber  object and two CustomButton  objects for the arrows. Each\nof these objects already knows how to handle its user interactions.\nMULTIPLE INHERITANCE\nYou’ve seen how a class can inherit from another class. In fact, Python (like some other\nprogramming languages) allows a class to inherit from more than just one class. This is\nknown as multiple inheritance . The Python syntax is for inheriting from more than one\nclass is quite straightforward:\nclass SomeClass (<BaseClass1> , <BaseClass2> , ...):\nHowever , it’s important to be aware that multiple inheritance can potentially introduce\nconflicts when the base classes you inherit from contain identically named methods\nand/or instance variables. Python does have rules (known as the method resolution\norder , or MRO ) to resolve these potential problems. I consider this an advanced topic\nand will not cover it here, but if you want to look into it, a detailed discussion can be\nfound at https://www .python.org/download/releases/2.3/mro .\nSummary\nThis was a very ambitious chapter on the topic of inheritance: the art of\n“programming by dif ference.” The basic idea of inheritance is to build a\nclass (a subclass) that incorporates all the methods and instance variables of\nanother class (a base class), thereby allowing you to reuse existing code.\nYour new subclass can choose to use or override the methods of the base\nclass, as well as define its own methods. A method in a subclass can find\nthe base class by using a call to super() .\nWe built two classes, InputNumber  and DisplayMoney  that provide highly\nreusable functionality . These classes are implemented as subclasses that use\nclasses in the pygwidgets  package as base classes.\nAny client code that uses your subclass will see an interface that\nincorporates methods defined in both the subclass and the base class. Any\nnumber of subclasses can be built using the same base class. An abstract\nclass is one that is not intended to be instantiated by client code, but rather', 'is intended only to be inherited from by subclasses. An abstract method in a\nbase class is one that must  be overridden in each subclass.\nWe worked through a number of examples to demonstrate inheritance in\nthe pygwidgets  package, including how the TextButton  and CustomButton\nclasses both inherit from a common base class, PygWidgetsButton .\nI showed how you can build a class hierarchy , where a class inherits from\nanother class, which in turn inherits from a third class, and so on.\nInheritance can be complex—reading someone else’ s code can be\nconfusing—but as we’ve seen, inheritance can be extremely powerful.', '11\nMANAGING MEMORY USED BY\nOBJECTS\nThis chapter will explain a few\nimportant concepts of Python and OOP,\nsuch as the lifetime of an object\n(including deleting an object) and class\nvariables, that didn’t fit well in the\nearlier chapters in this section. To tie all of this\ntogether, we’ll build a small game. I’ll also introduce\nslots, a memory management technique for objects.\nThis chapter should give you a better understanding\nof how your code can affect the way that memory is\nused by objects.\nObject Lifetime\nIn Chapter 2 , I defined an object as “Data, plus code that acts on that data,\nover time.” I’ve talked quite a bit about data (instance variables) and the\ncode that acts on that data (methods), but I haven’ t explained much about\nthe time aspect. That will be my focus here.', 'You already know that a program can create an object at any time. Often,\na program will create one or more objects at startup and use those objects\nthroughout its operation. However , in many cases a program will want to\ncreate an object when it needs it, but release or remove the object when it’ s\ndone using it to free up the resources that the object uses (memory , files,\nnetwork connections, and so on). Here are a few examples:\nA “transaction” object that’ s used while a customer is making an electronic\npurchase. When the purchase is completed, the object is destroyed.\nAn object to handle communication over the internet that’ s released when\nthe communication is completed.\nTransient objects in a game. The program could instantiate many copies of\nbad guys, aliens, spaceships, and so on; as the player destroys each one, the\nprogram can eliminate the underlying object.\nThe period of time from the instantiation of an object until it is destroyed\nis known as the object’ s lifetime . To understand the lifetime of an object,\nyou first need to know about a related underlying concept having to do with\nthe implementation of objects in Python (and some other OOP languages):\nthe reference count.\nReference Count\nThere are a number of dif ferent implementations of Python. The following\ndiscussion of reference counts applies to the of ficial version released by the\nPython Software Foundation—the version downloaded from python.or g—\nthat’s commonly known as CPython . Other implementations of Python may\nuse a dif ferent approach.\nPart of the philosophy of Python is that programmers should never have\nto worry about the details of managing memory . Python takes care of that\nfor you. However , having a basic idea of how Python manages memory will\nbe helpful in understanding how and when objects are released back to the\nsystem.\nWhenever a program instantiates an object from a class, Python allocates\nmemory for the storage of the instance variables defined in the class. Each\nobject also contains an extra internal field called a reference count , which', "keeps track of how many dif ferent variables refer to that object. I show how\nthis works in Listing 1 1-1.\nFile: ReferenceCount.py\n# Reference count example  \n \n1 class Square():  \n    def __init__(self, width, color):  \n        self.width = width  \n        self.color = color  \n \n# Instantiate an object\n2 oSquare1 = Square(5, 'red')  \nprint(oSquare1)  \n# Reference count of the Square object is 1  \n \n# Now set another variable to the same object\n3 oSquare2 = oSquare1  \nprint(oSquare2)  \n# Reference count of the Square object is 2\nListing 1 1-1: A simple Square  class for demonstrating r eference counting\nWe can use Python T utor ( http://pythontutor .com/ ) to step through our\ncode. W e start with a simple Square  class 1 containing a few instance\nvariables. W e then instantiate an object and assign it to the variable\noSquare1  2. Figur e 11-1 shows what we see after instantiating the first\nobject: as you can see, the variable oSquare1  refers to an instance of the\nSquare  class.", 'Figure 1 1-1: A single variable ( oSquare1 ) referring to an object\nNext, we set a second variable to refer to the same Square  object 3 and\nprint the value of the new variable. Note that the statement oSquare2 =\noSquare1  does not make a new copy of the Square  object! Figur e 11-2\nshows what we see after executing these two lines.\nFigure 1 1-2: Two variables referring to the same object', "The variables oSquare1  and oSquare2  both refer to the same Square\nobject. Y ou can also see in the top box that the two calls to print()  show\nthe same memory address. Therefore, the reference count of the object is\nnow 2. If we were to assign another variable:\noSquare3 = oSquare2  # or oSquare1\nthe reference count would be incremented to 3 (because all three variables\nwould refer to the same object), and so on.\nThe reference count of an object is important because when it reaches\nzero, Python marks the relevant memory as no longer in use by the\nprogram. This is known as being marked as garbage . Python has a garbage\ncollector that runs to reclaim any blocks of memory that have been marked\nas garbage; I’ll discuss that later in this chapter .\nThe Python Standard Library contains the getrefcount()  function,\nwhich returns the number of variables that refer to an object. Here, we use it\nto see the reference count after the first instantiation of a Square  object\nfrom the Square  class:\noSquare1 = Square(5, 'red')  \nprint('Reference count is', sys.getrefcount(oSquare1))\nThis prints a count of 2. This may be surprising—you likely expected\nthat the count would be 1. However , as the documentation of this function\nexplains, “The count returned is generally one higher than you might\nexpect, because it includes the (temporary) reference as an ar gument to\ngetrefcount() .”\nIncrementing the Reference Count\nThere are a few ways that an object’ s reference count is incremented:\n. When an additional variable is assigned to refer to the same object:\noSquare2 = oSquare1\n. When an object is passed into a function and therefore a local parameter\nvariable is set to refer to the object:", 'def myFunctionOrMethod(oLocalSquareParam):  \n    # oLocalSquareParam now refers to wherever the argument  \nrefers to\n    <body of myFunctionOrMethod>  \n \nmyFunctionOrMethod(oSquare1)  # call the function and pass in  \nthe object\n. When an object is put into a container such as a list or a dictionary:\nmyList = [oSquare1, someValue, someOtherValue]\nIf oSquare1  already refers to an object, after executing this line, the list\ncontains an additional reference to the same Square  object.\nDecrementing the Reference Count\nDecrementing the reference count also happens in a few ways. T o\ndemonstrate this, let’ s create an object and increment its reference count:\noSquare1 = Square(20, BLACK)  \noSquare2 = oSquare1  \nmyList = [oSquare1]  \nmyFunctionOrMethod(oSquare1)  # call the function and pass in  \nthe object\nWhen myFunctionOrMethod()  starts, the reference to the object is copied\ninto a local parameter variable for use inside the function. The reference\ncount of this Square  object is currently 4: two object variables, one copy\ninside a list, plus a local parameter variable inside the function. This\nreference count can be decremented:\n. When any variable that refers to an object is reassigned. For example:\noSquare2 = 5\n. Whenever a local variable that refers to an object goes out of scope. When a\nvariable is created inside of a function or method, the scope of that variable\nis limited to that function or method. When the execution of the current\nfunction or method ends, that variable literally goes away . In this example,', 'when myFunctionOrMethod()  ends, the local variable that refers to the\nobject is eliminated.\n. When an object is removed from a container like a list, tuple, or dictionary ,\nfor example with:\nmyList.pop()     \nCalling the remove()  method of a list would also decrement the reference\ncount.\n. When you use the del statement to explicitly delete a variable that refers to\nan object. This eliminates the variable and reduces the reference count of\nthe object:\ndel oSquare3  # delete the variable\n. If the reference count of the object’ s container (in this case, myList ) goes to\nzero:\ndel myList  # where myList has an element that refers to an  \nobject\nIf you have a variable that refers to an object, and you want to keep the\nvariable but lose the reference to the object, you can execute a statement\nlike the following:\noSquare1 = None\nThat keeps the variable name, but lowers the reference count of the\nobject.\nDeath Notice\nWhen the reference count of an object goes to zero, Python knows that the\nobject can be safely deleted. Right before destroying an object, Python calls\na magic method of that object named __del__()  to inform the object of its\nimpending demise.', "In any class, you can write your own version of the __del__()  method.\nIn your version, you can include any code you want your object to execute\n(ha! interesting word choice here) before the object disappears forever . For\nexample, your object may want to close a file, close a network connection,\nand so on.\nWhen an object is deleted, Python checks to see if any of its instance\nvariables refer to other objects. If so, the reference counts of those objects\nare also decremented. If this results in another object’ s reference count\ngoing to zero, then that object is deleted as well. This type of chained or\ncascading  deletion can go on as many layers deep as is necessary . Listing\n11-2 provides an example.\nFile: DeleteExample_T eacher_Student.py\n# Student class  \n \nclass Student():  \n    def __init__(self, name):  \n        self.name = name  \n        print('Creating Student object', self.name)  \n \n  1 def __del__(self):  \n        print('In the __del__ method for student:',  \nself.name)  \n \n# Teacher class  \nclass Teacher():  \n    def __init__(self):  \n       print('Creating the Teacher object')  \n     2 self.oStudent1 = Student('Joe')  \n       self.oStudent2 = Student('Sue')  \n       self.oStudent3 = Student('Chris')  \n \n  3 def __del__(self):  \n        print('In the __del__ method for Teacher')  \n \n# Instantiate the Teacher object (that creates Student  \nobjects)\n4 oTeacher = Teacher()  \n ", '# Delete the Teacher object\n5 del oTeacher\nListing 1 1-2: Classes demonstrating __del__()  methods\nHere we have two classes, Student  and Teacher . The main code\ninstantiates one Teacher  object 4, and its __init__()  method creates three\ninstances of the Student  class 2, one each for Joe, Sue, and Chris.\nTherefore, after starting up, the Teacher  object has three instance variables\nthat are Student  objects. The output from the first part is:\nCreating the Teacher object  \nCreating Student object Joe  \nCreating Student object Sue  \nCreating Student object Chris\nNext, the main code uses the del statement to delete the Teacher  object\n5. Since we wrote a __del__() method in the Teacher  class 3, that method\nof the Teacher  object  is called—which, for demonstration purposes, only\nprints a message.\nWhen the Teacher  object is deleted, Python sees that it contains three\nother objects (the three Student  objects). So, Python lowers the reference\ncount of each of those objects from 1 to 0.\nOnce this happens, the __del__()  method of the Student  objects is\ncalled 1, and each outputs a message. The memory used by all three of the\nStudent  objects is then marked as garbage. The output from the end of the\nprogram is:\nIn the __del__ method for Teacher\nIn the __del__ method for student: Joe\nIn the __del__ method for student: Sue\nIn the __del__ method for student: Chris\nBecause Python keeps track of reference counts for all objects, you\nrarely , if ever , have to worry about memory management in Python and\nrarely need to include a __del__()  method. However , you might consider\nusing a del statement to explicitly tell Python to delete objects that use a\nvery lar ge amount of memory when you are no longer using them. For', 'example, you might want to delete an object that loads a lar ge number of\nrecords from a database, or loads many images, when you are done using it.\nAlso, it is not guaranteed that Python will call the __del__()  method when\na program exits, so you should avoid putting any program-ending critical\ncode in this method.\nGarbage Collection\nWhen an object is deleted, either through the reference count going to zero\nor though the explicit use of a del statement, as the programmer you should\nconsider the object inaccessible.\nHowever , the specific implementation of the garbage collector is\ncompletely up to Python. The details of the algorithm that decides when the\nactual garbage collection code runs are not important to you as the\nprogrammer . It may run when your program instantiates an object and\nPython needs to allocate memory , or at random times, or at certain\nscheduled times. The algorithm may change from one release of Python to\nanother . Whichever it is, Python will take care of garbage collection, and\nyou needn’ t worry about the specifics.\nClass Variables\nI have talked extensively about how instance variables are defined in a class\nand how each object instantiated from a class gets its own set of all the\ninstance variables. The prefix self.  is used to identify each instance\nvariable. However , you can also create class variables  at the class level.\nclass variable\nA variable that is defined in and owned by a class. Only one\nof each class variable exists, independent of how many\ninstances of that class are created.\nYou create a class variable with an assignment statement, which by\nconvention is placed between the class  statement and the first def', 'statement, like so:\nclass MyDemoClass():  \n    myClassVariable = 0  # create a class variable and assign  \n0 to it \n \n    def __init__(self, <otherParameters> ): \n        # More code here\nBecause this class variable is owned by the class, in methods of the class\nyou would refer to it as MyDemoClass.myClassVariable . Every object\ninstantiated from a class has access to all class variables defined in the\nclass.\nThere are two typical uses for class variables: defining a constant and\ncreating a counter .\nClass V ariable Constants\nYou can create a class variable to be used as a constant, like so:\nclass MyClass():  \n    DEGREES_IN_CIRCLE = 360  # creating a class variable  \nconstant\nTo access this constant in methods of the class, you would write\nMyClass.DEGREES_IN_CIRCLE .\nAs a reminder , Python does not actually have constants. Instead, there is\na convention among Python programmers that any variable whose name\nconsists of all uppercase letters, with words separated by underscores, is\nmeant to be treated as a constant. That is, this type of variable should never\nbe reassigned.\nWe can also use class variable constants to save on resources (memory\nand time). Imagine we’re writing a game where we create many instances\nof a SpaceShip  class. W e create a picture of a spaceship and place the file in\na folder named images . Before considering class variables, the __init__()\nmethod of our SpaceShip  class would start by instantiating an Image  object\nlike this:', "class SpaceShip():  \n    def __init__(self, window, ...):  \n        self.image = pygwidgets.Image(window, (0, 0),  \n                                      'images/ship.png')\nThis technique works fine. However , coding it this way means that not\nonly does every object instantiated from the SpaceShip  class have to take\nthe time to load the image, but each object takes up all the memory needed\nto represent a copy of the same image. Instead, we can have the class load\nthe image once, and each SpaceShip  object then uses the single image kept\nin the class, like this:\nclass SpaceShip():  \n    SPACE_SHIP_IMAGE = pygame.image.load('images/ship.png')  \n    def __init__(self, window, ...):  \n        self.image = pygwidgets.Image(window, (0, 0),  \n                                      \nSpaceShip.SPACE_SHIP_IMAGE)\nAn Image  object (in pygwidgets , as used here) can use either a path to an\nimage or an already loaded image. Allowing the class to load the image\nonly once  makes startup faster and results in lower memory usage.\nClass V ariables for Counting\nA second way to use a class variable is to keep track of how many objects\nhave been instantiated from a class. Listing 1 1-3 shows an example.\nFile: ClassV ariable.py\n# Sample class  \n \nclass Sample():  \n  1 nObjects = 0  # this is a class variable of the Sample  \nclass \n    def __init__(self, name):  \n        self.name = name  \n      2 Sample.nObjects = Sample.nObjects + 1  \n \n    def howManyObjects(self):  \n      3 print('There are', Sample.nObjects, 'Sample objects')  ", " \n    def __del__(self):  \n      4 Sample.nObjects = Sample.nObjects - 1  \n \n# Instantiate 4 objects  \noSample1 = Sample('A')  \noSample2 = Sample('B')  \noSample3 = Sample('C')  \noSample4 = Sample('D')  \n \n# Delete 1 object  \ndel oSample3  \n \n# See how many we have  \noSample1.howManyObjects()\nListing 1 1-3: Using a class variable for counting objects instantiated\nfrom a class\nIn the Sample  class, nObjects  is a class variable because it is defined in\nthe class scope, typically between the class  statement and the first def\nstatement 1. It is used to count the number of Sample  objects that exist and\nis initialized to zero. All methods refer to this variable using the name\nSample.nObjects . Whenever a Sample  object is instantiated, the count is\nincremented 2. When one is deleted, the count is decremented 4. The\nhowManyObjects()  method reports the current count 3.\nThe main code creates four objects, then deletes one. When run, this\nprogram outputs:\nThere are 3 Sample objects\nPutting It All Together: Balloon Sample\nProgram\nIn this section, we’ll take a number of dif ferent concepts we’ve covered and\nput them all together in a relatively simple game—at least, simple from the\nuser’s point of view . The game will present some number of balloons in\nthree sizes that move upward in the window . The goal for the user is to pop", 'as many balloons as possible before they float of f the top of the window .\nSmall balloons are worth 30 points, medium balloons are worth 20 points,\nand lar ge balloons are worth 10 points.\nThe game could be extended to include many levels with faster -moving\nballoons, but for now , there is only a single level. The size and location of\neach balloon is chosen at random. Before each round, a Start button\nbecomes available that allows the user to play again. If you\nfind yourself in a situation where multiple Python modules require access to\nthe same constants, a good solution is to create a module of constants and\nimport that module into all modules that use the constants. Placing shared constants in a module and\nimporting that module in modules that use the constants is a simple and\neffective technique for ensuring that dif ferent parts of a program use\nconsistent values. We tell the balloon manager\nto draw , which triggers all the balloons to draw themselves. Then we draw\nthe bottom bar with its status data and the Start button.\nBalloon Manager\nThe balloon manager is responsible for keeping track of all balloons,\nincluding creating the Balloon  objects, telling each one to draw itself,\ntelling each to move, and keeping track of how many were popped and\nmissed. Listing 1 1-6 contains the code of the BalloonMgr  class.', "File: BalloonGame/BalloonMgr .py\n# BalloonMgr class  \n \nimport pygame  \nimport random  \nfrom pygame.locals import *  \nimport pygwidgets  \nfrom BalloonConstants import *  \nfrom Balloon import *  \n \n# BalloonMgr manages a list of Balloon objects  \nclass BalloonMgr():  \n  1 def __init__(self, window, maxWidth, maxHeight):  \n        self.window = window  \n        self.maxWidth = maxWidth  \n        self.maxHeight = maxHeight  \n \n  2 def start(self):  \n        self.balloonList = []  \n        self.nPopped = 0  \n        self.nMissed = 0  \n \n      3 for balloonNum in range(0, N_BALLOONS):  \n            randomBalloonClass = random.choice((BalloonSmall,  \n                                                \nBalloonMedium,  \n                                                \nBalloonLarge))  \n            oBalloon = randomBalloonClass(self.window,  \nself.maxWidth,  \n                                          self.maxHeight,  \nballoonNum)  \n            self.balloonList.append(oBalloon)  \n \n    def handleEvent(self, event):  \n      4 if event.type == MOUSEBUTTONDOWN:  \n            #
