The former is made up of multiple images, whereas the latter uses a\nsingle lar ger image that contains multiple subimages.\nThese two classes have dif ferent initializations, but the remaining\nmethods of the classes are identical. I explained the process of mer ging the\ntwo classes by building a common abstract base class.', 'I then introduced the Animation  class and the SpriteSheetAnimation\nclass in pygwidgets . I explained that these two classes only implement their\nown versions of the __init__()  method, inheriting their other methods\nfrom the common base class, PygAnimation . I concluded by showing a\ndemonstration program that provides examples of animations and sprite\nsheet animations.', '15\nSCENES\nGames and programs will often need to\npresent different scenes to the user. For\nthe purpose of this discussion, I’ll define\na scene as any window layout and\nrelated user interactions that are\nsignificantly different from any other. For example, a\ngame like Space Invaders may have a starting or\nsplash scene, a main game play scene, a high scores\nscene, and perhaps an ending or goodbye scene.\nIn this chapter I will discuss two dif ferent approaches to writing a program\nthat has multiple scenes. First, I’ll introduce the state machine technique,\nwhich works well for relatively small programs. Then I’ll show a fully\nobject-oriented approach where each scene is implemented as an object,\nunder the control of a scene manager . The latter is much more scalable for\nlarger programs.\nThe State Machine Approach\nAt the beginning of this book, we developed a software simulation of a light\nswitch. In Chapter 1 , we first implemented a light switch using procedural\ncode, and then we rewrote it using a class. In both cases, the position (or', 'state) of the switch was represented by a single Boolean variable; True\nrepresented on and False  represented of f.\nThere are many situations where a program or an object can be in one of a\nnumber of dif ferent states, and dif ferent code needs to run based on the\ncurrent state. For example, consider the series of steps involved in using an\nATM. There is a starting (greeting) state, then you need to put in your A TM\ncard; after this you’re prompted to enter your PIN, choose which action you\nwant to do, and so on. At any point, you may need to go back a step or even\nstart over . The general implementation approach is to use a state machine .\nstate machine\nA model that represents and controls the flow of execution\nthrough a series of states.\nThe implementation of a state machine consists of:\nA collection of predefined states, typically expressed as constants whose\nvalues are strings comprised of a word or short phrase that describes what\nhappens in the state\nA single variable to track the current state\nA starting state (from the set of predefined states)\nA set of clearly defined transitions between states\nA state machine can only be in one state at any given time, but can move\nto a new state, typically based on specific input from the user .\nIn Chapter 7  we discussed the GUI button classes in the pygwidgets\npackage. When rolling over and clicking a button, a user sees three dif ferent\nimages—up, over , and down—which correspond to dif ferent states of the\nbutton. The image switching is done in the handleEvent()  method (which is\ncalled whenever an event occurs). Let’ s take a closer look at how this is\nimplemented.\nThe handleEvent()  method is built as a state machine. The state is kept in\nan instance variable, self.state . Each button starts in the up state, showing\nthe “up” image. When the user moves the cursor over a button, we show the', "“over” image and the code transitions to the over state. When the user clicks\ndown on the button, we show the “down” image and the code moves into the\ndown state (internally called the armed  state). When the user releases the\nmouse button (clicks up), we again show the “over” image, and the code\ntransitions back to the over state (and handleEvent()  returns True  to\nindicate that a click has happened). If the user then moves the cursor of f the\nbutton, we show the “up” image again and transition back to the up state.\nNext, I’ll show you how we can use a state machine to represent dif ferent\nscenes that a user can encounter in a lar ger program. As a generic example,\nwe’ll have the following scenes: Splash  (starting), Play, and End. We’ll\ncreate a set of constants that represent the dif ferent states, create a variable\ncalled state , and assign it the value of the starting state:\nSTATE_SPLASH = 'splash'  \nSTATE_PLAY = 'play'  \nSTATE_END = 'end'  \nstate = STATE_SPLASH  # initialize to starting state\nIn order to perform dif ferent actions in the dif ferent states, in the\nprogram’ s main loop we use an if/elif/elif/.../else  construct that\nbranches based on the current value of the state  variable:\nwhile True:  \n    if state == STATE_SPLASH:  \n        # Do whatever you want to do in the Splash state here  \n    elif state == STATE_PLAY:  \n        # Do whatever you want to do in the Play state here  \n    elif state == STATE_END:  \n        # Do whatever you want to do in the End state here  \n    else:  \n        raise ValueError('Unknown value for state: ' + state)\nSince state  is initially set to STATE_SPLASH , only the first branch of the if\nstatement will run.\nThe idea of a state machine is that under certain circumstances, typically\ntriggered by some event, the program changes its state by assigning a\ndifferent value to the state  variable. For example, the starting Splash scene\ncould just show a game introduction with a Start button. When the user\nclicks the Start button, the game will execute an assignment statement that\nchanges the value of the state  variable to transition into the Play state:", 'state = STATE_PLAY\nOnce that line runs, only the code in the first elif  runs and completely\ndifferent code will execute—the code to show and react to the Play state.\nSimilarly , whenever and however the program reaches the ending\ncondition for the game, it will execute the following line to transition to the\nEnd state:\nstate = STATE_END\nFrom then on, every time the program goes around the while  loop, the\ncode of the second elif  branch will run.\nIn summary , a state machine has a set of states, one variable to keep track\nof which state the program is in, and a set of events that cause the program to\ntransition from one state to another . Since there is a single variable that keeps\ntrack of the state, the program can be in only one of the states at any time.\nDifferent actions that the user takes (clicking a button, pressing a key ,\ndragging an item, and so on) or other events (such as a timer running out)\ncan cause the program to transition from one state to another . Depending on\nwhich state it is in, the program may listen for dif ferent events and will\ntypically execute dif ferent code.\nA pygame Example with a State Machine\nNext, we’ll build a Rock, Paper , Scissors game that uses a state machine. The\nuser chooses rock, paper , or scissors; then the computer randomly chooses\namong the three. If the person and computer choose the same item, it’ s a tie.\nOtherwise, one point is awarded to the player or the computer , according to\nthe following rules:\nRock crushes scissors.\nScissors cuts paper .\nPaper covers rock.\nThe user will see the game as three scenes: an opening or Splash scene\n(Figur e 15-1 ), a Play scene ( Figur e 15-2 ), and a Results scene ( Figur e 15-3 ).', 'Figure 15-1 : The Rock, Paper , Scissors Splash scene\nThe Splash scene waits for the user to click the Start button.', 'Figure 15-2 : The Rock, Paper , Scissors Play scene\nThe Play scene is where the user makes a choice. After the user clicks an\nicon to indicate their choice, the computer makes a randomized choice.', 'Figure 15-3 : The Rock, Paper , Scissors Results scene\nThe Results scene shows the outcome of the round and the score. It waits\nfor the user to click Restart to play another round.\nIn this game, each value of state  corresponds to a dif ferent scene. Figur e\n15-4 is a state diagram  that shows the states and transitions (the actions or\nevents that cause the program to move from one state to another).', "Figure 15-4 : Rock, Paper , Scissors state diagram\nWhen sitting idle (waiting for the user), the current scene will typically\nremain unchanged. That is, inside the main event loop, the program will\nusually not change the value of the state  variable. (The state might change\nwhen a timer ends, but this will be rare.) This game starts in the Splash\nscene, and when the user presses the Start button, the game moves to the\nPlay scene. The play of the game then alternates between the Play and\nResults scenes. Though this is a simple example, a state diagram can be very\nuseful in understanding the flow of more complicated programs.\nListing 15-1  provides the code of the Rock, Paper , Scissors program, with\nthe boilerplate code omitted to save space.\nFile: RockPaperScissorsStateMachine/RockPaperScissors.py\n# Rock, Paper, Scissors in pygame\n# Demonstration of a state machine  \n \n--- snip ---  \n \nROCK = 'Rock'  \nPAPER = 'Paper'  \nSCISSORS = 'Scissors'  \n \n# Set constants for each of the three states  \nSTATE_SPLASH = 'Splash' 1 \nSTATE_PLAYER_CHOICE = 'PlayerChoice'  \nSTATE_SHOW_RESULTS = 'ShowResults'  \n \n# 3 - Initialize the world\n--- snip ---  ", "  \n# 4 - Load assets: image(s), sound(s), etc.\n--- snip ---\n# 5 - Initialize variables  \nplayerScore = 0  \ncomputerScore = 0  \nstate = STATE_SPLASH  2  # the starting state  \n \n# 6 - Loop forever  \nwhile True:  \n \n    # 7 - Check for and handle events  \n    for event in pygame.event.get():  \n        if event.type == pygame.QUIT:  \n            pygame.quit()   \n            sys.exit()  \n         \n        if state == STATE_SPLASH: 3 \n            if startButton.handleEvent(event):  \n                state = STATE_PLAYER_CHOICE  \n \n        elif state == STATE_PLAYER_CHOICE: 4  # let the user  \nchoose \n            playerChoice = ''  # indicates no choice yet  \n            if rockButton.handleEvent(event):  \n                playerChoice = ROCK  \n                rpsCollectionPlayer.replace(ROCK)  \n                 \n            elif paperButton.handleEvent(event):  \n                playerChoice = PAPER  \n                rpsCollectionPlayer.replace(PAPER)  \n                 \n            elif scissorButton.handleEvent(event):  \n                playerChoice = SCISSORS  \n                rpsCollectionPlayer.replace(SCISSORS)  \n \n            if playerChoice != '':  # player has made a  \nchoice, make computer choice\n                # Computer chooses from tuple of moves  \n                rps = (ROCK, PAPER, SCISSORS)  \n                computerChoice = random.choice(rps) # computer  \nchooses \n                rpsCollectionComputer.replace(computerChoice)  \n \n                # Evaluate the game  \n                if playerChoice == computerChoice:  # tie ", "                    resultsField.setValue('It is a tie!')  \n                    tieSound.play()  \n                     \n                elif playerChoice == ROCK and computerChoice  \n== SCISSORS:  \n                    resultsField.setValue('Rock breaks  \nScissors. You win!')  \n                    playerScore = playerScore + 1  \n                    winnerSound.play()  \n \n                elif playerChoice == ROCK and computerChoice  \n== PAPER:  \n                    resultsField.setValue('Rock is covered by  \nPaper. You lose.')  \n                    computerScore = computerScore + 1  \n                    loserSound.play()  \n                    \n                elif playerChoice == SCISSORS and  \ncomputerChoice == PAPER:  \n                    resultsField.setValue('Scissors cuts  \nPaper. You win!')  \n                    playerScore = playerScore + 1  \n                    winnerSound.play()  \n \n                elif playerChoice == SCISSORS and  \ncomputerChoice == ROCK:  \n                    resultsField.setValue('Scissors crushed by  \nRock. You lose.')  \n                    computerScore = computerScore + 1  \n                    loserSound.play()  \n \n                elif playerChoice == PAPER and computerChoice  \n== ROCK:  \n                    resultsField.setValue('Paper covers Rock.  \nYou win!')  \n                    playerScore = playerScore + 1  \n                    winnerSound.play()  \n \n                elif playerChoice == PAPER and computerChoice  \n== SCISSORS:  \n                    resultsField.setValue('Paper is cut by  \nScissors. You lose.')  \n                    computerScore = computerScore + 1  \n                    loserSound.play()  \n \n                # Show the player's score  \n                playerScoreCounter.setValue('Your Score: '+  \nstr(playerScore))  ", '                # Show the computer\'s score  \n                computerScoreCounter.setValue(\'Computer Score:  \n\'+ str(computerScore))  \n \n                state = STATE_SHOW_RESULTS  # change state  \n \n        elif state == STATE_SHOW_RESULTS: 5 \n            if restartButton.handleEvent(event):  \n                state = STATE_PLAYER_CHOICE  # change state  \n \n        else:  \n            raise ValueError(\'Unknown value for state:\',  \nstate) \n \n    # 8 - Do any "per frame" actions  \n    if state == STATE_PLAYER_CHOICE:  \n        messageField.setValue(\'       Rock             Paper     \nScissors\')  \n    elif state == STATE_SHOW_RESULTS:  \n        messageField.setValue(\'You                      \nComputer\')  \n \n    # 9 - Clear the window  \n    window.fill(GRAY)  \n \n    # 10 - Draw all window elements  \n    messageField.draw()  \n \n    if state == STATE_SPLASH: 6 \n        rockImage.draw()  \n        paperImage.draw()  \n        scissorsImage.draw()  \n        startButton.draw()  \n \n    # Draw player choices  \n    elif state == STATE_PLAYER_CHOICE: 7 \n        rockButton.draw()  \n        paperButton.draw()  \n        scissorButton.draw()  \n        chooseText.draw()        \n \n    # Draw the results  \n    elif state == STATE_SHOW_RESULTS: 8 \n        resultsField.draw()  \n        rpsCollectionPlayer.draw()  \n        rpsCollectionComputer.draw()  \n        playerScoreCounter.draw()  ', '        computerScoreCounter.draw()  \n        restartButton.draw()  \n \n    # 11 - Update the window  \n    pygame.display.update()  \n \n    # 12 - Slow things down a bit  \n    clock.tick(FRAMES_PER_SECOND)  # make pygame wait\nListing 15-1 : The Rock, Paper , Scissors game\nIn this listing, I have snipped the code that creates images, buttons, and\ntext fields for the Splash, Play , and Results scenes. The downloadable files\nfor the book contain the full source code and all the associated art.\nBefore the program goes into the main loop, we define all three states 1,\ninstantiate and load all the screen elements, and set the starting state 2.\nWe do dif ferent event checks depending on which state the program is in.\nIn the Splash state, we only check for clicking the Start button 3. In the Play\nstate, we check for a click on the Rock, Paper , or Scissors icon buttons 4. In\nthe Results state, we only check for a click on the Restart button 5.\nPressing a button or making a selection in one scene changes the value of\nthe state  variable and therefore moves the game into a dif ferent scene. At\nthe bottom of the main loop 6 7 8, we draw dif ferent screen elements\ndepending on which state the program is currently in.\nThis technique works well for a small number of states/scenes. However ,\nin a program with more complicated rules or one that has many scenes and/or\nstates, keeping track of what should be done where can become very\ndifficult. Instead, we can take advantage of many of the object-oriented\nprogramming techniques introduced earlier in this book and build a dif ferent\narchitecture based on independent scenes, all controlled by an object\nmanager object.\nA Scene Manager for Managing Many Scenes\nThe second approach to building a program with multiple scenes is to use a\nscene manager : an object that centralizes the handling of dif ferent scenes.\nWe’ll create a SceneMgr  class and instantiate a single oSceneMgr  object from', 'it. In the following discussion, I’ll refer to the oSceneMgr  object as the scene\nmanager , since we only instantiate one. As you’ll see, the scene manager and\nthe related scenes take advantage of encapsulation, inheritance, and\npolymorphism.\nUsing the scene manager can be a little tricky , but the resulting program\narchitecture results in a highly modular , easy-to-modify program. A program\nthat uses the scene manager will be made up of the following files:\nMain pr ogram The small main program (that you write) must first create an\ninstance of every scene identified in your program, then create an instance of\nthe scene manager , passing a list of the scenes and a frame rate. T o start your\nprogram, you call the run()  method of the scene manager . For each new\nproject you build, you must write a new main program.\nScene manager The scene manager is written for you and is available as the\nSceneMgr  class in the pyghelpers.py  file. It keeps track of all the dif ferent\nscenes, remembers which one is current, calls methods in the current scene,\nallows for switching between scenes, and handles communication between\nscenes.\nScenes Your program can have as many scenes as you want or need. Each\nscene is typically developed as a separate Python file. Each scene class must\ninherit from the prewritten Scene  base class and have a set of methods whose\nnames are predefined. The scene manager uses polymorphism to call these\nmethods in the current scene. I have provided a template ExampleScene.py\nfile to show you how to build a scene.\nThe code for the SceneMgr  class and the code for the Scene  base class live\nin the pyghelpers  package. The scene manager is an object manager object\nthat manages any number of Scene  objects.\nA Demo Program Using a Scene Manager\nAs a demonstration, we’ll build a Scene Demo program that contains three\nsimple scenes: Scene A, Scene B, and Scene C. The idea is that from any\nscene, you can click a button to get to any other scene. Figures 15-5 through\n15-7 show screenshots of the three scenes.', 'Figure 15-5 : What the user sees in Scene A\nFrom Scene A, you can get to Scene B or Scene C.\nFigure 15-6 : What the user sees in Scene B\nFrom Scene B, you can get to Scene A or Scene C.', 'Figure 15-7 : What the user sees in Scene C\nFrom Scene C, you can get to Scene A or Scene B.\nThe structure of the project folder is shown in Figur e 15-8 . Note that this\nassumes that you have already installed the pygwidgets  and pyghelpers\nmodules in the proper site-packages  folder .\nFigure 15-8 : The project folder showing the main program and the different scene files\nMain_SceneDemo.py  is the main program. Constants.py  contains a few\nconstants shared by the main program and all the scenes. SceneA.py ,\nSceneB.py , and SceneC.py  are the actual scenes, each containing a related\nscene class. SceneExample.py  is a sample file that shows what a typical\nscene file might look like. It’ s not used in this program, but you can refer to\nit to get an understanding of the basics of writing a typical scene.', 'Figur e 15-9  shows how the objects in the program relate to each other .\nFigure 15-9 : The hierarchy of objects in a project\nLet’s see how the dif ferent parts of a program using the scene manager\nwork together , starting with the main program.\nThe Main Program\nThe main program will be unique for every project. Its purpose is to initialize\nthe pygame environment, instantiate all the scenes, create an instance of the\nSceneMgr , then transfer control to the scene manager , oSceneMgr . Listing 15-\n2 presents the code of the demo main program.\nFile: SceneDemo/Main_SceneDemo.py\n# Scene Demo main program with three scenes  \n \n--- snip ---\n# 1 - Import packages  \nimport pygame  \n1 import pyghelpers  ', ' \nfrom SceneA import *  \nfrom SceneB import *  \nfrom SceneC import *  \n \n# 2 - Define constants\n2 WINDOW_WIDTH = 640  \nWINDOW_HEIGHT = 180  \nFRAMES_PER_SECOND = 30  \n \n# 3 - Initialize the world  \npygame.init()  \nwindow = pygame.display.set_mode((WINDOW_WIDTH,  \nWINDOW_HEIGHT))  \n \n# 4 - Load assets: image(s), sound(s), etc.  \n \n# 5 - Initialize variables\n# Instantiate all scenes and store them into a list\n3 scenesList = [SceneA(window),  \n              SceneB(window),  \n              SceneC(window)]  \n \n# Create the scene manager, passing in the scenes list and the  \nFPS\n4 oSceneMgr = pyghelpers.SceneMgr(scenesList, FRAMES_PER_SECOND)  \n \n# Tell the scene manager to start running\n5 oSceneMgr.run()\nListing 15-2 : A sample main pr ogram using the scene manager\nThe code of the main program is relatively short. W e start by importing\npyghelpers , then all the scenes (in this case, Scene A, Scene B, and Scene\nC) 1. We then define a few more constants, initialize pygame, and create the\nwindow 2. Next, we create an instance of each scene and store all the scenes\nin a list 3. After this line executes, we have an initialized object for each\nscene.\nWe then instantiate the scene manager object ( oSceneMgr ) 4 from the\nSceneMgr  class. When we create this object, we need to pass in two values:\nThe list of scenes, so the scene manager can be aware of all the scenes. The\nfirst scene in the list of scenes is used as the starting scene for the program.', "The frames per second (frame rate) that the program should maintain.\nFinally , we tell the scene manager to start running by calling its run()\nmethod 5. The scene manager always maintains a single scene as the current\nscene—the one that the user sees and interacts with.\nNotice that with this approach, the main program implements the\ninitialization of a typical pygame program but does not build the main loop.\nInstead, the main loop is built into the scene manager itself.\nBuilding the Scenes\nTo understand the interaction between the scene manager and any individual\nscene, I’ll explain how a typical scene is built.\nEach time through its loop, the scene manager calls a predefined set of\nmethods in the current scene that are intended to handle events, do any per -\nframe actions, and draw anything that needs to be drawn in that scene.\nTherefore, the code of each scene must be split up into these methods. The\napproach makes use of polymorphism: each scene needs to implement a\ncommon set of methods.\nMethods to Implement in Each Scene\nEach scene is implemented as a class that inherits from the Scene  base class\ndefined in the pyghelpers.py  file. Therefore, each scene must import\npyghelpers . At a minimum, a scene needs to contain an __init__()  method\nand must override the getSceneKey() , handleInputs() , and draw()\nmethods from the base class.\nEvery scene must have a unique scene key —a string used by the scene\nmanager to identify each scene. I recommend that you build a file with a\nname like Constants.py  that contains the keys for all the scenes and import\nthis file into each scene file. For example, the Constants.py  file for the\nsample program contains:\n# Scene keys (any unique values):  \nSCENE_A = 'scene A'  \nSCENE_B = 'scene B'  \nSCENE_C = 'scene C'", 'During its initialization, the scene manager calls the getSceneKey()\nmethod of each scene, which simply returns its unique scene key . The scene\nmanager then builds an internal dictionary of scene keys and scene objects.\nWhen any scene in the program wants to switch to a dif ferent scene, it will\ncall self.goToScene()  (described in the following section) and pass in the\nscene key of the tar get scene. The scene manager uses this key in the\ndictionary to find the associated scene object; it then sets the new scene\nobject as the current scene and calls its methods.\nEach scene must contain its own version of handleInputs()  to handle any\nevents that would typically be handled in the main loop and its own version\nof draw()  to draw anything the scene wants to draw in the window . If your\nscene does not override these two methods, it will not be able to respond to\nany events and will not draw anything in the window .\nLet’s take a closer look at the four methods you need to implement for\neach scene:\ndef __init__(self, window):\nEach scene should begin with its own __init__()  method. The window\nparameter is the window into which your program draws. Y ou should start\nyour method with this statement to save the window  parameter for use in\nthe draw()  method:\n    self.window = window\nAfter that, you can include any other initialization code you want or need,\nsuch as code for instantiating buttons and text fields, loading images and\nsounds, and so on.\ndef getSceneKey(self):\nThis method must be implemented in every scene that you write. Y our\nmethod must return the unique scene key associated with this scene.\ndef handleInputs(self, events, keyPressedList):', 'This method must be implemented in every scene that you write. It should do\neverything needed to deal with events or keys. The events  parameter is a list\nof events that happened since the last frame, and keyPressedList  is a list of\nBooleans representing the state of all keyboard keys ( True  means down). T o\nfind whether a particular key is up or down, you should use a constant rather\nthan an integer index. The constants representing all keys of the keyboard are\navailable in the pygame documentation\n(https://www .pygame.or g/docs/r ef/key .html ).\nYour implementation of this method should contain a for loop that loops\nthrough all events in the list that is passed in. If you want, it can also contain\ncode to implement the continuous mode of handling the keyboard, as\ndescribed in Chapter 5 .\ndef draw(self):\nThis method must be implemented in every scene that you write. It should\ndraw everything that needs to be drawn in the current scene.\nThe scene manager also calls the following methods in each scene. In the\nScene  base class, these methods each contain a simple pass  statement, so\nthey don’ t do anything. Y ou can override any or all of them to execute any\ncode you want for a particular scene:\ndef enter(self, data):\nThis method is called after the scene manager has made the transition into\nthis scene. There is a single data  parameter , with None  as a default. If data  is\nnot None , then the information it contains was sent from the previous scene\nwhen it called goToScene()  (described in the next section). The value of\ndata  can take any form—from a single string or numeric value to a list or\ndictionary to an object—as long as the leaving scene and the entering scene\nagree on the type of data being passed. The enter()  method should do\nwhatever it needs to do when this scene is about to be given control.\ndef update(self):\nThis method is called in every frame. Here, you can perform any actions you\nwould have done in step 8 of the original 12-step template introduced in', 'Chapter 5 . For example, you might want this method to move images on the\nscreen, check for collisions, and so on.\ndef leave(self):\nThis method is called by the scene manager whenever the program is about\nto transition to a dif ferent scene. It should do any cleanup that needs to be\ndone before leaving, such as writing information to a file.\nNavigating Between Scenes\nThe scene manager and the Scene  base class provide a simple way to\nnavigate between scenes. When the program wants to transition to another\nscene, the current scene should call its own goToScene()  method, which is in\nthe inherited Scene  base class, like this:\n    self.goToScene(nextSceneKey, data)   \nThe goToScene()  method communicates to the scene manager that you\nwant to transition to a dif ferent scene, whose scene key is nextSceneKey .\nYou should make all scene keys available via a file such as Constants.py . The\ndata  parameter is any optional information that you want to pass on to the\nnext scene. If no data needs to be transferred, you can eliminate this\nargument.\nTypical calls would look like this:\nself.goToScene( SOME_SCENE_KEY )  # no data to be passed\n# Or \nself.goToScene( ANOTHER_SCENE_KEY , data=someValueOrValues )  # \ngo to a scene and pass data\nThe value of data  can take any form, as long as the scenes being left and\nentered both understand the format. In response to this call, before leaving\nthe current scene, the scene manager calls that scene’ s leave()  method.\nWhen the next scene is about to be activated, the scene manager calls that\nscene’ s enter()  method and passes the value of data  to the new scene.\nQuitting the Program', "The scene manager takes care of three dif ferent ways that the user can quit\nthe currently running program:\nBy clicking the close button at the top of the window .\nBy pressing the ESCAPE  key.\nThrough any additional mechanism such as a Quit button. In this case, make\nthe following call (which is also built into the Scene  base class):\n    self.quit()  # quits the program  \nA Typical Scene\nListing 15-3  shows an example of a typical scene—this is the SceneA.py  file\nthat implements Scene A in the demo program, shown in Figur e 15-5 .\nRemember that the main loop is implemented by the scene manager . The only\ndifferences are the titles shown, the buttons drawn, and the ef fects of clicking\nthe buttons to transfer to the appropriate new scene.\nRock, Paper, Scissors Using Scenes\nLet’s build an alternate implementation of the Rock, Paper , Scissors game\nusing the scene manager . To the user , the game will work exactly the same\nway as the earlier state machine version. W e’ll build a Splash scene, a Play\nscene, and a Results scene.\nAll of the source code is available, so I won’ t go through every Python\nfile. In the Play scene, the user is presented with a\nset of images (rock, paper , and scissors) and asked to choose one. Clicking\nan image transfers control to the Results scene. in Rock, Paper , Scissors\nHere, I’ve snipped some of the game evaluation logic. The enter()\nmethod 2 is the most important method in this class. When the player makes\na choice in the previous Play scene, the program transitions to this Results', "scene. First, we extract the player ’s and computer ’s choices that were passed\nin from the Play scene as a dictionary , which looks like this:\n{'player': playerChoice, 'computer': computerChoice}\nIn the __init__()  method 1, we create ImageCollection  objects for the\nplayer and computer , each containing the rock, paper , and scissors images. W e\nupdate the player ’s or the computer ’s score and show the scores in the\nmatching text display fields.\nAfter the enter()  method runs (one time for each round), the\nhandleInputs()  method 5 is called in every frame by the scene manager .\nWhen the user clicks Restart, we call the inherited goToScene()  method to\ntransfer back to the Play scene.\nThe draw()  method 6 draws everything in the window for this scene.\nIn this scene, we don’ t do any additional work in each frame, so we don’ t\nneed to write an update()  method. When the scene manager calls update() ,\nthe inherited method in the Scene  base class runs and just executes a pass\nstatement.\nCommunication Between Scenes\nThe scene manager provides a set of methods that allow scenes to\ncommunicate with each other by sending or requesting information. This\ncommunication won’ t be needed by all programs but can be highly useful.\nThe scene manager allows any scene to:\nRequest information from another scene\nSend information to another scene\nSend information to all other scenes", 'In the following sections, I’ll call the scene the user is seeing the current\nscene. The scene that the current scene is sending information to or\nrequesting information from is the target scene. The methods used to transfer\ninformation are all implemented in the Scene  base class. Therefore, all\nscenes (which must inherit from the Scene  base class) have access to these\nmethods using self.<method> ().\nRequesting Information from a T arget Scene\nTo request information from any other scene, a scene makes a call to the\ninherited request()  method, like this:\n    self.request(targetSceneKey, requestID)\nThis call allows the current scene to ask for information from a tar get\nscene, identified by its scene key ( targetSceneKey ). The requestID\nuniquely identifies the information you are asking for . The value used for a\nrequestID  would normally be a constant defined in a file like Constants.py .\nThe call returns the requested information. A typical call would look like\nthis:\n    someData = self.request( SOME_SCENE_KEY , \nSOME_INFO_CONSTANT )\nThis ef fectively says, “Issue a request to the SOME_SCENE_KEY  scene asking\nfor information identified by SOME_INFO_CONSTANT .” The data is returned and\nassigned to the someData  variable.\nThe scene manager acts as an intermediary: it receives the call to\nrequest()  and turns it into a call to respond()  in the tar get scene. T o make a\ntarget scene able to give information, you must implement a respond()\nmethod in that scene’ s class. The method should start like this:\ndef respond(self, requestID):\nThe typical code of a respond()  method checks the value of the\nrequestID  parameter and returns the appropriate data. The data returned can\nbe formatted in any way that the current scene and the tar get scene agree on.', 'Sending Information to a T arget Scene\nTo send information to a tar get scene, the current scene makes a call to the\ninherited send()  method, like this:\n    self.send(targetSceneKey, sendID, info)\nThis call allows the current scene to send information to a tar get scene,\nidentified by its scene key ( targetSceneKey ). The sendID  uniquely identifies\nthe information you are sending. The info  parameter is the information you\nwant to send to the tar get scene.\nA typical call would look like this:\n    self.send( SOME_SCENE_KEY , SOME_INFO_CONSTANT , data)\nThis ef fectively says, “Send information to the SOME_SCENE_KEY  scene.\nThe information is identified by SOME_INFO_CONSTANT , and the information is\nin the value of the variable data .”\nThe scene manager receives the call to send()  and turns it into a call to\nreceive()  in the tar get scene. T o allow a scene to send information to\nanother scene, you must implement a receive()  method in your tar get scene\nclass, like this:\ndef receive(self, receiveID, info):\nThe receive()  method can contain an if/elif/else  construct if it needs\nto handle dif ferent values for receiveID . The transmitted information can be\nformatted in any way that the current scene and the tar get scene agree on.\nSending Information to All Scenes\nAs an additional convenience, a scene can send information to all other\nscenes using the single method sendAll() :\n    self.sendAll(sendID, info)\nThis call allows the current scene to send information to all the other\nscenes. The sendID  uniquely identifies the information you are sending. The', 'info  parameter is the information you want to send to all scenes.\nA typical call would look like this:\n    self.sendAll( SOME_INFO_CONSTANT , data)\nThis ef fectively says, “Send information to all scenes. The information is\nidentified by SOME_INFO_CONSTANT , and the information is in the value of the\nvariable data .”\nFor this to work, all scenes other than the current scene must implement\nthe receive()  method, as described in the previous section. The scene\nmanager sends the message to all scenes (other than the current scene). The\ncurrent scene may contain a receive()  method for information sent by other\nscenes.\nTesting Communications Among Scenes\nThe Scene Demo program (with Scene A, Scene B, and Scene C), discussed\nearlier with Listings 15-2 and 15-3, contains code in each scene that\ndemonstrates calls to send() , request() , and sendAll() . In addition, the\nscenes each implement simple versions of the receive()  and respond()\nmethods. In the demo program, you can send a message to another scene by\npressing A, B, or C. Pressing X sends a message to all scenes. Pressing 1, 2,\nor 3 sends a request to get data from a tar get scene. The tar get scene responds\nwith a string.\nImplementation of the Scene Manager\nHere we’ll look at how the scene manager is implemented. However , one\nimportant lesson of OOP is that the developer of client code doesn’ t need to\nunderstand the implementation of a class, only the interface. W ith respect to\nthe scene manager , you don’ t need to know how it works, only what methods\nyou must implement in your scenes, when they are called, and what methods\nyou can call. Therefore, if you’re not interested in the internals, you can go\nright to the Summary . If you are interested, this section goes through the\nimplementation details, and along the way you’ll learn an interesting\ntechnique to allow for two-way communication between objects.', 'The scene manager is implemented in a class named SceneMgr  in the\npyghelpers  module. As explained previously , in your main program, you\ncreate a single instance of the scene manager like this:\n    oSceneMgr = SceneMgr(scenesList, FRAMES_PER_SECOND)  \nThe last line of your main program needs to be:\n    oSceneMgr.run()\nListing 15-6  contains the code of the __init__()  method of the SceneMgr\nclass.\n    --- snip ---  \n    def __init__(self, scenesList, fps):  \n         \n# Build a dictionary, each entry of which is a sceneKey :  \nscene object  \n      1 self.scenesDict = {}  \n      2 for oScene in scenesList:  \n            key = oScene.getSceneKey()  \n            self.scenesDict[key] = oScene  \n \n        # The first element in the list is used as the  \nstarting scene  \n      3 self.oCurrentScene = scenesList[0]  \n        self.framesPerSecond = fps  \n \n        # Give each scene a reference back to the SceneMgr.\n        # This allows any scene to do a goToScene, request,  \nsend, \n        # or sendAll, which gets forwarded to the scene  \nmanager.  \n      4 for key, oScene in self.scenesDict.items():  \n            oScene._setRefToSceneMgr(self)\nListing 15-6 : The __init__()  method of the SceneMgr  class\nThe __init__()  method keeps track of all scenes in a dictionary 1. It\niterates through the list of scenes, asking each scene for its scene key , and\nbuilds a dictionary 2. The first scene object in the list of scenes is used as the\nstarting scene 3.', "The last part of the __init__()  method does some interesting work. The\nscene manager holds a reference to every scene, so it can send messages to\nany and every scene. But every scene also needs to be able to send messages\nto the scene manager . To allow each scene to do that, the last for loop in the\n__init__()  method calls the special method _setRefToSceneMgr()  4 that\nlives in the base class of every scene, and it passes self , which is a reference\nto the scene manager . The entire code of this method consists of a single line:\ndef _setRefToSceneMgr(self, oSceneMgr):  \n--- snip ---  \n    self.oSceneMgr = oSceneMgr  \nThis method just stores this reference back to the scene manager in an\ninstance variable, self.oSceneMgr . Each scene can use this variable to make\ncalls to the scene manager . I’ll show how scenes use this a little later in this\nsection.\nrun() Method\nFor every project you build, you have to write a small main program that\ninstantiates the scene manager . The last step in your main program is a call to\nthe run()  method of the scene manager . This is where the main loop of the\nwhole program lives. Listing 15-7  contains the code of that method.\ndef run(self):  \n \n--- snip ---  \n    clock = pygame.time.Clock()  \n \n    # 6 - Loop forever  \n    while True:  \n \n      1 keysDownList = pygame.key.get_pressed()  \n \n        # 7 - Check for and handle events  \n      2 eventsList = []  \n        for event in pygame.event.get():  \n            if (event.type == pygame.QUIT) or \\  \n                    ((event.type == pygame.KEYDOWN) and  \n                    (event.key == pygame.K_ESCAPE)):  \n                # Tell the current scene we're leaving  \n                self.oCurrentScene.leave()  ", '                pygame.quit()  \n                sys.exit()  \n \n            eventsList.append(event)  \n \n        # Here, we let the current scene process all events,\n        # do any "per frame" actions in its update method,\n        # and draw everything that needs to be drawn.  \n      3 self.oCurrentScene.handleInputs(eventsList,  \nkeysDownList)  \n      4 self.oCurrentScene.update()  \n      5 self.oCurrentScene.draw()  \n \n        # 11 - Update the window  \n      6 pygame.display.update()  \n \n        # 12 - Slow things down a bit  \n        clock.tick(self.framesPerSecond)  \nListing 15-7 : The run() method of the SceneMgr  class\nThe run()  method is the key to how the scene manager works. Remember\nthat all scenes must be polymorphic—at a minimum, each must implement a\nhandleInputs()  and a draw()  method. Each time through the loop, the\nrun()  method does the following:\nGets a list of all keyboard keys 1 (False  means up, True  means down).\nBuilds a list of events 2 that have happened since the last time through the\nloop.\nMakes calls to the polymorphic methods 3 of the current scene. The current\nscene is always kept in an instance variable named self.oCurrentScene . In\nthe call to the scene’ s handleInputs()  method, the scene manager passes in\nthe list of events that have happened and the list of keys. Each scene is\nresponsible for handling the events and for dealing with the state of the\nkeyboard.\nCalls the update()  method 4 to allow the scene to do any per -frame actions.\nThe Scene  base class implements an update()  method that just contains a\npass  statement, but a scene can override this method with any code it wants\nto execute.', 'Calls the draw()  method 5 to allow the scene to draw anything it needs to\ndraw in the window .\nAt the bottom of the loop (identical to the standard main loop without a\nscene manager), the method updates the window 6 and waits for an\nappropriate amount of time.\nMain Methods\nThe remaining methods of the SceneMgr  class implement the navigation and\ncommunication between scenes:\n_goToScene () Called to transfer to a dif ferent scene\n_request_respond () Called to query data in another scene\n_send_receive () Called to send information from one scene to another\n_sendAll_receive () Called to send information from one scene to all other\nscenes\nThe code of any scenes that you write should not call these methods\ndirectly , and they should not be overwritten. The underscore in front of their\nnames implies that these are private (internal) methods. While they are not\ncalled directly within the scene manager itself, they are called by the Scene\nbase class.\nTo explain how these methods work, I’ll start by giving an overview of the\nsteps involved when a scene wants to navigate to another scene. T o make a\ntransition to a tar get scene, the current scene calls:\nself.goToScene( SOME_SCENE_KEY )\nWhen a scene makes this call, the call goes to the goToScene()  method in\nthe inherited Scene  base class. The code of the inherited method consists of a\nsingle line:\ndef goToScene(self, nextSceneKey, data=None):  \n--- snip ---  \n    self.oSceneMgr._goToScene(nextSceneKey, data)\nThis makes a call to the private _goToScene()  method in the scene\nmanager . Within the scene manager ’s method, we need to give the current', 'scene an opportunity to do any cleanup that might be needed, then transfer\ncontrol to the new scene. Here is the code of the _ goToScene()  method of\nthe scene manager:\ndef _goToScene(self, nextSceneKey, dataForNextScene):  \n--- snip ---  \n    if nextSceneKey is None:  # meaning, exit  \n        pygame.quit()  \n        sys.exit()  \n \n    # Call the leave method of the old scene to allow it to  \nclean up.\n    # Set the new scene (based on the key) and\n    # call the enter method of the new scene.  \n  1 self.oCurrentScene.leave()  \n    pygame.key.set_repeat(0) # turn off repeating characters  \n    try:  \n      2 self.oCurrentScene = self.scenesDict[nextSceneKey]  \n    except KeyError:  \n        raise KeyError("Trying to go to scene \'" +  \nnextSceneKey +  \n                "\' but that key is not in the dictionary of  \nscenes.")  \n  3 self.oCurrentScene.enter(dataForNextScene)\nThe _goToScene()  method performs a number of steps to transition from\nthe current scene to the tar get scene. First, it calls leave()  in the current\nscene 1 so the current scene can do any necessary cleanup. Then, using the\ntarget scene key that was passed in, it finds the object for the tar get scene 2\nand sets that as the current scene. Finally , it calls enter()  for the new current\nscene 3 to allow the new current scene to do any required setup.\nFrom this point on, the run()  method of the scene manager loops and calls\nthe handleInputs() , update() , and draw()  methods of the current scene.\nThese methods will be called in the current scene until the program executes\nanother call to self.goToScene()  to transition to yet another scene or the\nuser quits the program.\nCommunication Between Scenes\nFinally , let’s discuss how one scene communicates with another scene. T o\nrequest information from another scene, a scene only needs to make a call to', 'self.request() , which lives in the Scene  base class, like this:\ndataRequested = self.request( SOME_SCENE_KEY , \nSOME_DATA_IDENTIFIER )\nThe tar get scene must have a respond()  method. That method needs to be\ndefined like this:\ndef respond(self, requestID):\nIt uses the value of requestID  to uniquely identify what data to retrieve\nand returns that data. Again, the requesting scene and the tar get scene must\nagree on the value of any identifier(s). The full process is shown in Figur e\n15-10 .\nFigure 15-10 : The communication path of one scene asking for information from another\nscene\nThe current scene cannot get information from another scene directly ,\nsince the current scene does not have a reference to any other scene. Instead,\nit uses the scene manager as an intermediary . Here is how it all works:', '. The current scene makes a call to self.request() , which lives in the\ninherited Scene  base class.\n. The Scene  base class has a reference to the scene manager in its instance\nvariable self.oSceneMgr , to allow its methods to call methods of the scene\nmanager . The self.request()  method calls the scene manager ’s\n_request_respond()  method to request information from a tar get scene.\n. The scene manager has a dictionary of all scene keys and related objects, and\nit uses the parameter that’ s passed in to find the object associated with the\ntarget scene. It then calls the respond()  method in the tar get scene.\n. The respond()  method in the tar get scene (which you must write) does\nwhatever it needs to do to generate the data that was asked for , then returns\nthe data to the scene manager .\n. The scene manager returns the data to the request()  method in the Scene\nbase class inherited by the current scene.\n. Finally , the request()  method in the Scene  base class returns the data to the\noriginal caller .\nThe same mechanism is used to implement send()  and sendAll() . The\nonly dif ference is that when sending a message to a scene or to all scenes,\nthere is no data to be returned to the original caller .\nSummary\nIn this chapter , I introduced two dif ferent ways to implement a program that\nincorporates multiple scenes. A state machine is a technique for representing\nand controlling the flow of execution through a series of states; you can use\nit to implement a program with a small number of scenes. The scene\nmanager is designed to help you build lar ger multi-scene applications by\nproviding navigation and a general way for scenes to communicate with each\nother . I also explained how the scene manager implements all this\nfunctionality .\nThe scene manager and the Scene  base class provide clear examples of the\nthree main tenets of object-oriented programming: encapsulation,\npolymorphism, and inheritance. Each scene is a good example of\nencapsulation because all the code and data of a scene is written as a class.', 'Each scene class must be polymorphic, in that it must implement a common\nset of methods in order for it to work with calls from the scene manager .\nFinally , each scene inherits from a common Scene  base class. T wo-way\ncommunication between the scene manager and the Scene  base class is\nimplemented by every scene using the inherited methods and instance\nvariables in the base class.', '16\nFULL GAME: DODGER\nIn this chapter we’ll build a full game\ncalled Dodger that uses many of the\ntechniques and concepts that have been\nexplained in this book. This is a fully\nobject-oriented extended version of a\ngame originally developed by Al Sweigart in his book\nInvent Your Own Computer Games with Python (No\nStarch, 2016; the basic game concept, graphics, and\nsounds are used by permission).\nBefore I get into the game itself, I’ll introduce a set of functions that\npresent modal dialogs that we’ll use in the game. A modal dialog is one that\nforces the user to interact with it—choosing an option, for example—before\nthey can continue using the underlying program. These dialogs stop the\nprogram from running until an option is clicked.\nModal Dialogs\nThe pyghelpers  module has two types of modal dialogs:\nYes/No dialogs  present a question and wait for the user to click one of two\nbuttons. The text of these buttons defaults to Y es and No, though you can use\nany text you like (for example, OK and Cancel). If no text is specified for the', "No button, this dialog can be used as an alert, with only a Y es (or typically ,\nOK) button.\nAnswer dialogs  present a question, a text field for the user to type in, and a\nset of buttons with text defaulting to OK and Cancel. The user can answer\nthe question and click OK or cancel (close) the dialog by clicking Cancel.\nYou present each type of dialog to the user by calling a particular function\nin the pyghelpers  module. Each dialog comes in two flavors: a simple\nTextButton -based version and a more complicated custom version. The\nsimple text version uses a default layout with two TextButton  objects that’ s\ngreat for quick prototyping. In the custom version, you can provide a\nbackground for the dialog, customize the question text, customize the answer\ntext (with an Answer dialog), and provide customized artwork for the\nbuttons.\nYes/No and Alert Dialogs\nWe’ll first look at the Y es/No dialog, starting with the text version.\nText V ersion\nHere is the interface of the textYesNoDialog()  function:\ntextYesNoDialog(theWindow, theRect, prompt,  \nyesButtonText='Yes',  \n                noButtonText='No',  \n                backgroundColor=DIALOG_BACKGROUND_COLOR,  \n                textColor=DIALOG_BLACK)\nWhen you call this function, you need to pass in the window to draw into,\na rectangle object or tuple representing the location and size of the dialog to\ncreate, and a text prompt to be shown. Y ou can also optionally specify the\ntext of the two buttons, a background color , and the color of the prompt text.\nIf not specified, the button text defaults to Y es and No.\nHere is a typical call to this function:\nreturnedValue = pyghelpers.textYesNoDialog(window,  \n                           (75, 100, 500, 150),  \n                           'Do you want fries with that?')", "This call shows the dialog in Figur e 16-1 .\nFigure 16-1 : A typical textYesNoDialog  dialog\nThe Y es and No buttons are instances of the TextButton  class in\npygwidgets . The main program stops while the dialog shows. When the user\nclicks a button, the function returns True  for Y es or False  for No. Y our code\ndoes whatever needs to be done based on the returned Boolean value; then\nthe main program continues running where it left of f.\nYou can also use this function to create a simple Alert dialog with only\none button. Here\nis a typical call:\nuserAnswer = pyghelpers.textAnswerDialog(window, (75, 100,  \n500, 200),  \n                        'What is your favorite flavor of ice  \ncream?')  \nif userAnswer is not None:  \n    # User pressed OK, do whatever you want with the variable  \nuserAnswer  \nelse: \n    # Here do whatever you want knowing that the user pressed  \nCancel\nThis will display the dialog in Figur e 16-4 .\nFigure 16-4 : A typical textAnswerDialog  dialog\nCustom V ersion\nTo implement a custom Answer dialog, you should write an intermediate\nfunction, similar to the approach shown with customYesNoDialog() . Listing 16-2  shows the code of a typical\nintermediate function.\ndef showCustomAnswerDialog(theWindow, theText):  \n    oDialogBackground = pygwidgets.Image(theWindow, (60, 80),  \n                            'images/dialog.png')  \n    oPromptDisplayText = pygwidgets.DisplayText(theWindow, (0,  \n120),  ", "                            theText, width=WINDOW_WIDTH,  \n                            justified='center', fontSize=36)  \n    oUserInputText = pygwidgets.InputText(theWindow, (225,  \n165), '',  \n                            fontSize=36, initialFocus=True)  \n    oNoButton = pygwidgets.CustomButton(theWindow, (105, 235),   \n                            'images/cancelNormal.png',  \n                            over='images/cancelOver.png',  \n                            down='images/cancelDown.png',  \n                            \nisabled='images/cancelDisabled.png')  \n    oYesButton = pygwidgets.CustomButton(theWindow, (375,  \n235),  \n                            'images/okNormal.png',  \n                            over='images/okOver.png',  \n                            down='images/okDown.png',  \n                            disabled='images/okDisabled.png')  \n    response = pyghelpers.customAnswerDialog(theWindow,  \n                            oDialogBackground,  \noPromptDisplayText,  \n                            oUserInputText,  \n                            oYesButton, oNoButton)  \n    return response\nListing 16-2 : An intermediate function to cr eate a custom Answer dialog\nYou can customize the entire appearance of the dialog: background image,\nfonts, and sizes and placement of the display and input text fields and the two\nbuttons. T o show a custom dialog, your main code would call the\nintermediate function and pass in the prompt text, like this:\nuserAnswer = showCustomAnswerDialog(window,  \n                        'What is your favorite flavor of ice  \ncream?')\nThat call displays a custom Answer dialog like the one shown in Figur e\n16-5.", 'Figure 16-5 : A typical customAnswerDialog  dialog\nIf user clicks OK, the function returns the text the user entered. If the user\nclicks the Cancel button, the function returns None .\nA demo program that demonstrates all the types of dialogs,\nDialogT ester/Main_DialogT ester.py, is available with the downloadable\nresources for the book.\nBuilding a Full Game: Dodger\nIn this section, we’ll put all the material from this part of the book together in\nthe context of a game called Dodger . From the user ’s point of view , the game\nis extremely simple: get as many points as you can by dodging the red\nBaddies and making contact with the green Goodies.\nGame Overview\nRed Baddies will drop from the top of the window , and the user must avoid\nthem. Any Baddie that makes it all the way down to the bottom of the game\narea is removed, and the user gains one point. The user moves the mouse to\ncontrol a Player icon. If the Player touches any Baddie, the game is over . A\nsmall number of green Goodies show up randomly and move horizontally ,\nand the user gets 25 points for any Goodie they touch.\nThe game has three scenes: a starting or Splash scene with instructions, a\nPlay scene where you play the game, and a High Scores scene where you can\nview the top 10 high scores. If you score within the top 10, you’re given the', 'option of entering your name and score into the high scores table. Figur e 16-\n6 shows the three scenes.\nFigure 16-6 : The Splash, Play , and High Score scenes (left to right)\nImplementation\nThe contents of the Dodger  project folder are as follows (filenames are in\nitalic):\n__init__.py  Empty file that indicates that this is a Python package\nBaddies.py  Contains the Baddie  and BaddieMgr  classes\nConstants.py  Contains constants used by multiple scenes\nGoodies.py  Contains the Goodie  and GoodieMgr  classes\nHighScoresData.py  Contains the HighScoresData  class\nimages  Folder that contains all the artwork for the game\nMain_Dodger .py The main program\nPlayer .py Contains the Player  class\nSceneHighScores.py  The scene that shows and records high scores\nScenePlay .py The main Play scene', "Scene.Splash.py  The Splash scene\nsounds  Folder that contains all the sound files for the game\nThe project folder is included with the book’ s resources. I won’ t talk\nthrough the entirety of the code, but I’ll go through the source files and\nexplain how the key parts work.\nFile: Dodger/Constants.py\nThis file contains constants that can be used by more than one source file.\nThe most important constants are the scene keys:\n# Scene keys  \nSCENE_SPLASH = 'scene splash'  \nSCENE_PLAY = 'scene play'  \nSCENE_HIGH_SCORES = 'scene high scores'\nThe values of these constants are unique strings that identify the dif ferent\nscenes.\nFile: Main_Dodger .py\nThe main file performs the necessary initialization, then passes control to the\nscene manager . The most important code in the file is this:\n# Instantiate all scenes and store them in a list  \nscenesList = [SceneSplash(window)  \n              SceneHighScores(window)  \n              ScenePlay(window)]  \n \n# Create the scene manager, passing in the scenes list and the  \nFPS \noSceneMgr = pyghelpers.SceneMgr(scenesList, FRAMES_PER_SECOND)  \n \n# Tell the scene manager to start running  \noSceneMgr.run()\nHere we create an instance of each scene, instantiate the scene manager ,\nthen turn over control to the scene manager . The scene manager ’s run()\nmethod gives control to the first scene in the list. In this game, it gives\ncontrol to the Splash scene.", 'As discussed in the previous chapter , each scene class inherits from the\nScene  base class. In addition to providing its own __init__()  method, each\nof these classes is required to override the getSceneKey() , handleInputs() ,\nand draw()  methods from the base class.\nFile: Dodger/SceneSplash.py\nThe Splash scene shows the user a graphic with the rules of the game and\nthree buttons: Start, Quit, and Go to High Scores. The code for this scene’ s\nclass only contains the required methods; all other methods default to the\nones in the Scene  base class.\nThe __init__()  method creates an Image  object for the background image\nand three CustomButton  objects for the user ’s options.\nThe getSceneKey()  method must be implemented in all scenes; it just\nreturns a unique key for the scene.\nThe handleInputs()  method checks for the user clicking any of the\nbuttons. If the user clicks Start, we call the inherited self.goToScene()\nmethod to ask the scene manager to transfer control to the Play scene.\nSimilarly , clicking the Go to High Scores button takes the user to the High\nScores scene. If the user clicks Quit, we call the scene’ s inherited\nself.quit()  method, which quits the program.\nIn the draw()  method, the program draws the background and all three\nbuttons.\nFile: Dodger/ScenePlay .py\nThe Play scene manages the actual playing of the game: the user moving the\nPlayer icon, generation and movement of the Baddies and Goodies, and\ncollision detection. It also manages the display elements at the bottom of the\nwindow , including the current game score and high score, and responds to\nclicks on the Quit, Go to High Scores, and Start buttons and the Background\nMusic checkbox.\nThere is quite a bit of code for the Play scene, so I’ll break it up into\nsmaller chunks (Listings 16-3 through 16-7) to explain the methods. The\nscene adheres to the design rules established in Chapter 15  by implementing\nthe __init__() , handleInputs() , update() , and draw()  methods. It also', "implements an enter()  method to handle what the scene should do when it\nbecomes the active scene and a leave()  method for what the scene should\ndo when the user navigates away . Finally , it has a reset()  method for\nresetting the state before starting a new round. class\nWhen run, the main code of the game instantiates all the scenes. In the\nPlay scene, the __init__()  method creates all the buttons and text display\nfields for the bottom of the window 1, then loads the sounds. V ery\nimportantly , we use composition, discussed in Chapters 4  and 10, to create a\nPlayer object ( oPlayer ), a Baddie manager object ( oBaddieMgr ), and a\nGoodie manager object ( oGoodieMgr ) 2. A round of the game starts\nwhen the user presses New Game.\nAll scenes must have a getSceneKey()  method 4 that returns a string\nrepresenting the current scene. Listing 16-4  shows the code that retrieves the\nscores and resets the game upon request.\n  1 def enter(self, data):  \n        self.getHiAndLowScores()  \n \n  2 def getHiAndLowScores(self):  \n        # Ask the High Scores scene for a dict of scores \n        # that looks like this:\n        #  {'highest': highestScore, 'lowest': lowestScore}  \n      3 infoDict = self.request(SCENE_HIGH_SCORES,  ", "HIGH_SCORES_DATA)  \n        self.highestHighScore = infoDict['highest']  \n        self.highScoreText.setValue(self.highestHighScore)  \n        self.lowestHighScore = infoDict['lowest']  \n \n  4 def reset(self):  # start a new game  \n        self.score = 0  \n        self.scoreText.setValue(self.score)  \n        self.getHiAndLowScores()  \n \n        # Tell the managers to reset themselves  \n      5 self.oBaddieMgr.reset()  \n        self.oGoodieMgr.reset()  \n \n        if self.backgroundMusic:  \n            pygame.mixer.music.play(-1, 0.0)  \n      6 self.startButton.disable()  \n        self.highScoresButton.disable()  \n        self.soundCheckBox.disable()  \n        self.quitButton.disable()  \n        pygame.mouse.set_visible(False)\nListing 16-4 : The enter() , getHiAndLowScores() , and reset()  methods\nof the ScenePlay  class\nWhen navigating to the Play scene, the scene manager calls enter()  1,\nwhich in turn calls the getHiAndLowScores()  method 2. That method issues\na request to the High Scores scene 3 to retrieve the highest and lowest scores\nfrom the high scores table, so we can draw the highest score from that table\nin the bar at the bottom of the window . At the end of each game, it compares\nthe game’ s score to the lowest top 10 score to see if this game ranks in the\ntop 10.\nWhen the user clicks the New Game button, the reset()  method 4 is\ncalled to reinitialize everything that needs to be reset before starting a new\nround of the game. The reset()  method tells the Baddie manager and the\nGoodie manager to reinitialize themselves by calling their own reset()\nmethods 5, disables the buttons at the bottom of the screen so they cannot be\npressed during game play 6, and hides the pointer cursor . During play , the\nuser moves the mouse to control the Player icon in the window .\nThe code in Listing 16-5  deals with user input.", '  1 def handleInputs(self, eventsList, keyPressedList):  \n      2 if self.playingState == STATE_PLAYING:  \n            return  # ignore button events while playing  \n \n        for event in eventsList:  \n          3 if self.startButton.handleEvent(event):  \n                self.reset()  \n                self.playingState = STATE_PLAYING  \n \n          4 if self.highScoresButton.handleEvent(event):  \n                self.goToScene(SCENE_HIGH_SCORES)  \n \n          5 if self.soundCheckBox.handleEvent(event):  \n                self.backgroundMusic =  \nself.soundCheckBox.getValue()  \n \n          6 if self.quitButton.handleEvent(event):  \n                self.quit()\nListing 16-5 : The handleInputs()  method of the ScenePlay  class\nThe handleInputs()  method 1 is responsible for click events. If the state\nmachine is in the playing state, the user cannot click the buttons, so we don’ t\nbother checking for events 2. If the user presses New Game 3, we call\nreset()  to reinitialize variables and change the state machine to the playing\nstate. Listing 16-6  shows the code for the\nactual game play .\n  1 def update(self):  \n        if self.playingState != STATE_PLAYING:  \n            return  # only update when playing  \n \n        # Move the Player to the mouse position, get back its  \nrect \n      2 mouseX, mouseY = pygame.mouse.get_pos()  \n        playerRect = self.oPlayer.update(mouseX, mouseY)  ', " \n        # Tell the GoodieMgr to move all Goodies\n        # Returns the number of Goodies that the Player  \ncontacted  \n      3 nGoodiesHit = self.oGoodieMgr.update(playerRect)  \n        if nGoodiesHit > 0:  \n            self.dingSound.play()  \n            self.score = self.score + (nGoodiesHit *  \nPOINTS_FOR_GOODIE)  \n \n        # Tell the BaddieMgr to move all the Baddies\n        # Returns the number of Baddies that fell off the  \nbottom \n      4 nBaddiesEvaded = self.oBaddieMgr.update()  \n        self.score = self.score + (nBaddiesEvaded *  \nPOINTS_FOR_BADDIE_EVADED)  \n        self.scoreText.setValue(self.score)  \n \n        # Check if the Player has hit any Baddie  \n      5 if self.oBaddieMgr.hasPlayerHitBaddie(playerRect):  \n            pygame.mouse.set_visible(True)  \n            pygame.mixer.music.stop()  \n \n            self.gameOverSound.play()  \n            self.playingState = STATE_GAME_OVER  \n          6 self.draw()  # force drawing of game over message  \n \n          7 if self.score > self.lowestHighScore:  \n                scoreAsString = ′Your score: ′ +  \nstr(self.score) + ′\\n′  \n                if self.score > self.highestHighScore:  \n                    dialogText = (scoreString +  \n                                  'is a new high score,  \nCONGRATULATIONS!')  \n                else:  \n                    dialogText = (scoreString +  \n                                  'gets you on the high scores  \nlist.') \n \n                result = showCustomYesNoDialog(self.window,  \ndialogText)  \n                if result:   # navigate  \n                    self.goToScene(SCENE_HIGH_SCORES,  \nself.score)   \n \n            self.startButton.enable()  \n            self.highScoresButton.enable()  ", '            self.soundCheckBox.enable()  \n            self.quitButton.enable()\nListing 16-6 : The update()  method of the ScenePlay  class\nThe scene manager calls the update()  method of the ScenePlay  class 1 in\nevery frame. This method handles everything that happens while the game is\nbeing played. First, it tells the Player object to move the Player ’s icon to the\nposition of the mouse. Then it calls the Player ’s update()  method 2, which\nreturns the current rectangle of the icon in the window . We use this to see if\nthe Player ’s icon has contacted any Goodies or Baddies.\nNext, it calls the Goodie manager ’s update()  method 3 to move all the\nGoodies. This method returns the number of Goodies that the Player has\ncontacted, which we use to increase the score.\nThis is followed by a call to the Baddie manager ’s update()  method 4 to\nmove all the Baddies. That method returns the number of Baddies that have\nfallen of f the bottom of the game area.\nWe then check to see if the Player has contacted any Baddies 5. If so, the\ngame is over and we display a Game Over graphic. W e also make a special\ncall to our draw()  method 6 because we may put up a dialog for the user , and\nthe game’ s main loop will not draw the Game Over graphic until the user\nclicks one of the buttons in the dialog.\nFinally , when the game ends, if the current game score is higher than the\ntenth-best score 7, we put up a dialog giving the user the option of recording\ntheir score into the high scores list. If the score of the current game is a new\nall-time high score, we give a special message in the dialog.\nThe code in Listing 16-7  draws the game characters.\n  1 def draw(self):  \n        self.window.fill(BLACK)  \n     \n        # Tell the managers to draw all the Baddies and  \nGoodies \n        self.oBaddieMgr.draw()  \n        self.oGoodieMgr.draw()  \n     \n        # Tell the Player to draw itself  \n        self.oPlayer.draw()  ', '     \n        # Draw all the info at the bottom of the window  \n      2 self.controlsBackground.draw()  \n        self.titleText.draw()  \n        self.scoreText.draw()  \n        self.highScoreText.draw()  \n        self.soundCheckBox.draw()  \n        self.quitButton.draw()  \n        self.highScoresButton.draw()  \n        self.startButton.draw()  \n \n      3 if self.playingState == STATE_GAME_OVER:  \n            self.gameOverImage.draw()  \n \n  4 def leave(self):  \n        pygame.mixer.music.stop()\nListing 16-7 : The draw()  and leave()  methods of the ScenePlay  class\nThe draw()  method tells the Player to draw itself and the Goodie and\nBaddie managers to draw all the Goodies and Baddies 1. We then draw the\nbottom portion of the window 2 with all the buttons and text display fields. If\nwe are in the game over state 3, we draw the Game Over image.\nWhen the user navigates away from this scene, the scene manager calls the\nleave()  method 4 and we stop any music.\nFile: Dodger/Baddies.py\nThe Baddies.py  file contains two classes: Baddie  and BaddieMgr . The Play\nscene creates the single Baddie manager object, which creates and maintains\na list of all Baddies. The Baddie manager instantiates objects from the\nBaddie  class every few frames, based on a timer . Listing 16-8  contains the\ncode for the Baddie  class.\n# Baddie class\n--- snip imports ---  \n \nclass Baddie():  \n    MIN_SIZE = 10  \n    MAX_SIZE = 40  \n    MIN_SPEED = 1  \n    MAX_SPEED = 8  ', "    # Load the image only once\n    1 BADDIE_IMAGE = pygame.image.load('images/baddie.png')  \n \n    def __init__(self, window):  \n        self.window = window  \n        # Create the image object  \n        size = random.randrange(Baddie.MIN_SIZE,  \nBaddie.MAX_SIZE + 1)  \n        self.x = random.randrange(0, WINDOW_WIDTH - size)  \n        self.y = 0 - size  # start above the window  \n      2 self.image = pygwidgets.Image(self.window, (self.x,  \nself.y),  \n                                      Baddie.BADDIE_IMAGE)  \n \n        # Scale it  \n        percent = (size * 100) / Baddie.MAX_SIZE  \n        self.image.scale(percent, False)  \n        self.speed = random.randrange(Baddie.MIN_SPEED,  \n                                      Baddie.MAX_SPEED + 1)  \n \n  3 def update(self):  # move the Baddie down  \n        self.y = self.y + self.speed  \n        self.image.setLoc((self.x, self.y))  \n        if self.y > GAME_HEIGHT:  \n            return True  # needs to be deleted  \n        else:  \n            return False  # stays in the window  \n \n  4 def draw(self):  \n        self.image.draw()  \n \n  5 def collide(self, playerRect):  \n        collidedWithPlayer = self.image.overlaps(playerRect)  \n        return collidedWithPlayer\nListing 16-8 : The Baddie  class\nWe load the image of the Baddie as a class variable 1 so the single image\nis shared by all Baddies.\nThe __init__()  method 2 chooses a random size for each new Baddie, so\nthe user sees dif ferently sized Baddies. It chooses a random x-coordinate and\na y-coordinate that will place the image just above the window . It then", "creates an Image  object and scales the image down to the selected size 2.\nLastly , it chooses a random speed.\nThe Baddie manager , the code for which I’ll show in a moment, calls the\nupdate()  method 3 in every frame: the code here moves the location of the\nBaddie down by the number of pixels that represents its speed. If the Baddie\nhas moved of f the bottom of the game area, we return True  to say this Baddie\nis ready to be removed. Otherwise, we return False  to tell the Baddie\nmanager to leave this Baddie in the window .\nThe draw()  method 4 draws the Baddie at its new location.\nThe collide()  method 5 checks to see if the Player and the Baddie\nintersect.\nThe BaddieMgr  class, shown in Listing 16-9 , creates and manages a list of\nBaddie  objects; this is a classic example of an object manager object.\n# BaddieMgr class  \nclass BaddieMgr():  \n    ADD_NEW_BADDIE_RATE = 8  # how often to add a new Baddie  \n \n  1 def __init__(self, window):  \n        self.window = window  \n        self.reset()  \n \n  2 def reset(self):  # called when starting a new game  \n        self.baddiesList = []  \n        self.nFramesTilNextBaddie =  \nBaddieMgr.ADD_NEW_BADDIE_RATE  \n \n  3 def update(self):  \n        # Tell each Baddie to update itself\n        # Count how many Baddies have fallen off the bottom  \n        nBaddiesRemoved = 0  \n      4 baddiesListCopy = self.baddiesList.copy()  \n        for oBaddie in baddiesListCopy:  \n          5 deleteMe = oBaddie.update()  \n            if deleteMe:  \n                self.baddiesList.remove(oBaddie)  \n                nBaddiesRemoved = nBaddiesRemoved + 1  \n \n        # Check if it's time to add a new Baddie  \n      6 self.nFramesTilNextBaddie = self.nFramesTilNextBaddie  ", '- 1 \n        if self.nFramesTilNextBaddie == 0:  \n            oBaddie = Baddie(self.window)  \n            self.baddiesList.append(oBaddie)  \n            self.nFramesTilNextBaddie =  \nBaddieMgr.ADD_NEW_BADDIE_RATE  \n \n        # Return the count of Baddies that were removed  \n        return nBaddiesRemoved  \n \n  7 def draw(self):  \n        for oBaddie in self.baddiesList:  \n            oBaddie.draw()  \n \n  8 def hasPlayerHitBaddie(self, playerRect):  \n        for oBaddie in self.baddiesList:  \n            if oBaddie.collide(playerRect):  \n                return True  \n        return False\nListing 16-9 : The BaddieMgr class\nThe __init__()  method calls 1 the BaddieMgr ’s own reset()  method to\nset the list of Baddie  objects to the empty list. W e use the frame-counting\napproach to create a new Baddie relatively often, to keep things interesting.\nWe use the instance variable self.nFramesTilNextBaddie  for counting\nframes.\nThe reset()  method 2 is called when starting a new round of the game. It\nclears the list of Baddies and resets the frame counter .\nThe update()  method 3 is where the real management of Baddies\nhappens. Our intent here is to loop through all the Baddies, telling each one\nto update its own position and removing any that have fallen of f the bottom\nof the window . However , there is a potential bug. If you simply iterate\nthrough a list and remove an element that matches your criteria for deletion,\nthe list is immediately compacted. When this happens, the element directly\nfollowing the one that was deleted will be skipped; in this loop, that element\nwill not be told to update itself. Though I didn’ t go into detail at the time, we\nencountered the same problem in Chapter 1 1 in the Balloon game, where we\nneeded to eliminate balloons that floated of f the top of the window . There I', 'employed a solution using the reversed()  function applied to the list, to\niterate in the reverse order (see Listing 1 1-6).\nHere I’ve implemented a more generalized solution 4. The approach used\nin the BaddieMgr  class is to make a copy of the list and iterate over the\ncopied  list; then, if we find an element that meets the criteria for deletion (in\nthis case a Baddie that has fallen of f the bottom of the window), we remove\nthat element (that specific Baddie) from the original  list. W ith this approach,\nwe are iterating over a dif ferent list than the one from which we are\nremoving elements.\nAs we iterate through the Baddies, the call to the update()  method of each\nBaddie 5 returns a Boolean: False  to indicate it’ s still moving down the\nwindow or True  to indicate that it has fallen of f the bottom. W e count the\nnumber of Baddies that fall of f the bottom and remove each one from the list.\nAt the end of the method, we return the count to the main code so it can\nupdate the score.\nIn every frame, we also check to see if it’ s time to create a new Baddie 6.\nWhen we’ve gone through the constant ADD_NEW_BADDIE_RATE  number of\nframes, we create a new Baddie  object and add it to the list of Baddies.\nThe draw()  method 7 iterates through the list of Baddies and calls the\ndraw()  method of each Baddie to draw itself at its appropriate location.\nFinally , the hasPlayerHitBaddie()  method 8 checks to see if the Player ’s\nrectangle intersects any Baddie. The code iterates through the list of Baddies\nand calls the collide()  method of each. If there was an intersection\n(overlap) with any Baddie, then we report that back to the main code, which\nends the game.\nFile: Dodger/Goodies.py\nThe GoodieMgr  and Goodie  classes are very similar to the BaddieMgr  and\nBaddie  classes. The Goodie manager is an object manager object that\nmaintains a list of Goodies. The dif ference from the Baddie manager is that it\nwill randomly place a Goodie at either the left edge of the window (in which\ncase it moves right) or the right edge (so it moves left). It also creates new\nGoodies after a randomized number of frames. When the Player intersects\nwith a Goodie, the user is rewarded with 25 points. The update()  method of', "the Goodie manager uses the technique described in the previous section: it\nmakes a copy of the Goodies list and iterates through the copy .\nFile: Dodger/Player .py\nThe Player  class, shown in Listing 16-10 , manages the Player icon and\nkeeps track of where it should appear in the game window .\n# Player class\n--- snip imports ---  \n \nclass Player():  \n  1 def __init__(self, window):  \n        self.window = window  \n        self.image = pygwidgets.Image(window,  \n                                      (-100, -100),  \n'images/player.png')  \n        playerRect = self.image.getRect()  \n        self.maxX = WINDOW_WIDTH - playerRect.width  \n        self.maxY = GAME_HEIGHT - playerRect.height  \n \n    # Every frame, move the Player icon to the mouse position\n    # Limits the x- and y-coordinates to the game area of the  \nwindow \n  2 def update(self, x, y):  \n        if x < 0:  \n            x = 0  \n        elif x > self.maxX:  \n            x = self.maxX  \n        if y < 0:  \n            y = 0  \n        elif y > self.maxY:  \n            y = self.maxY  \n \n        self.image.setLoc((x, y))  \n        return self.image.getRect()  \n \n  3 def draw(self):  \n        self.image.draw()\nListing 16-10 : The Player  class\nThe __init__()  method 1 loads the Player icon image and sets up a\nnumber of instance variables for use later .", "The update()  method 2 is called in every frame by the Play scene. The\nbasic idea is to show the Player icon at the mouse location, which is passed\nin. W e do a few checks to ensure that the icon remains within the rectangle of\nthe playable area.
