We can just send messages to\nthese objects, asking them to update or reset themselves, and they do\nwhatever they need to do in response. The Goodie and Baddie managers pass\nthese messages on to all the Goodies and Baddies that they manage.\nFile: Dodger/SceneHighScores.py\nThe High Scores scene displays the top 10 high scores (and the names of the\nplayers) in a table. It reads and writes the data as a file in JSON format. The data is\nalways kept in order , from the highest to the lowest score. For example, the\nfile representing the 10 highest scores might look like this:\n[[\'Moe\', 987], [\'Larry\', 812], ... [\'Curly\', 597]]\nListing 16-14  shows the code of the HighScoresData  class.\n# HighScoresData class  \nfrom Constants import *  \nfrom pathlib import Path  \nimport json  \n \nclass HighScoresData():  \n    """The data file is stored as a list of lists in JSON  \nformat.\n    Each list is made up of a name and a score:\n        [[name, score], [name, score], [name, score] ...]\n    In this class, all scores are kept in self.scoresList.\n    The list is kept in order of scores, highest to lowest.\n    """ \n  1 def __init__(self):  \n        self.BLANK_SCORES_LIST = N_HIGH_SCORES * [[\'-----\',  ', "0]] \n      2 self.oFilePath = Path('HighScores.json')  \n \n        # Try to open and load the data from the data file  \n        try:  \n          3 data = self.oFilePath.read_text()  \n        except FileNotFoundError:  # no file, set to blank  \nscores and save  \n          4 self.scoresList = self.BLANK_SCORES_LIST.copy()  \n            self.saveScores()  \n            return  \n \n          # File exists, load the scores from the JSON file  \n          5 self.scoresList = json.loads(data)  \n \n  6 def addHighScore(self, name, newHighScore):  \n        # Find the appropriate place to add the new high score  \n        placeFound = False  \n        for index, nameScoreList in  \nenumerate(self.scoresList):  \n            thisScore = nameScoreList[1]  \n            if newHighScore > thisScore:  \n                # Insert into proper place, remove last entry  \n                self.scoresList.insert(index, [name,  \nnewHighScore])  \n                self.scoresList.pop(N_HIGH_SCORES)  \n                placeFound = True  \n                break  \n        if not placeFound:  \n            return  # score does not belong in the list  \n \n        # Save the updated scores  \n        self.saveScores()  \n \n  7 def saveScores(self):  \n        scoresAsJson = json.dumps(self.scoresList)  \n        self.oFilePath.write_text(scoresAsJson)  \n \n  8 def resetScores(self):  \n        self.scoresList = self.BLANK_SCORES_LIST.copy()  \n        self.saveScores()     \n \n  9 def getScoresAndNames(self):  \n        namesList = []  \n        scoresList = []  \n        for nameAndScore in self.scoresList:  ", "            thisName = nameAndScore[0]  \n            thisScore = nameAndScore[1]  \n            namesList.append(thisName)  \n            scoresList.append(thisScore)  \n \n        return scoresList, namesList  \n \n a def getHighestAndLowest(self):  \n        # Element 0 is the highest entry, element -1 is the  \nlowest\n        highestEntry = self.scoresList[0]  \n        lowestEntry = self.scoresList[-1]  \n        # Get the score (element 1) of each sublist  \n        highestScore = highestEntry[1]  \n        lowestScore = lowestEntry[1]  \n        return highestScore, lowestScore\nListing 16-14 : The HighScoresData  class\nIn the __init__()  method 1, we first create a list of all blank entries. W e\nuse the Path  module to create a path object with the location of the data file\n2.\nNOTE\nThe path shown in this listing is in the same folder as the code. This\nis fine for learning the concept of file input and output. However , if\nyou intend to shar e your pr ogram with other people to play on their\ncomputers, it would be better to use a differ ent path in the user ’s\nhome folder . If the file is not found 4, we set the scores to\nthe list of blank entries, call saveScores()  to save the scores, and return.\nOtherwise, we read the contents of the file 5 and convert from JSON format\nto a list of lists.\nThe addHighScores()  method 6 is responsible for adding a new high\nscore to the list. Because that operation will extend the list, we remove the last element\nto only keep the top 10. W e also check that the new score should actually be\ninserted into the list. It uses these\nresults to determine if a user ’s score qualifies the user to enter their name and\nscore into the high scores table.\nExtensions to the Game\nThe overall architecture is modular , allowing for ease of modification. Each\nscene encapsulates its own data and methods, while communication and\nnavigation are handled by the scene manager . Extensions can be handled in\none scene without af fecting anything in other scenes.\nFor example, rather than the game ending as soon as the Player icon hits a\nBaddie, you might want the user to start with some number of lives; when\nthe Player icon hits a Baddie, the number of lives is decremented by one, and', 'the game is over when the player runs out of lives. This kind of change\nwould be relatively easy to implement and would af fect only the Play scene.\nAs another idea, the user might start with a small number of bombs that\nthey can detonate when they’re in a bind, eliminating all Baddies within a\ngiven radius around the Player icon. The count of bombs would be\ndecremented each time one is used, until it reaches zero. This change would\naffect only the code of the Play scene and the Baddie manager .\nOr maybe you want to keep track of more high scores—say , 20 instead of\n10. A change like that can be made in the High Scores scene without\naffecting the Play or Splash scenes.\nSummary\nThis chapter demonstrated how to create and use Y es/No and Answer dialogs\n—both text and customizable versions. W e then focused on building a full\nobject-oriented game program, Dodger .\nWe used the pygwidgets  module for all the buttons, text displays, and\ninput text fields. W e used the pyghelpers  module for all the dialogs. The\nSceneMgr  allowed us to split up the game into smaller , more manageable\npieces (Scene  objects) and to navigate between scenes.\nThe game used or demonstrated the following object-oriented concepts:\nEncapsulation  Each scene handles only the things specific to the scene.\nPolymorphism  Each scene implements the same methods.\nInheritance  Each scene inherits from the Scene  base class.\nObject manager object  The Play scene uses composition to create a Baddie\nmanager object, self.oBaddieMgr , and a Goodie manager object,\nself.oGoodieMgr , each of which manages a list of its objects.\nShar ed constants  We use separate modules for Goodies and Baddies, and a\nConstants.py  file allows us to easily share constants across modules.', '17\nDESIGN PATTERNS AND WRAP-UP\nIn this final chapter, I’ll introduce the\nobject-oriented programming concept\nof design patterns, which are reusable\nOOP solutions to commonly occurring\nsoftware problems. We’ve already seen\none design pattern in this book: using an object\nmanager object to manage a list or dictionary of\nobjects. Many full books have been written on the\ntopic of design patterns, so we obviously cannot\ndiscuss all of them. In this chapter we’ll focus on the\nModel View Controller pattern that’s used to break a\nsystem into smaller, more manageable and more\nmodifiable parts. Lastly, I’ll give a wrap-up on OOP.\nModel View Controller\nThe Model V iew Contr oller (MVC)  design pattern enforces a clear split\nbetween a collection of data and the way that data is represented to the user .\nThe pattern separates the functionality into three parts: the model, the view ,\nand the controller . Each part has a clearly defined responsibility , and each is\nimplemented by one or more objects.', 'The model stores data. The view is responsible for drawing the\ninformation from the model in one of perhaps multiple ways. The controller\ntypically creates the model and view objects, handles all user interactions,\ncommunicates changes to the model, and tells the view to display the data.\nThis separation makes the overall system highly maintainable and\nmodifiable.\nFile Display Example\nAs a good example of the MVC pattern, consider the way files are\ndisplayed in the macOS Finder or the W indows File Explorer . Say we have\na folder that contains four files and a subfolder . The end user can choose to\ndisplay these items as a list, as in Figur e 17-1 .\nFigure 17-1 : Files in a folder shown as a list\nAlternatively , the user can choose to display the same items as icons, as\nin Figur e 17-2 .\nFigure 17-2 : Files in a folder shown as icons\nThe underlying data for both displays is identical, but the representation\nof the information to the user is dif ferent. In this example, the data is the list', 'of files and subfolders; it’ s kept in a model object. The view object displays\nthe data in whatever way the user chooses: as a list, as icons, as a detailed\nlist, and so on. The controller tells the view to display the information in the\nlayout chosen by the user .\nStatistical Display Example\nAs a more extensive example of the MVC pattern, let’ s consider a program\nthat simulates rolling a pair of dice many times and shows the results. In\neach roll we’ll add the values of the two dice, so the sum—which we’ll call\nan outcome —must be between 2 and 12. The data consists of the count of\nthe number of times each outcome is rolled and the percentage of the total\nnumber of rolls each outcome makes up. The program can display this data\nin three dif ferent representations: a bar chart, a pie chart, and a text table. It\ndefaults to a bar chart and displays the result after simulating rolling a pair\nof dice 2,500 times. Since this program is just intended as a working\ndemonstration of the MVC pattern, we’ll generate the output using pygame\nand pygwidgets . For more professional-looking charts and displays, I\nsuggest that you look into Python data visualization libraries such as\nMatplotlib, Seaborn, Plotly , Bokeh, and others that are designed for this\npurpose.\nFigur e 17-3  shows the data displayed as a bar chart.', 'Figure 17-3 : Dice roll data as a bar chart\nBeneath each bar is the outcome, the count of the number of times that\noutcome was rolled, and the percentage of the total number of rolls that\ncount represents. The height of each bar corresponds to the count (or\npercentage). Clicking Roll Dice runs the simulation again, using the number\nof rolls specified in the input field. The user can click the dif ferent radio\nbuttons to show dif ferent views of the same data. If the user selects the Pie\nChart radio button, the data is displayed as in Figur e 17-4 .', 'Figure 17-4 : Dice roll data as a pie chart\nIf the user selects the T ext radio button, the data is displayed as in Figur e\n17-5.', 'Figure 17-5 : Dice roll data as text\nThe user can change the value in the “Number of rolls” field to roll the\ndice as many times as they wish. This data in this program is based on\nstatistics and randomness. W ith dif ferent sample sizes the exact counts will\nobviously vary , but the percentages should always be approximately the\nsame.\nI won’ t show the full listing of the program here, but will focus on a few\nkey lines that demonstrate the setup and the flow of control in the MVC\npattern. The full program is available for download with the rest of the\nbook’ s resources, in the MVC_RollTheDice  folder . The folder contains the\nfollowing files:\nMain_MVC.py  The main Python file', 'Controller .py Contains the Controller  class\nModel.py  Contains the Model  class\nBarV iew .py Contains the BarView  class that displays the bar chart\nBin.py  Contains the Bin class that draws a single bar in the bar chart\nPieV iew .py Contains the PieView  class that displays the pie chart\nT extV iew .py Contains the TextView  class that displays the text view\nConstants.py  Contains constants that are shared by multiple modules\nThe main program instantiates a Controller  object and runs the main\nloop. Code in the main loop forwards all events (other than the\npygame.QUIT  event) to the controller to handle.\nThe Controller\nThe controller is the supervisor of the whole program. It starts by\ninstantiating the Model  object. It then instantiates one of each of the\ndifferent view objects: BarView , PieView , and TextView . Here is the startup\ncode in the __init__()  method of the Controller  class:\n# Instantiate the model  \nself.oModel = Model()  \n# Instantiate different view objects  \nself.oBarView = BarView(self.window, self.oModel)  \nself.oPieView = PieView(self.window, self.oModel)  \nself.oTextView = TextView(self.window, self.oModel)\nWhen the Controller  object instantiates these View  objects, it passes in\nthe Model  object so each View  object can request information directly from\nthe model. Dif ferent implementations of the MVC pattern might handle\ncommunication between these three elements dif ferently; for example, a\ncontroller could act as an intermediary , requesting data from the model and\nforwarding it on to the current view , rather than allowing the model and\nview to communicate directly .\nThe controller draws and reacts to everything outside the black rectangle\nin the window , including the title, the image of the dice, and the radio', 'buttons. It draws the Quit and Roll Dice buttons and reacts when they’re\nclicked, and it handles any changes the user makes to the number of rolls.\nThe Controller  object keeps a current View  object, which determines\nwhich view is currently displayed. W e set it by default to the BarView\nobject (the bar chart):\nself.oView = self.oBarView\nWhen the user clicks a radio button, the Controller  sets its current View\nobject to the newly selected view and tells the new View  object to update\nitself by calling its update()  method:\nif self.oBarButton.handleEvent(event):  \n    self.oView = self.oBarView  \n    self.oView.update()  \nelif self.oPieButton.handleEvent(event):  \n    self.oView = self.oPieView  \n    self.oView.update()  \nelif self.oTextButton.handleEvent(event):  \n    self.oView = self.oTextView  \n    self.oView.update()\nAt startup, and whenever the user clicks Roll Dice, the controller\nvalidates the number of rolls specified in the “Number of rolls” field and\ntells the model to generate new data:\nself.oModel.generateRolls(nRounds)\nAll the views are polymorphic, so in each frame the Controller  object\ncalls the draw()  method of the current View  object:\nself.oView.draw()  # tell the current view to draw itself\nThe Model\nThe model is responsible for obtaining (and potentially updating)\ninformation. In this program, the Model  object is simple: it simulates rolling\na pair of dice many times, stores the results in instance variables, and\nreports the data when a View  object requests it.', 'When asked to generate data, the model runs a loop simulating rolling the\ndice and stores its data in two dictionaries: self.rollsDict , which uses\neach outcome as a key and the count as a value, and self.percentsDict ,\nwhich uses each outcome as a key and the percentage of rolls as a value.\nIn more complex programs, the model could get its data from a database,\nthe internet, or other sources. For example, a Model  object could maintain\nstock information, population data, city housing data, temperature readings,\nand so on.\nIn this model, the getRoundsRollsPercents()  method is called by the\nView  objects to retrieve all the data at once. However , a model may contain\nmore information than any one view might need. Therefore, dif ferent View\nobjects can call dif ferent methods in the Model  object to request dif ferent\ninformation from the same model. T o support this, in the sample program\nI’ve included a number of additional getter methods\n(getNumberOfRounds() , getRolls() , and getPercents() ) that a\nprogrammer could use when building a new View  object, to get only the\ndata that the new view might want to display .\nThe V iew\nA View  object is responsible for displaying data to the user . In our sample\nprogram, we have three dif ferent View  objects that display the same\nunderlying information in three dif ferent forms; each displays the\ninformation within the black rectangle in the window . At startup, and when\nthe user clicks Roll Dice, the controller calls the update()  method of the\ncurrent View  object. All View  objects then make the same call to the Model\nobject to get the current data:\nnRounds, resultsDict, percentsDict =  \nself.oModel.getRoundsRollsPercents()\nThe View  object then formats the data in its own way and presents it to\nthe user .\nAdvantages of the MVC Pattern', 'The MVC design pattern breaks up responsibility into separate classes that\nact independently but work collectively . Building the components as\ndistinct classes and minimizing the interactions between the resulting\nobjects allows each individual component to be less complex and less error\nprone. Once the interface of each component is defined, the code of the\nclasses can even be written by dif ferent programmers.\nWith the MVC approach, each component demonstrates the core OOP\nconcepts of encapsulation and abstraction. Using an MVC object structure,\nthe model can change the way it represents the data internally without\naffecting the controller or the view . As mentioned previously , the model\nmight contain more data than any single view needs. And as long as the\ncontroller doesn’ t change the way it communicates with the model, and the\nmodel continues to return the requested information to the view in an\nagreed-upon way , the model can add new data without breaking the system.\nThe MVC model also makes it easy to add enhancements. For example,\nin our dice-rolling program, the model could keep track of the count of the\ndifferent combinations of rolls of the two dice that make up each outcome,\nsuch as getting a 5 by rolling a 1 and a 4 or a 2 and a 3. W e could then\nmodify the BarChart  view to obtain this additional information from the\nmodel and show each bar split into smaller bars to display the percentages\nof each combination.\nEach of the View  objects is entirely customizable. The TextView  could\nuse dif ferent fonts and font sizes, or a dif ferent layout. The PieView  could\nshow the wedges in dif ferent colors. The bars in the BarView  could be\nthicker or taller , or be shown in dif ferent colors, or even be displayed\nhorizontally . Any such changes would be made only in the appropriate View\nobject, completely independent of the model or the controller .\nThe MVC pattern also makes it easy to add a new way to view the data,\nby writing a new View  class. The only additional changes required would be\nto have the controller draw another radio button, instantiate the new View\nobject, and call the new View  object’ s update()  method when the user\nselects the new view .', 'NOTE\nMVC and other design patterns ar e independent of any specific\ncomputer language and can be used in any language that supports\nOOP . If you’r e inter ested in learning mor e, I suggest that you sear ch\nthe web for OOP design patterns such as the Factory , Flyweight,\nObserver , and V isitor patterns; ther e are numer ous video and text\ntutorials (as well as books) available on all of these. For a general\nintroduction, Design Patterns : Elements of Reusable Object-\nOriented Software  (Addison-W esley) by Erich Gamma, Richar d\nHelm, Ralph Johnson, and John Vlissides (the Gang of Four) is\nconsider ed the bible of design patterns.\nWrap-Up\nWhen thinking about object-oriented programming, remember my initial\ndefinition of an object: data plus code that acts on that data over time.\nOOP gives you a new way to think about programming, of fering an easy\nand convenient way to group together data and code that acts on that data.\nYou write classes and instantiate objects from those classes. Each object\ngets a set of all instance variables defined in the class, but the instance\nvariables in dif ferent objects can contain dif ferent data and remain\nindependent of each other . The methods of the objects can work dif ferently\nbecause they’re working on dif ferent data. Objects can be instantiated at\nany time and can be destroyed at any time.\nWhen instantiating multiple objects from one class, you typically build a\nlist or dictionary of objects, then later iterate over that list or dictionary ,\ncalling methods of each object.\nAs a final reminder , the three main tenets of OOP are:\nEncapsulation  Everything in one place, objects own their data.\nPolymorphism  Different objects can implement the same methods.\nInheritance  A class can extend or modify the behavior of another class.', 'Objects often work in hierarchies; they can use composition to instantiate\nother objects and can call methods of lower -level objects to ask them to do\nwork or provide information.\nTo give you a clear visual representation of OOP in action, most of the\nexamples in this book focused on widgets and other objects that can be\nuseful in a gaming environment. I developed the pygwidgets  and\npyghelpers  packages to demonstrate many dif ferent OOP techniques and to\nallow you to easily use GUI widgets in pygame programs. I hope you find\nthese packages useful and go on to use them to develop fun or useful\nprograms of your own.\nMore importantly , I hope that you recognize that object-oriented\nprogramming is a general-purpose approach that can be applied in a wide\nvariety of circumstances. Any time you see two or more functions that need\nto operate on a shared set of data, you should consider building a class and\ninstantiating an object. Y ou may also want to consider building an object\nmanager object to manage a group of objects.\nWith all that said, I’d like to of fer my congratulations: you’ve made it to\nthe end of the book! Although actually , this should be considered the\nbeginning of your journey into object-oriented programming. Hopefully , the\nconcepts described in this book have given you a framework that you can\nbuild on—but the only way to truly get a handle on how OOP works is to\nwrite lots and lots of code. Over time, you will start to notice patterns that\nyou will use again and again in your code. Understanding how to structure\nyour classes is a dif ficult process. Only through experience will it begin to\nbecome easier to ensure that you have the proper methods and instance\nvariables in the correct classes.\nPractice, practice, practice!', 'I N D E X\nPlease note that index links to approximate location of each term.\nSymbols\n+ operator example, polymorphism, 193–194\n* (asterisk), using with import statement, 61\n@ (at) property , 174–177\n== (equal to), magic method name, 196\n> (greater than), magic method name, 196\n>= (greater than or equal to), magic method name, 196\n< (less than), magic method name, 196\n<= (less than or equal to), magic method name, 196\n!= (not equal to), magic method name, 196\nA\nabc (abstract base class) module, Python Standard Library , 232\nAbortTransaction  exception, 79, 81\nabsolute path, using with pygame, 101\nabstract base class ( abc) module, Python Standard Library , 232\nabstract classes and methods, 231–234.
